S6 EEE ⚡ Power System II (EET304)
⏱️

3-Hour Exam Time Budget (20 Questions / 100 Marks Strategy)

Part A (10 Short Answers × 3 Marks = 30M): Allocate 5 mins per answer (50 mins total) — target ½ to 1 page highlighting key formula and core definition.
Part B (10 Essay Answers × 7 Marks = 70M): Allocate 12 mins per answer (120 mins total) — target 1.5 to 2 pages with schematic circuit/block diagrams and step-by-step derivations.
Strategy & Review Buffer (10 mins): 5 mins at start for question choice planning + 5 mins at end for question numbering and equation verification.

Course Code: EET304 • 5 Modules • 26 Questions

Power System II

Advanced electrical power network analysis: Per-unit representation, symmetrical components ($I_0, I_1, I_2$), symmetrical and unsymmetrical fault analysis (LG, LL, LLG), load flow studies (Gauss-Seidel & Newton-Raphson), power system stability (swing equation, equal area criterion), and economic dispatch of thermal units.

Module 1

Symmetrical Faults and Z-bus Formulation

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: PS2-Q1

Derive the mathematical expression for the instantaneous short-circuit current on a transmission line modeled as an RL circuit. Explain the DC offset component and the doubling effect. Derive the mathematical expression for the instantaneous short-circuit current on a transmission line modeled as an RL circuit. Explain the DC offset component and the doubling effect. Derive the mathematical expression for the instantaneous short-circuit current on a transmission line modeled as an RL circuit. Explain the DC offset component and the doubling effect.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$i(t) = \frac{V_m}{|Z|} \sin(\omega t + \alpha - \theta) - \frac{V_m}{|Z|} \sin(\alpha - \theta) e^{-t/\tau}, \quad i_{peak} \approx 2 \frac{V_m}{|Z|}$$

Short circuit on an RL line produces a symmetrical AC wave plus a decaying DC offset. If fault occurs at voltage zero ($\alpha - \theta = -\pi/2$), maximum DC offset causes the Doubling Effect ($i_{peak} \approx 2 I_m$).

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$i(t) = \frac{V_m}{|Z|} \sin(\omega t + \alpha - \theta) - \frac{V_m}{|Z|} \sin(\alpha - \theta) e^{-t/\tau}, \quad i_{peak} \approx 2 \frac{V_m}{|Z|}$$

Short circuit on an RL line produces a symmetrical AC wave plus a decaying DC offset. If fault occurs at voltage zero ($\alpha - \theta = -\pi/2$), maximum DC offset causes the Doubling Effect ($i_{peak} \approx 2 I_m$).

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$i(t) = \frac{V_m}{|Z|} \sin(\omega t + \alpha - \theta) - \frac{V_m}{|Z|} \sin(\alpha - \theta) e^{-t/\tau}, \quad i_{peak} \approx 2 \frac{V_m}{|Z|}$$

Short circuit on an RL line produces a symmetrical AC wave plus a decaying DC offset. If fault occurs at voltage zero ($\alpha - \theta = -\pi/2$), maximum DC offset causes the Doubling Effect ($i_{peak} \approx 2 I_m$).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A short-circuit fault on a power transmission line produces severe electromagnetic transients governed by the series resistance $R$ and inductance $L$ of the line conductors. To analyze this transient behavior, consider a series RL circuit energized by a sinusoidal voltage source $v(t) = V_m \sin(\omega t + \alpha)$, where $\alpha$ is the switching angle determining the exact point on the voltage wave when the short-circuit occurs at $t = 0$.

Applying Kirchhoff's Voltage Law (KVL) to the faulted loop for $t \ge 0$: $$L \frac{di(t)}{dt} + R i(t) = V_m \sin(\omega t + \alpha)$$ This is a first-order non-homogeneous linear differential equation. Its total solution consists of the sum of the complementary function (transient natural response $i_{tr}(t)$) and the particular integral (steady-state forced response $i_{ss}(t)$): $$i(t) = i_{tr}(t) + i_{ss}(t) = A e^{-\frac{R}{L}t} + \frac{V_m}{|Z|} \sin(\omega t + \alpha - \theta)$$ where $|Z| = \sqrt{R^2 + \omega^2 L^2}$ is the circuit impedance and $\theta = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{\omega L}{R}\right)$ is the impedance angle.

To evaluate the arbitrary constant $A$, we enforce the initial physical boundary condition: because the transmission line possesses magnetic inductance, current cannot change instantaneously. Assuming the pre-fault current is zero ($i(0^-) = 0$): $$i(0) = A e^0 + \frac{V_m}{|Z|} \sin(\alpha - \theta) = 0 \implies A = -\frac{V_m}{|Z|} \sin(\alpha - \theta)$$ Substituting constant $A$ back into the total current expression: $$i(t) = \frac{V_m}{|Z|} \sin(\omega t + \alpha - \theta) - \frac{V_m}{|Z|} \sin(\alpha - \theta) e^{-t/\tau}$$ where $\tau = \frac{L}{R}$ is the circuit time constant.

The total short-circuit current consists of two distinct components: 1. Symmetrical AC Component ($i_{ac}(t)$): A sinusoidal current of constant peak amplitude $\frac{V_m}{|Z|}$ oscillating at system frequency $\omega$. 2. Decaying DC Offset Component ($i_{dc}(t)$): An exponential unidirectional current initialized at $-\frac{V_m}{|Z|} \sin(\alpha - \theta)$ decaying to zero with time constant $\tau = L/R$.

The magnitude of the DC offset depends entirely on the switching angle $\alpha$: - Case 1 (Zero DC Offset): If the fault occurs when $\alpha - \theta = 0$ or $\pi$ (i.e., at voltage maximum for a purely inductive line), the DC offset is identically zero ($i_{dc}(t) = 0$), and the fault current is completely symmetrical from $t = 0$. - Case 2 (Maximum DC Offset / The Doubling Effect): If the fault occurs when $\alpha - \theta = -\frac{\pi}{2}$ (i.e., at voltage zero crossing for an inductive line where $\theta \approx 90^\circ$ and $\alpha = 0$), the initial DC offset reaches its theoretical maximum: $$i_{dc}(0) = -\frac{V_m}{|Z|} \sin\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = +\frac{V_m}{|Z|}$$ At the first positive half-cycle peak ($\omega t = \pi$): $$i_{total}(\pi/\omega) = \frac{V_m}{|Z|}\sin(\pi - \pi/2) + \frac{V_m}{|Z|} e^{-\frac{\pi}{\omega\tau}} \approx \frac{V_m}{|Z|} + \frac{V_m}{|Z|} = 2 \frac{V_m}{|Z|}$$ This doubling of peak current is termed the Doubling Effect. Circuit breakers must be mechanically rated to withstand this immense peak momentary electromechanical force.

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: PS2-Q2

Explain the physical phenomena occurring during a symmetrical 3-phase fault at the terminals of an unloaded synchronous generator. Define subtransient, transient, and steady-state reactances with neat oscillogram. Explain the physical phenomena occurring during a symmetrical 3-phase fault at the terminals of an unloaded synchronous generator. Define subtransient, transient, and steady-state reactances with neat oscillogram. Explain the physical phenomena occurring during a symmetrical 3-phase fault at the terminals of an unloaded synchronous generator. Define subtransient, transient, and steady-state reactances with neat oscillogram.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I'' = \frac{E_g}{X_d''}, \quad I' = \frac{E_g}{X_d'}, \quad I = \frac{E_g}{X_d}, \quad E'' = V_t + j I_L X_d''$$

Damper currents choke stator flux in subtransient period ($X_d''$), field currents resist in transient period ($X_d'$), settling to steady state ($X_d$). Subtransient internal voltage $E'' = V_t + j I_L X_d''$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I'' = \frac{E_g}{X_d''}, \quad I' = \frac{E_g}{X_d'}, \quad I = \frac{E_g}{X_d}, \quad E'' = V_t + j I_L X_d''$$

Damper currents choke stator flux in subtransient period ($X_d''$), field currents resist in transient period ($X_d'$), settling to steady state ($X_d$). Subtransient internal voltage $E'' = V_t + j I_L X_d''$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I'' = \frac{E_g}{X_d''}, \quad I' = \frac{E_g}{X_d'}, \quad I = \frac{E_g}{X_d}, \quad E'' = V_t + j I_L X_d''$$

Damper currents choke stator flux in subtransient period ($X_d''$), field currents resist in transient period ($X_d'$), settling to steady state ($X_d$). Subtransient internal voltage $E'' = V_t + j I_L X_d''$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

When a symmetrical three-phase short circuit occurs at the terminals of an energized synchronous generator, the fault current undergoes a profound transient evolution that is fundamentally different from a static RL circuit. The effective internal reactance of the machine does not remain constant; instead, it varies progressively through three distinct operating regimes governed by the magnetic flux coupling between the stator armature windings, rotor field winding, and rotor damper (amortisseur) windings.

Physical Phenomena During Machine Short Circuit: Prior to the fault, the air gap flux is produced entirely by the rotor DC field excitation. When the three-phase fault strikes, heavy, lagging demagnetizing armature currents immediately flow through the stator windings. By Lenz's law, magnetic flux linkages cannot change instantaneously. To oppose the sudden penetration of this demagnetizing armature reaction flux: 1. Induced currents surge instantaneously in both the low-resistance rotor damper bars and the main field winding. 2. Because the damper windings have a very small leakage inductance and close proximity to the stator surface, they force the initial armature flux into high-reluctance leakage paths between the stator and rotor teeth, severely restricting the mutual air-gap flux. 3. As the damper currents decay rapidly according to their short subtransient time constant $T_d''$ (typically 0.02 to 0.05 seconds), the flux penetrates into the main field winding. 4. The field winding induced currents decay more slowly according to the transient time constant $T_d'$ (typically 0.5 to 2.0 seconds). 5. Finally, after all rotor eddy currents dissipate, the machine reaches the steady-state condition where the armature reaction flux fully counteracts the field MMF, leaving only the steady-state air-gap flux.

Classification of Generator Reactances: Based on this time-varying flux penetration, three distinct machine reactances are defined: 1. Subtransient Reactance ($X_d''$): The effective reactance during the first few cycles ($0 \le t \le 3$ cycles) while both damper and field windings carry induced currents. It represents the smallest internal reactance ($X_d'' < X_d' < X_d$), resulting in the highest initial RMS current: $$I'' = \frac{E_g}{X_d''}$$ 2. Transient Reactance ($X_d'$): The effective reactance after damper currents have fully decayed but field currents still resist flux changes ($3 \text{ cycles} \le t \le 30$ cycles): $$I' = \frac{E_g}{X_d'}$$ 3. Steady-State Synchronous Reactance ($X_d$): The continuous reactance after all transients have ceased, comprising armature leakage reactance $X_l$ and armature reaction reactance $X_{ar}$: $$X_d = X_l + X_{ar}, \quad I = \frac{E_g}{X_d}$$

Internal Voltages Under Loaded Conditions: If the generator is supplying a steady pre-fault load current $I_L$ at terminal voltage $V_t$ with power factor angle $\theta$: 1. Subtransient Internal Voltage ($E''$): $$E'' = V_t + j I_L X_d''$$ The subtransient fault current injected into a terminal fault is: $$I_{f}'' = \frac{E''}{X_d''}$$ 2. Transient Internal Voltage ($E'$): $$E' = V_t + j I_L X_d'$$ The transient fault current is: $$I_{f}' = \frac{E'}{X_d'}$$ 3. Steady-State Excitation EMF ($E_g$): $$E_g = V_t + j I_L X_d$$

Oscillographic Analysis: By plotting the symmetrical envelope of the fault current oscillogram on semi-logarithmic paper, the total AC envelope current is expressed mathematically as: $$i_{ac}(t) = \sqrt{2} \left[ (I'' - I') e^{-t/T_d''} + (I' - I) e^{-t/T_d'} + I \right] \cos(\omega t)$$ This analytical decomposition enables protection engineers to select circuit breakers whose interrupting capabilities precisely match the decaying short-circuit current at the moment of contact separation.

3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: PS2-Q3

Define Short Circuit MVA. Explain the selection criteria for circuit breaker ratings, differentiating between rated symmetrical interrupting current and rated momentary making current. Define Short Circuit MVA. Explain the selection criteria for circuit breaker ratings, differentiating between rated symmetrical interrupting current and rated momentary making current. Define Short Circuit MVA. Explain the selection criteria for circuit breaker ratings, differentiating between rated symmetrical interrupting current and rated momentary making current.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$SCMVA = \frac{MVA_{base}}{X_{th, pu}}, \quad I_{making, peak} = 1.6 \times \sqrt{2} \times I_{sc, sym} \approx 2.55 \times I_{sc, sym}$$

Short Circuit MVA measures fault severity. Breaker rated making current ($2.55 I_{sc}$) withstands peak first-cycle electromechanical forces; interrupting current is evaluated at contact separation (2 to 5 cycles).

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$SCMVA = \frac{MVA_{base}}{X_{th, pu}}, \quad I_{making, peak} = 1.6 \times \sqrt{2} \times I_{sc, sym} \approx 2.55 \times I_{sc, sym}$$

Short Circuit MVA measures fault severity. Breaker rated making current ($2.55 I_{sc}$) withstands peak first-cycle electromechanical forces; interrupting current is evaluated at contact separation (2 to 5 cycles).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

In power system design, the severity of a prospective fault at a given bus is quantified by the Short Circuit MVA (also known as fault level). The short circuit MVA defines the total apparent power entering a bolted three-phase symmetrical fault.

The three-phase Short Circuit MVA is mathematically defined as: $$SCMVA = \sqrt{3} \times V_{L,rated} \times I_{sc} \times 10^{-6} \text{ MVA}$$ where $V_{L,rated}$ is the nominal line-to-line operating voltage in volts, and $I_{sc}$ is the symmetrical RMS short-circuit current in amperes. In the per-unit system: $$I_{sc,pu} = \frac{V_{th,pu}}{X_{th,pu}} \approx \frac{1.0}{X_{th,pu}}$$ where $X_{th,pu}$ is the positive-sequence Thevenin reactance of the power network viewed from the fault point on the chosen system base MVA ($MVA_{base}$). Multiplying per-unit fault current by base MVA yields the direct formula: $$SCMVA = \frac{MVA_{base}}{X_{th,pu}}$$

Circuit Breaker Selection Criteria: Circuit breakers are electromechanical protective switches that must perform two distinct physical duties during a fault: 1. Momentary (Making) Current Rating: When a breaker closes onto an existing fault, its contacts must withstand the peak mechanical electromagnetic forces ($F \propto i^2$) produced by the initial peak current during the first half-cycle. Because the fault may contain the maximum possible DC offset (doubling effect), the rated making current is defined as: $$I_{making,peak} = 1.6 \times \sqrt{2} \times I_{sc,sym} \approx 2.55 \times I_{sc,sym}$$ where the factor $\sqrt{2}$ converts RMS to peak, and the factor $1.6$ accounts for the decaying DC offset present at $t = 10 \text{ ms}$ (in a $50 \text{ Hz}$ system).

2. Symmetrical Interrupting Current Rating: When a breaker opens to extinguish the fault arc, contact separation occurs after a finite operating delay comprising relay pickup time plus breaker mechanical parting time (typically 2 to 5 cycles, corresponding to 40 to 100 ms). Because the high-frequency subtransient DC and AC components have partially decayed during this interval, the required interrupting current is evaluated using generator transient reactances modified by standard IEEE/ANSI multiplying factors: - For 2-cycle breaker: Multiplying factor = $1.4 \times I_{sc,sym}$ - For 3-cycle breaker: Multiplying factor = $1.2 \times I_{sc,sym}$ - For 5-cycle breaker: Multiplying factor = $1.1 \times I_{sc,sym}$ - For 8-cycle breaker: Multiplying factor = $1.0 \times I_{sc,sym}$

The rated interrupting MVA of the circuit breaker is specified as: $$MVA_{interrupting} = \sqrt{3} \times V_{L,rated} \times I_{interrupting,sym}$$ Breakers must be selected such that both their making current and interrupting MVA exceed the maximum calculated fault duty at their installation bus.

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: PS2-Q4

Explain the step-by-step algorithm for the formation of the Bus Impedance Matrix (Z_bus). Derive the mathematical formulas for the four types of branch additions and Kron reduction. Explain the step-by-step algorithm for the formation of the Bus Impedance Matrix (Z_bus). Derive the mathematical formulas for the four types of branch additions and Kron reduction. Explain the step-by-step algorithm for the formation of the Bus Impedance Matrix (Z_bus). Derive the mathematical formulas for the four types of branch additions and Kron reduction.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$Z_{ij}^{new} = Z_{ij}^{old} - \frac{Z_{iq} Z_{qj}}{Z_{qq}}, \quad I_{f, k} = \frac{V_k(0)}{Z_{kk}}, \quad V_i^{fault} = V_i(0) - \frac{Z_{ik}}{Z_{kk}} V_k(0)$$

Z_bus algorithm builds impedance matrix by adding branches in 4 topological types. Loop-closing branches (Types 3 and 4) are eliminated by Kron reduction. Driving-point impedance $Z_{kk}$ directly yields fault current.

⚡ Key Equation

$$Z_{ij}^{new} = Z_{ij}^{old} - \frac{Z_{iq} Z_{qj}}{Z_{qq}}, \quad I_{f, k} = \frac{V_k(0)}{Z_{kk}}, \quad V_i^{fault} = V_i(0) - \frac{Z_{ik}}{Z_{kk}} V_k(0)$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$Z_{ij}^{new} = Z_{ij}^{old} - \frac{Z_{iq} Z_{qj}}{Z_{qq}}, \quad I_{f, k} = \frac{V_k(0)}{Z_{kk}}, \quad V_i^{fault} = V_i(0) - \frac{Z_{ik}}{Z_{kk}} V_k(0)$$

Z_bus algorithm builds impedance matrix by adding branches in 4 topological types. Loop-closing branches (Types 3 and 4) are eliminated by Kron reduction. Driving-point impedance $Z_{kk}$ directly yields fault current.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Bus Impedance Matrix ($Z_{bus}$) is the inverse of the Bus Admittance Matrix ($Y_{bus}$). While $Y_{bus}$ is sparse and easily formed by inspection, $Z_{bus}$ is a dense matrix whose elements directly provide the driving-point Thevenin impedances and transfer impedances essential for fault studies. The $Z_{bus}$ building algorithm synthesizes the matrix branch-by-branch without requiring matrix inversion.

Let an existing power network consist of $m$ independent buses plus reference bus $0$, characterized by the known impedance relationship: $$V_{bus} = Z_{bus} I_{bus}$$ When a new branch of impedance $z_b$ is connected, the matrix is updated according to four fundamental topological modifications:

Type 1 Modification: Adding branch $z_b$ from a new bus $p$ to reference bus $0$. - A new bus $p = m + 1$ is added. Because this new bus is connected only to the reference, its voltage is unaffected by currents injected into existing buses: $$Z_{pi} = Z_{ip} = 0 \quad \forall i = 1, 2, \dots, m$$ $$Z_{pp} = z_b$$ The augmented matrix grows in dimension by one: $$Z_{bus}^{new} = \begin{bmatrix} Z_{bus}^{old} & 0 \\ 0 & z_b \end{bmatrix}$$

Type 2 Modification: Adding branch $z_b$ from a new bus $p$ to an existing bus $k$. - The current $I_p$ injected into new bus $p$ must flow entirely through branch $z_b$ into existing bus $k$. Hence, the voltage of new bus $p$ is $V_p = V_k + z_b I_p$: $$Z_{pi} = Z_{ip} = Z_{ki} \quad \forall i = 1, 2, \dots, m$$ $$Z_{pp} = Z_{kk} + z_b$$ The matrix grows to $(m+1) \times (m+1)$: $$Z_{bus}^{new} = \begin{bmatrix} Z_{bus}^{old} & Z_{col, k} \\ Z_{row, k} & Z_{kk} + z_b \end{bmatrix}$$

Type 3 Modification: Adding branch $z_b$ from an existing bus $k$ to reference bus $0$. - No new bus is introduced, but a closed loop is formed. We temporarily treat reference bus $0$ as a fictitious new bus $q$, creating an intermediate $(m+1) \times (m+1)$ matrix using the Type 2 rule: $$Z_{qi} = Z_{iq} = Z_{ki}, \quad Z_{qq} = Z_{kk} + z_b$$ Because bus $q$ is actually the reference bus, its potential must be zero: $V_q = 0$. The $(m+1)$-th row equation is: $$V_q = 0 = \sum_{j=1}^m Z_{qj} I_j + Z_{qq} I_q \implies I_q = -\frac{1}{Z_{qq}} \sum_{j=1}^m Z_{qj} I_j$$ Substituting $I_q$ back into the first $m$ bus voltage equations eliminates the fictitious $(m+1)$-th row and column via Kron Reduction: $$Z_{ij}^{new} = Z_{ij}^{old} - \frac{Z_{iq} Z_{qj}}{Z_{qq}} = Z_{ij}^{old} - \frac{Z_{ik} Z_{kj}}{Z_{kk} + z_b} \quad \forall i, j = 1, \dots, m$$

Type 4 Modification: Adding branch $z_b$ between two existing buses $j$ and $k$. - This closes an internal mesh loop without creating a new physical bus. We temporarily create a fictitious loop bus $q$, where the voltage drop across the new branch satisfies $V_j - V_k + z_b I_q = 0$. The intermediate column and row entries are: $$Z_{qi} = Z_{iq} = Z_{ji} - Z_{ki} \quad \forall i = 1, \dots, m$$ $$Z_{qq} = Z_{jj} + Z_{kk} - 2 Z_{jk} + z_b$$ Eliminating the fictitious loop current $I_q$ via Kron Reduction restores the $m \times m$ dimension: $$Z_{il}^{new} = Z_{il}^{old} - \frac{Z_{iq} Z_{ql}}{Z_{qq}} = Z_{il}^{old} - \frac{(Z_{ij} - Z_{ik})(Z_{jl} - Z_{kl})}{Z_{jj} + Z_{kk} - 2 Z_{jk} + z_b}$$

Application to Fault Analysis: The diagonal element $Z_{kk}$ represents the driving-point Thevenin impedance directly at bus $k$. For a bolted three-phase fault at bus $k$ with pre-fault voltage $V_k(0) = 1.0 \text{ pu}$: $$I_{f,k} = \frac{V_k(0)}{Z_{kk}} = \frac{1.0}{Z_{kk}}$$ The post-fault voltage at any other bus $i$ during the fault at bus $k$ is determined algebraically in a single line: $$V_i^{fault} = V_i(0) - Z_{ik} I_{f,k} = V_i(0) - \frac{Z_{ik}}{Z_{kk}} V_k(0)$$ This demonstrates the tremendous computational superiority of $Z_{bus}$ for automated short-circuit analysis.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Consider a classic KTU university examination problem: A three-phase power system consists of two synchronous generators $G_1$ and $G_2$ feeding a transmission network through step-up transformers $T_1$ and $T_2$. The equipment ratings are: - Generator $G_1$: $100 \text{ MVA}, 15 \text{ kV}, X_d'' = 15\%$ ($0.15 \text{ pu}$). - Generator $G_2$: $50 \text{ MVA}, 15 \text{ kV}, X_d'' = 20\%$ ($0.20 \text{ pu}$). - Transformer $T_1$: $100 \text{ MVA}, 15 / 132 \text{ kV}, X = 10\%$ ($0.10 \text{ pu}$). - Transformer $T_2$: $50 \text{ MVA}, 15 / 132 \text{ kV}, X = 8\%$ ($0.08 \text{ pu}$). - Transmission line connecting the high-voltage sides of $T_1$ and $T_2$ has a total series reactance of $X_{line} = 25 \ \Omega$.

A symmetrical three-phase bolted short-circuit occurs at the high-voltage terminals of transformer $T_1$ (Bus 1). The system is initially operating unloaded at rated voltage ($132 \text{ kV}$). Select a common system base of $100 \text{ MVA}$ and $132 \text{ kV}$ on the transmission line. Determine: (i) The per-unit reactance diagram. (ii) The Thevenin equivalent reactance at the fault point. (iii) The total fault current in kA and fault MVA. (iv) The individual short-circuit current contributions from generators $G_1$ and $G_2$.

Step 1: Determine Base Quantities: - Common Base Power: $MVA_{base} = 100 \text{ MVA}$. - Transmission line base voltage: $kV_{base, line} = 132 \text{ kV}$. - Generator side base voltage: $$kV_{base, gen} = 132 \times \frac{15}{132} = 15 \text{ kV}$$ - Base impedance on $132 \text{ kV}$ line: $$Z_{base, line} = \frac{(kV_{base, line})^2}{MVA_{base}} = \frac{132^2}{100} = \frac{17424}{100} = 174.24 \ \Omega$$ - Base current on $132 \text{ kV}$ line: $$I_{base, line} = \frac{MVA_{base} \times 10^3}{\sqrt{3} \times kV_{base, line}} = \frac{100 \times 10^3}{\sqrt{3} \times 132} = \frac{100000}{228.63} = 437.38 \text{ A} = 0.4374 \text{ kA}$$

Step 2: Convert all reactances to the common $100 \text{ MVA}$ base: Using the standard conversion formula: $$X_{new, pu} = X_{old, pu} \times \left(\frac{MVA_{base, new}}{MVA_{base, old}}\right) \times \left(\frac{kV_{base, old}}{kV_{base, new}}\right)^2$$ - For Generator $G_1$: Rated on $100 \text{ MVA}, 15 \text{ kV}$: $$X_{G1} = 0.15 \text{ pu}$$ - For Generator $G_2$: Rated on $50 \text{ MVA}, 15 \text{ kV}$: $$X_{G2} = 0.20 \times \left(\frac{100}{50}\right) \times \left(\frac{15}{15}\right)^2 = 0.20 \times 2 = 0.40 \text{ pu}$$ - For Transformer $T_1$: Rated on $100 \text{ MVA}$: $$X_{T1} = 0.10 \text{ pu}$$ - For Transformer $T_2$: Rated on $50 \text{ MVA}$: $$X_{T2} = 0.08 \times \left(\frac{100}{50}\right) = 0.08 \times 2 = 0.16 \text{ pu}$$ - For Transmission Line ($25 \ \Omega$): $$X_{line, pu} = \frac{X_{actual}}{Z_{base, line}} = \frac{25}{174.24} = 0.1435 \text{ pu}$$

Step 3: Construct Per-Unit Reactance Diagram: - Branch 1 (from Generator $G_1$ to Fault Bus 1): $$X_{branch1} = X_{G1} + X_{T1} = 0.15 + 0.10 = 0.25 \text{ pu}$$ - Branch 2 (from Generator $G_2$ through $T_2$ and Line to Fault Bus 1): $$X_{branch2} = X_{G2} + X_{T2} + X_{line} = 0.40 + 0.16 + 0.1435 = 0.7035 \text{ pu}$$

Step 4: Calculate Thevenin Impedance at Fault Point (Bus 1): Because the generators operate in parallel with neutral references connected together: $$X_{th} = X_{branch1} \parallel X_{branch2} = \frac{X_{branch1} \times X_{branch2}}{X_{branch1} + X_{branch2}} = \frac{0.25 \times 0.7035}{0.25 + 0.7035} = \frac{0.175875}{0.9535} = 0.1844 \text{ pu}$$

Step 5: Calculate Fault Current and Short-Circuit MVA: Pre-fault voltage at Bus 1 is $V_f = 1.0 \angle 0^\circ \text{ pu}$. - Total fault current in per-unit: $$I_{f, pu} = \frac{V_f}{j X_{th}} = \frac{1.0}{j 0.1844} = -j 5.422 \text{ pu}$$ - Total physical fault current: $$I_f = |I_{f, pu}| \times I_{base, line} = 5.422 \times 0.4374 \text{ kA} = 2.371 \text{ kA} \quad (2371 \text{ A})$$ - Short-Circuit MVA ($SCMVA$): $$SCMVA = \frac{MVA_{base}}{X_{th}} = \frac{100}{0.1844} = 542.3 \text{ MVA}$$

Step 6: Generator Fault Current Contributions: Using the current divider rule on the two parallel branches: - Contribution from Generator $G_1$ path: $$I_{f1, pu} = I_{f, pu} \times \frac{X_{branch2}}{X_{branch1} + X_{branch2}} = 5.422 \times \frac{0.7035}{0.9535} = 4.000 \text{ pu}$$ Physical current in $132 \text{ kV}$ line from $T_1$: $$I_{f1} = 4.000 \times 0.4374 = 1.750 \text{ kA}$$ - Contribution from Generator $G_2$ path: $$I_{f2, pu} = I_{f, pu} \times \frac{X_{branch1}}{X_{branch1} + X_{branch2}} = 5.422 \times \frac{0.25}{0.9535} = 1.422 \text{ pu}$$ Physical current from $G_2$: $$I_{f2} = 1.422 \times 0.4374 = 0.622 \text{ kA}$$ Check: $I_{f1} + I_{f2} = 1.750 + 0.622 = 2.372 \text{ kA} \approx I_f$. This rigorous step-by-step working provides the complete solution expected in KTU university evaluations.

Consider a classic KTU university examination problem: A three-phase power system consists of two synchronous generators $G_1$ and $G_2$ feeding a transmission network through step-up transformers $T_1$ and $T_2$. This rigorous step-by-step working provides the complete solution expected in KTU university evaluations.

Consider a classic KTU university examination problem: A three-phase power system consists of two synchronous generators $G_1$ and $G_2$ feeding a transmission network through step-up transformers $T_1$ and $T_2$. The equipment ratings are: - Generator $G_1$: $100 \text{ MVA}, 15 \text{ kV}, X_d'' = 15\%$ ($0.15 \text{ pu}$). - Generator $G_2$: $50 \text{ MVA}, 15 \text{ kV}, X_d'' = 20\%$ ($0.20 \text{ pu}$). - Transformer $T_1$: $100 \text{ MVA}, 15 / 132 \text{ kV}, X = 10\%$ ($0.10 \text{ pu}$). - Transformer $T_2$: $50 \text{ MVA}, 15 / 132 \text{ kV}, X = 8\%$ ($0.08 \text{ pu}$). - Transmission line connecting the high-voltage sides of $T_1$ and $T_2$ has a total series reactance of $X_{line} = 25 \ \Omega$.

(ii) The Thevenin equivalent reactance at the fault point. (iii) The total fault current in kA and fault MVA. (iv) The individual short-circuit current contributions from generators $G_1$ and $G_2$.

Module 2

Symmetrical Components and Unsymmetrical Faults

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: PS2-Q6

State Fortescue's theorem and explain the symmetrical components transformation. Define the complex operator 'a', state its mathematical properties, and prove the invariance of complex power. State Fortescue's theorem and explain the symmetrical components transformation. Define the complex operator 'a', state its mathematical properties, and prove the invariance of complex power. State Fortescue's theorem and explain the symmetrical components transformation. Define the complex operator 'a', state its mathematical properties, and prove the invariance of complex power.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{abc} = A V_{012}, \quad A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \\ 1 & a & a^2 \end{bmatrix}, \quad a = 1\angle 120^\circ, \quad 1 + a + a^2 = 0$$

Fortescue's theorem resolves unbalanced 3-phase phasors into positive ($1, a^2, a$), negative ($1, a, a^2$), and zero ($1, 1, 1$) sequence sets. Complex power is strictly invariant: $S_{3\phi} = 3(V_0 I_0^* + V_1 I_1^* + V_2 I_2^*)$.

$$V_{abc} = A V_{012}, \quad A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \\ 1 & a & a^2 \end{bmatrix}, \quad a = 1\angle 120^\circ, \quad 1 + a + a^2 = 0$$

Fortescue's theorem resolves unbalanced 3-phase phasors into positive ($1, a^2, a$), negative ($1, a, a^2$), and zero ($1, 1, 1$) sequence sets. Complex power is strictly invariant: $S_{3\phi} = 3(V_0 I_0^* + V_1 I_1^* + V_2 I_2^*)$.

$$V_{abc} = A V_{012}, \quad A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \\ 1 & a & a^2 \end{bmatrix}, \quad a = 1\angle 120^\circ, \quad 1 + a + a^2 = 0$$

Fortescue's theorem resolves unbalanced 3-phase phasors into positive ($1, a^2, a$), negative ($1, a, a^2$), and zero ($1, 1, 1$) sequence sets. Complex power is strictly invariant: $S_{3\phi} = 3(V_0 I_0^* + V_1 I_1^* + V_2 I_2^*)$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

In 1918, Dr. C. L. Fortescue introduced the method of symmetrical components, which revolutionized the mathematical analysis of unbalanced polyphase electrical networks. Fortescue's Theorem states that any unbalanced set of $N$ related phase vectors can be uniquely decomposed into $N$ symmetrical components consisting of balanced systems of vectors.

For a three-phase power system with phase variables $V_a, V_b, V_c$, the phasors are resolved into three symmetrical sets: 1. Positive Sequence Components ($V_{a1}, V_{b1}, V_{c1}$): Balanced three-phase phasors of equal magnitude, displaced mutually by $120^\circ$, possessing the identical phase sequence ($a-b-c$) as the original system: $$V_{b1} = a^2 V_{a1}, \quad V_{c1} = a V_{a1}$$ 2. Negative Sequence Components ($V_{a2}, V_{b2}, V_{c2}$): Balanced three-phase phasors of equal magnitude, displaced by $120^\circ$, but possessing the reversed phase sequence ($a-c-b$): $$V_{b2} = a V_{a2}, \quad V_{c2} = a^2 V_{a2}$$ 3. Zero Sequence Components ($V_{a0}, V_{b0}, V_{c0}$): Three uniphase phasors of strictly equal magnitude and identical phase angle, displaced by $0^\circ$: $$V_{a0} = V_{b0} = V_{c0}$$

The Complex Operator $a$: The operator $a$ is a unit phasor defined by a counter-clockwise rotation of $120^\circ$ ($2\pi/3 \text{ radians}$) in the complex plane: $$a = 1 \angle 120^\circ = e^{j 2\pi/3} = -\frac{1}{2} + j \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = -0.5 + j 0.866$$ $$a^2 = 1 \angle 240^\circ = 1 \angle -120^\circ = -\frac{1}{2} - j \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = -0.5 - j 0.866$$ $$a^3 = 1 \angle 360^\circ = 1 \angle 0^\circ = 1$$ Fundamental identities of the $a$-operator include: $$1 + a + a^2 = 1 + (-0.5 + j0.866) + (-0.5 - j0.866) = 0$$ $$a - a^2 = (-0.5 + j0.866) - (-0.5 - j0.866) = j\sqrt{3} = \sqrt{3}\angle 90^\circ$$ $$a^2 - a = -j\sqrt{3} = \sqrt{3}\angle -90^\circ$$

Transformation Matrices: Expressing the phase voltages as the superposition of sequence components: $$V_a = V_{a0} + V_{a1} + V_{a2}$$ $$V_b = V_{b0} + V_{b1} + V_{b2} = V_{a0} + a^2 V_{a1} + a V_{a2}$$ $$V_c = V_{c0} + V_{c1} + V_{c2} = V_{a0} + a V_{a1} + a^2 V_{a2}$$ In vector-matrix form: $$\begin{bmatrix} V_a \\ V_b \\ V_c \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \\ 1 & a & a^2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} V_{a0} \\ V_{a1} \\ V_{a2} \end{bmatrix} \implies V_{abc} = A V_{012}$$ where $A$ is the symmetrical component transformation matrix. Inverting matrix $A$ yields the symmetrical component sequence extraction matrix: $$V_{012} = A^{-1} V_{abc} = \frac{1}{3} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a & a^2 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} V_a \\ V_b \\ V_c \end{bmatrix}$$ Identical transformation matrices apply to phase and sequence currents ($I_{abc} = A I_{012}$).

Invariance of Complex Power: Total three-phase complex power is $S_{3\phi} = V_{abc}^T I_{abc}^*$. Substituting $V_{abc} = A V_{012}$ and $I_{abc}^* = (A I_{012})^* = A^* I_{012}^*$: $$S_{3\phi} = (A V_{012})^T (A^* I_{012}^*) = V_{012}^T (A^T A^*) I_{012}^*$$ Evaluating $A^T A^*$: $$A^T A^* = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \\ 1 & a & a^2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a & a^2 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 3 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 3 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 3 \end{bmatrix} = 3 I$$ Therefore, the total three-phase complex power expressed in symmetrical components is: $$S_{3\phi} = 3 \left( V_{a0} I_{a0}^* + V_{a1} I_{a1}^* + V_{a2} I_{a2}^* \right)$$ This proves that complex power can be evaluated independently in the three decoupled sequence networks and summed with a scaling factor of 3.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

In 1918, Dr. C. L. Fortescue introduced the method of symmetrical components, which revolutionized the mathematical analysis of unbalanced polyphase electrical networks. Fortescue's Theorem states that any unbalanced set of $N$ related phase vectors can be uniquely decomposed into $N$ symmetrical components consisting of balanced systems of vectors.

For a three-phase power system with phase variables $V_a, V_b, V_c$, the phasors are resolved into three symmetrical sets: 1. Positive Sequence Components ($V_{a1}, V_{b1}, V_{c1}$): Balanced three-phase phasors of equal magnitude, displaced mutually by $120^\circ$, possessing the identical phase sequence ($a-b-c$) as the original system: $$V_{b1} = a^2 V_{a1}, \quad V_{c1} = a V_{a1}$$ 2. Negative Sequence Components ($V_{a2}, V_{b2}, V_{c2}$): Balanced three-phase phasors of equal magnitude, displaced by $120^\circ$, but possessing the reversed phase sequence ($a-c-b$): $$V_{b2} = a V_{a2}, \quad V_{c2} = a^2 V_{a2}$$ 3. Zero Sequence Components ($V_{a0}, V_{b0}, V_{c0}$): Three uniphase phasors of strictly equal magnitude and identical phase angle, displaced by $0^\circ$: $$V_{a0} = V_{b0} = V_{c0}$$

The Complex Operator $a$: The operator $a$ is a unit phasor defined by a counter-clockwise rotation of $120^\circ$ ($2\pi/3 \text{ radians}$) in the complex plane: $$a = 1 \angle 120^\circ = e^{j 2\pi/3} = -\frac{1}{2} + j \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = -0.5 + j 0.866$$ $$a^2 = 1 \angle 240^\circ = 1 \angle -120^\circ = -\frac{1}{2} - j \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = -0.5 - j 0.866$$ $$a^3 = 1 \angle 360^\circ = 1 \angle 0^\circ = 1$$ Fundamental identities of the $a$-operator include: $$1 + a + a^2 = 1 + (-0.5 + j0.866) + (-0.5 - j0.866) = 0$$ $$a - a^2 = (-0.5 + j0.866) - (-0.5 - j0.866) = j\sqrt{3} = \sqrt{3}\angle 90^\circ$$ $$a^2 - a = -j\sqrt{3} = \sqrt{3}\angle -90^\circ$$

Transformation Matrices: Expressing the phase voltages as the superposition of sequence components: $$V_a = V_{a0} + V_{a1} + V_{a2}$$ $$V_b = V_{b0} + V_{b1} + V_{b2} = V_{a0} + a^2 V_{a1} + a V_{a2}$$ $$V_c = V_{c0} + V_{c1} + V_{c2} = V_{a0} + a V_{a1} + a^2 V_{a2}$$ In vector-matrix form: $$\begin{bmatrix} V_a \\ V_b \\ V_c \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \\ 1 & a & a^2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} V_{a0} \\ V_{a1} \\ V_{a2} \end{bmatrix} \implies V_{abc} = A V_{012}$$ where $A$ is the symmetrical component transformation matrix. Inverting matrix $A$ yields the symmetrical component sequence extraction matrix: $$V_{012} = A^{-1} V_{abc} = \frac{1}{3} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a & a^2 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} V_a \\ V_b \\ V_c \end{bmatrix}$$ Identical transformation matrices apply to phase and sequence currents ($I_{abc} = A I_{012}$).

Invariance of Complex Power: Total three-phase complex power is $S_{3\phi} = V_{abc}^T I_{abc}^*$. Substituting $V_{abc} = A V_{012}$ and $I_{abc}^* = (A I_{012})^* = A^* I_{012}^*$: $$S_{3\phi} = (A V_{012})^T (A^* I_{012}^*) = V_{012}^T (A^T A^*) I_{012}^*$$ Evaluating $A^T A^*$: $$A^T A^* = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \\ 1 & a & a^2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a & a^2 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 3 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 3 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 3 \end{bmatrix} = 3 I$$ Therefore, the total three-phase complex power expressed in symmetrical components is: $$S_{3\phi} = 3 \left( V_{a0} I_{a0}^* + V_{a1} I_{a1}^* + V_{a2} I_{a2}^* \right)$$ This proves that complex power can be evaluated independently in the three decoupled sequence networks and summed with a scaling factor of 3.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

In 1918, Dr. C. L. Fortescue introduced the method of symmetrical components, which revolutionized the mathematical analysis of unbalanced polyphase electrical networks. Fortescue's Theorem states that any unbalanced set of $N$ related phase vectors can be uniquely decomposed into $N$ symmetrical components consisting of balanced systems of vectors.

For a three-phase power system with phase variables $V_a, V_b, V_c$, the phasors are resolved into three symmetrical sets: 1. Positive Sequence Components ($V_{a1}, V_{b1}, V_{c1}$): Balanced three-phase phasors of equal magnitude, displaced mutually by $120^\circ$, possessing the identical phase sequence ($a-b-c$) as the original system: $$V_{b1} = a^2 V_{a1}, \quad V_{c1} = a V_{a1}$$ 2. Negative Sequence Components ($V_{a2}, V_{b2}, V_{c2}$): Balanced three-phase phasors of equal magnitude, displaced by $120^\circ$, but possessing the reversed phase sequence ($a-c-b$): $$V_{b2} = a V_{a2}, \quad V_{c2} = a^2 V_{a2}$$ 3. Zero Sequence Components ($V_{a0}, V_{b0}, V_{c0}$): Three uniphase phasors of strictly equal magnitude and identical phase angle, displaced by $0^\circ$: $$V_{a0} = V_{b0} = V_{c0}$$

The Complex Operator $a$: The operator $a$ is a unit phasor defined by a counter-clockwise rotation of $120^\circ$ ($2\pi/3 \text{ radians}$) in the complex plane: $$a = 1 \angle 120^\circ = e^{j 2\pi/3} = -\frac{1}{2} + j \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = -0.5 + j 0.866$$ $$a^2 = 1 \angle 240^\circ = 1 \angle -120^\circ = -\frac{1}{2} - j \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = -0.5 - j 0.866$$ $$a^3 = 1 \angle 360^\circ = 1 \angle 0^\circ = 1$$ Fundamental identities of the $a$-operator include: $$1 + a + a^2 = 1 + (-0.5 + j0.866) + (-0.5 - j0.866) = 0$$ $$a - a^2 = (-0.5 + j0.866) - (-0.5 - j0.866) = j\sqrt{3} = \sqrt{3}\angle 90^\circ$$ $$a^2 - a = -j\sqrt{3} = \sqrt{3}\angle -90^\circ$$

Transformation Matrices: Expressing the phase voltages as the superposition of sequence components: $$V_a = V_{a0} + V_{a1} + V_{a2}$$ $$V_b = V_{b0} + V_{b1} + V_{b2} = V_{a0} + a^2 V_{a1} + a V_{a2}$$ $$V_c = V_{c0} + V_{c1} + V_{c2} = V_{a0} + a V_{a1} + a^2 V_{a2}$$ In vector-matrix form: $$\begin{bmatrix} V_a \\ V_b \\ V_c \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \\ 1 & a & a^2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} V_{a0} \\ V_{a1} \\ V_{a2} \end{bmatrix} \implies V_{abc} = A V_{012}$$ where $A$ is the symmetrical component transformation matrix. Inverting matrix $A$ yields the symmetrical component sequence extraction matrix: $$V_{012} = A^{-1} V_{abc} = \frac{1}{3} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a & a^2 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} V_a \\ V_b \\ V_c \end{bmatrix}$$ Identical transformation matrices apply to phase and sequence currents ($I_{abc} = A I_{012}$).

Invariance of Complex Power: Total three-phase complex power is $S_{3\phi} = V_{abc}^T I_{abc}^*$. Substituting $V_{abc} = A V_{012}$ and $I_{abc}^* = (A I_{012})^* = A^* I_{012}^*$: $$S_{3\phi} = (A V_{012})^T (A^* I_{012}^*) = V_{012}^T (A^T A^*) I_{012}^*$$ Evaluating $A^T A^*$: $$A^T A^* = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \\ 1 & a & a^2 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a & a^2 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 3 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 3 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 3 \end{bmatrix} = 3 I$$ Therefore, the total three-phase complex power expressed in symmetrical components is: $$S_{3\phi} = 3 \left( V_{a0} I_{a0}^* + V_{a1} I_{a1}^* + V_{a2} I_{a2}^* \right)$$ This proves that complex power can be evaluated independently in the three decoupled sequence networks and summed with a scaling factor of 3.

Draw and explain the positive, negative, and zero sequence equivalent circuits of synchronous generators, three-phase transformers (with various winding and grounding connections), and transmission lines. Draw and explain the positive, negative, and zero sequence equivalent circuits of synchronous generators, three-phase transformers (with various winding and grounding connections), and transmission lines. Draw and explain the positive, negative, and zero sequence equivalent circuits of synchronous generators, three-phase transformers (with various winding and grounding connections), and transmission lines.

$$Z_0 = Z_{g0} + 3 Z_n, \quad Z_1 = Z_2 = Z_{leakage} \text{ (Transformer)}, \quad Z_0 \approx 2.5 \text{ to } 3.5 \times Z_1 \text{ (Line)}$$

⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$Z_0 = Z_{g0} + 3 Z_n, \quad Z_1 = Z_2 = Z_{leakage} \text{ (Transformer)}, \quad Z_0 \approx 2.5 \text{ to } 3.5 \times Z_1 \text{ (Line)}$$

Generator neutral grounding impedance appears as $3Z_n$ in zero-sequence network. Transformers block zero sequence unless grounded star ($Y_g$); delta ($\Delta$) circulates zero sequence internally.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

To calculate unsymmetrical faults, power systems are transformed into three uncoupled single-phase sequence networks: the Positive-Sequence Network, the Negative-Sequence Network, and the Zero-Sequence Network. The physical characteristics and sequence impedances of the core equipment are formulated as follows:

1. Synchronous Generator: - Positive Sequence Network: The rotor rotates synchronously in the identical direction as the positive-sequence stator MMF. The air-gap flux is stationary relative to the rotor field poles. Hence, the positive-sequence impedance is simply the machine reactance ($Z_1 = jX_d''$ or $jX_d'$). Because the generator produces balanced positive-sequence internal EMFs, an internal source $E_a$ is present: $$V_{a1} = E_a - I_{a1} Z_1$$ - Negative Sequence Network: The negative-sequence stator currents set up a magnetic field rotating at synchronous speed in the direction opposite to rotor rotation. This field sweeps past the rotor at twice synchronous speed ($2\omega_s$), inducing high-frequency eddy currents in the damper bars and rotor surface. These eddy currents heavily choke the penetration of flux, yielding a small negative-sequence impedance ($Z_2 \approx j \frac{X_d'' + X_q''}{2}$). Because the machine generates no negative-sequence voltages, no internal EMF source exists: $$V_{a2} = -I_{a2} Z_2$$ - Zero Sequence Network: Zero-sequence currents in the three stator phases are identical in magnitude and time phase ($I_{a0} = I_{b0} = I_{c0}$). The MMFs produced in the three spatially displaced windings cancel in the main air gap, producing no net fundamental air-gap flux. Only slot and end-turn leakage fluxes exist, resulting in a very small internal zero-sequence impedance $Z_{g0}$. If the generator neutral is grounded through an impedance $Z_n$, the total current entering ground is $I_n = I_a + I_b + I_c = 3 I_{a0}$. The neutral potential with respect to ground is $V_n = -I_n Z_n = -3 I_{a0} Z_n$. The phase-to-ground zero-sequence voltage drop is: $$V_{a0} = -I_{a0} Z_{g0} - 3 I_{a0} Z_n = -I_{a0}(Z_{g0} + 3 Z_n) = -I_{a0} Z_0$$ Thus, the total zero-sequence impedance is: $$Z_0 = Z_{g0} + 3 Z_n$$

2. Three-Phase Transformers: Because a transformer is a static electromagnetic device, phase rotation does not affect core reluctance or leakage flux paths. Therefore: $$Z_1 = Z_2 = Z_{leakage}$$ The Zero-Sequence Network of a transformer depends critically on its winding connections and neutral grounding: - Core Rules for Zero Sequence Flow: 1. Zero-sequence current can flow into a star ($Y$) winding if and only if the neutral is connected to ground. If ungrounded, zero-sequence current is physically blocked ($I_0 = 0$, open circuit). 2. If the neutral is grounded through $Z_n$, an impedance $3Z_n$ is inserted in series with the leakage impedance. 3. A delta ($\Delta$) winding provides a closed circulating path for zero-sequence currents ($I_{a0} + I_{b0} + I_{c0}$ circulate inside the delta mesh), but zero-sequence current cannot enter or leave the external line terminals. Thus, delta windings isolate zero-sequence currents between primary and secondary sides. - Common Configurations: - Star Grounded - Delta ($Y_g - \Delta$): Zero-sequence current can flow from the power system into the $Y_g$ side and to ground; the $\Delta$ side forms a path to ground reference on the secondary, but isolates the secondary line terminals (shunt path to reference). - Star Grounded - Star Grounded ($Y_g - Y_g$): A complete through-path exists from primary lines to secondary lines, provided both neutrals are grounded and return paths exist. - Star Ungrounded - Delta ($Y - \Delta$): Open circuit on star side, shunt closed loop on delta side. - Delta - Delta ($\Delta - \Delta$): Both line terminals are completely open-circuited to zero-sequence current; circulating currents are confined within both delta meshes.

3. Transmission Lines: For completely transposed overhead transmission lines, self impedance is $Z_s$ and mutual coupling between conductors is $Z_m$. - Positive Sequence Impedance: $$Z_1 = Z_s - Z_m$$ - Negative Sequence Impedance: $$Z_2 = Z_s - Z_m = Z_1$$ - Zero Sequence Impedance: Zero-sequence currents return through the earth or ground wires. Due to high earth resistivity and large loop return distance: $$Z_0 = Z_s + 2 Z_m + 3 Z_e$$ In typical high-voltage lines, $Z_0$ is significantly larger than $Z_1$, usually in the ratio $Z_0 \approx 2.5 \text{ to } 3.5 \times Z_1$.

Long Derivation ID: PS2-Q8

Derive the expressions for sequence currents and fault current for a Single Line-to-Ground (LG) fault on phase 'a' through a fault impedance Zf. Draw the sequence network interconnection. Derive the expressions for sequence currents and fault current for a Single Line-to-Ground (LG) fault on phase 'a' through a fault impedance Zf. Draw the sequence network interconnection. Derive the expressions for sequence currents and fault current for a Single Line-to-Ground (LG) fault on phase 'a' through a fault impedance Zf. Draw the sequence network interconnection.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I_{a0} = I_{a1} = I_{a2} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}, \quad I_f = 3 I_{a1} = \frac{3 E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}$$

Single Line-to-Ground fault enforces identical sequence currents ($I_{a0} = I_{a1} = I_{a2}$), requiring a Series interconnection of positive, negative, and zero sequence networks with $3Z_f$ inserted.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Single Line-to-Ground (LG) fault is by far the most frequent unsymmetrical fault in electrical power systems, accounting for approximately 70% to 80% of all system disturbances. It occurs when one phase conductor contacts the ground or a grounded tower structure, often initiated by lightning flashover or tree contact.

Consider a balanced three-phase synchronous generator operating with internal sequence voltages: positive-sequence source $E_a$, $E_b = a^2 E_a$, $E_c = a E_a$, with zero negative- and zero-sequence generated EMFs. Let an LG fault occur on phase $a$ through a fault impedance $Z_f$ to ground.

Step 1: Formulate the Physical Boundary Conditions at the Fault Point: Because phases $b$ and $c$ are healthy and open-circuited at the fault point: $$I_b = 0$$ $$I_c = 0$$ The terminal voltage of the faulted phase $a$ is dropped across the fault impedance: $$V_a = I_a Z_f$$

Step 2: Determine Sequence Currents from Symmetrical Components: Applying the inverse transformation $I_{012} = A^{-1} I_{abc}$: $$\begin{bmatrix} I_{a0} \\ I_{a1} \\ I_{a2} \end{bmatrix} = \frac{1}{3} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a & a^2 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} I_a \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$$ Carrying out the matrix multiplication: $$I_{a0} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + 0 + 0) = \frac{1}{3} I_a$$ $$I_{a1} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + a(0) + a^2(0)) = \frac{1}{3} I_a$$ $$I_{a2} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + a^2(0) + a(0)) = \frac{1}{3} I_a$$ This yields the fundamental sequence current identity: $$I_{a0} = I_{a1} = I_{a2} = \frac{1}{3} I_a$$ The physical fact that all three sequence currents are strictly identical in magnitude and phase indicates that the three sequence networks must be connected in Series!

Step 3: Relate Sequence Voltages and Sequence Currents: The terminal sequence voltages expressed in terms of Thevenin sequence impedances are: $$V_{a0} = -I_{a0} Z_0$$ $$V_{a1} = E_a - I_{a1} Z_1$$ $$V_{a2} = -I_{a2} Z_2$$

Step 4: Enforce the Voltage Boundary Condition: Expressing the faulted phase voltage in terms of its sequence components: $$V_a = V_{a0} + V_{a1} + V_{a2} = I_a Z_f$$ Substituting $I_a = 3 I_{a1}$: $$V_{a0} + V_{a1} + V_{a2} = 3 I_{a1} Z_f$$ Substituting the sequence voltage expressions: $$(-I_{a0} Z_0) + (E_a - I_{a1} Z_1) + (-I_{a2} Z_2) = 3 I_{a1} Z_f$$ Since $I_{a0} = I_{a2} = I_{a1}$: $$-I_{a1} Z_0 + E_a - I_{a1} Z_1 - I_{a1} Z_2 = 3 I_{a1} Z_f$$ Rearranging terms to isolate $E_a$: $$E_a = I_{a1} Z_1 + I_{a1} Z_2 + I_{a1} Z_0 + 3 I_{a1} Z_f = I_{a1} \left( Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f \right)$$ Solving explicitly for the sequence current $I_{a1}$: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}$$

Step 5: Determine Total Fault Current: The total physical fault current entering the ground is $I_f = I_a = 3 I_{a1}$: $$I_f = I_a = \frac{3 E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}$$ For a bolted dead-ground fault ($Z_f = 0$): $$I_f = \frac{3 E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0}$$

Sequence Network Interconnection: To satisfy both $I_{a0} = I_{a1} = I_{a2}$ and $E_a - I_{a1}(Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3Z_f) = 0$, the positive-, negative-, and zero-sequence Thevenin networks are connected in a single closed Series loop with an added impedance $3Z_f$ inserted in the return path.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

The Single Line-to-Ground (LG) fault is by far the most frequent unsymmetrical fault in electrical power systems, accounting for approximately 70% to 80% of all system disturbances. It occurs when one phase conductor contacts the ground or a grounded tower structure, often initiated by lightning flashover or tree contact.

Consider a balanced three-phase synchronous generator operating with internal sequence voltages: positive-sequence source $E_a$, $E_b = a^2 E_a$, $E_c = a E_a$, with zero negative- and zero-sequence generated EMFs. Let an LG fault occur on phase $a$ through a fault impedance $Z_f$ to ground.

Step 1: Formulate the Physical Boundary Conditions at the Fault Point: Because phases $b$ and $c$ are healthy and open-circuited at the fault point: $$I_b = 0$$ $$I_c = 0$$ The terminal voltage of the faulted phase $a$ is dropped across the fault impedance: $$V_a = I_a Z_f$$

Step 2: Determine Sequence Currents from Symmetrical Components: Applying the inverse transformation $I_{012} = A^{-1} I_{abc}$: $$\begin{bmatrix} I_{a0} \\ I_{a1} \\ I_{a2} \end{bmatrix} = \frac{1}{3} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a & a^2 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} I_a \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$$ Carrying out the matrix multiplication: $$I_{a0} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + 0 + 0) = \frac{1}{3} I_a$$ $$I_{a1} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + a(0) + a^2(0)) = \frac{1}{3} I_a$$ $$I_{a2} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + a^2(0) + a(0)) = \frac{1}{3} I_a$$ This yields the fundamental sequence current identity: $$I_{a0} = I_{a1} = I_{a2} = \frac{1}{3} I_a$$ The physical fact that all three sequence currents are strictly identical in magnitude and phase indicates that the three sequence networks must be connected in Series!

Step 3: Relate Sequence Voltages and Sequence Currents: The terminal sequence voltages expressed in terms of Thevenin sequence impedances are: $$V_{a0} = -I_{a0} Z_0$$ $$V_{a1} = E_a - I_{a1} Z_1$$ $$V_{a2} = -I_{a2} Z_2$$

Step 4: Enforce the Voltage Boundary Condition: Expressing the faulted phase voltage in terms of its sequence components: $$V_a = V_{a0} + V_{a1} + V_{a2} = I_a Z_f$$ Substituting $I_a = 3 I_{a1}$: $$V_{a0} + V_{a1} + V_{a2} = 3 I_{a1} Z_f$$ Substituting the sequence voltage expressions: $$(-I_{a0} Z_0) + (E_a - I_{a1} Z_1) + (-I_{a2} Z_2) = 3 I_{a1} Z_f$$ Since $I_{a0} = I_{a2} = I_{a1}$: $$-I_{a1} Z_0 + E_a - I_{a1} Z_1 - I_{a1} Z_2 = 3 I_{a1} Z_f$$ Rearranging terms to isolate $E_a$: $$E_a = I_{a1} Z_1 + I_{a1} Z_2 + I_{a1} Z_0 + 3 I_{a1} Z_f = I_{a1} \left( Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f \right)$$ Solving explicitly for the sequence current $I_{a1}$: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}$$

Step 5: Determine Total Fault Current: The total physical fault current entering the ground is $I_f = I_a = 3 I_{a1}$: $$I_f = I_a = \frac{3 E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}$$ For a bolted dead-ground fault ($Z_f = 0$): $$I_f = \frac{3 E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0}$$

Sequence Network Interconnection: To satisfy both $I_{a0} = I_{a1} = I_{a2}$ and $E_a - I_{a1}(Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_0 + 3Z_f) = 0$, the positive-, negative-, and zero-sequence Thevenin networks are connected in a single closed Series loop with an added impedance $3Z_f$ inserted in the return path.

Step 2: Determine Sequence Currents from Symmetrical Components: Applying the inverse transformation $I_{012} = A^{-1} I_{abc}$: $$\begin{bmatrix} I_{a0} \\ I_{a1} \\ I_{a2} \end{bmatrix} = \frac{1}{3} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a & a^2 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} I_a \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$$ Carrying out the matrix multiplication: $$I_{a0} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + 0 + 0) = \frac{1}{3} I_a$$ $$I_{a1} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + a(0) + a^2(0)) = \frac{1}{3} I_a$$ $$I_{a2} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + a^2(0) + a(0)) = \frac{1}{3} I_a$$ This yields the fundamental sequence current identity: $$I_{a0} = I_{a1} = I_{a2} = \frac{1}{3} I_a$$ The physical fact that all three sequence currents are strictly identical in magnitude and phase indicates that the three sequence networks must be connected in Series!

Derive the expressions for sequence currents and fault current for a Line-to-Line (LL) fault between phases 'b' and 'c' through fault impedance Zf. Draw the sequence network interconnection. $$I_{a0} = 0, \quad I_{a1} = -I_{a2} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f}, \quad I_f = -j\sqrt{3} I_{a1} = \frac{\sqrt{3} E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f} \angle -90^\circ$$

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A Line-to-Line (LL) fault occurs when two phase conductors make physical contact or become bridged by an electric arc without involving ground. LL faults represent approximately 15% of all power system faults.

Let a line-to-line fault occur between phases $b$ and $c$ through an arc fault impedance $Z_f$. The system is excited by positive-sequence internal EMF $E_a$.

Step 1: Formulate the Physical Boundary Conditions at the Fault Point: 1. Phase $a$ remains healthy and unfaulted: $$I_a = 0$$ 2. Because current flows out of phase $b$ directly into phase $c$ through impedance $Z_f$ with no connection to ground: $$I_b = -I_c \implies I_b + I_c = 0$$ 3. The potential difference between the faulted phases equals the voltage drop across $Z_f$: $$V_b - V_c = I_b Z_f$$

Step 2: Evaluate Symmetrical Component Currents: Using the inverse symmetrical component transformation: - Zero sequence current: $$I_{a0} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + I_b + I_c) = \frac{1}{3}(0 + I_b - I_b) = 0$$ Because zero sequence current is identically zero, the zero-sequence network is completely open and inactive during an LL fault! - Positive sequence current: $$I_{a1} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + a I_b + a^2 I_c) = \frac{1}{3}(0 + a I_b + a^2(-I_b)) = \frac{1}{3}(a - a^2) I_b$$ Using the operator identity $a - a^2 = j\sqrt{3}$: $$I_{a1} = \frac{j\sqrt{3}}{3} I_b = \frac{j}{\sqrt{3}} I_b \implies I_b = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{j} I_{a1} = -j\sqrt{3} I_{a1}$$ - Negative sequence current: $$I_{a2} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + a^2 I_b + a I_c) = \frac{1}{3}(0 + a^2 I_b + a(-I_b)) = \frac{1}{3}(a^2 - a) I_b$$ Using $a^2 - a = -j\sqrt{3}$: $$I_{a2} = -\frac{j\sqrt{3}}{3} I_b = -\frac{j}{\sqrt{3}} I_b = -I_{a1}$$ This establishes the vital sequence current relationship: $$I_{a1} = -I_{a2}, \quad I_{a0} = 0$$

Step 3: Evaluate Sequence Voltage Relationship: Expressing the phase voltages $V_b$ and $V_c$ in terms of sequence components: $$V_b = V_{a0} + a^2 V_{a1} + a V_{a2}$$ $$V_c = V_{a0} + a V_{a1} + a^2 V_{a2}$$ Subtracting $V_c$ from $V_b$: $$V_b - V_c = (a^2 - a) V_{a1} + (a - a^2) V_{a2} = (a^2 - a)(V_{a1} - V_{a2}) = -j\sqrt{3}(V_{a1} - V_{a2})$$ Equating this to the boundary condition $V_b - V_c = I_b Z_f = (-j\sqrt{3} I_{a1}) Z_f$: $$-j\sqrt{3}(V_{a1} - V_{a2}) = -j\sqrt{3} I_{a1} Z_f$$ Dividing both sides by $-j\sqrt{3}$: $$V_{a1} - V_{a2} = I_{a1} Z_f$$

Step 4: Solve for Sequence Currents and Fault Current: Substituting the Thevenin voltage equations $V_{a1} = E_a - I_{a1} Z_1$ and $V_{a2} = -I_{a2} Z_2 = -(-I_{a1}) Z_2 = I_{a1} Z_2$: $$(E_a - I_{a1} Z_1) - (I_{a1} Z_2) = I_{a1} Z_f$$ $$E_a = I_{a1} Z_1 + I_{a1} Z_2 + I_{a1} Z_f = I_{a1} (Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f)$$ Solving for $I_{a1}$: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f}$$ The physical fault current in phase $b$ is: $$I_f = I_b = -j\sqrt{3} I_{a1} = \frac{-j\sqrt{3} E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f} = \frac{\sqrt{3} E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f} \angle -90^\circ$$ For a bolted dead-short fault ($Z_f = 0$): $$I_f = \frac{\sqrt{3} E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2}$$ Assuming $Z_1 \approx Z_2$, the magnitude of line-to-line fault current is: $$|I_{f, LL}| = \frac{\sqrt{3} E_a}{2 Z_1} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \left(\frac{E_a}{Z_1}\right) \approx 0.866 \times I_{f, 3\phi}$$ The line-to-line fault current is approximately 86.6% of the symmetrical three-phase fault current.

Sequence Network Interconnection: Because $I_{a1} = -I_{a2}$ and $V_{a1} - V_{a2} = I_{a1} Z_f$, the positive-sequence network and negative-sequence network are connected in Parallel opposition through the fault impedance $Z_f$. The zero-sequence network remains completely disconnected.

Step 4: Solve for Sequence Currents and Fault Current: Substituting the Thevenin voltage equations $V_{a1} = E_a - I_{a1} Z_1$ and $V_{a2} = -I_{a2} Z_2 = -(-I_{a1}) Z_2 = I_{a1} Z_2$: $$(E_a - I_{a1} Z_1) - (I_{a1} Z_2) = I_{a1} Z_f$$ $$E_a = I_{a1} Z_1 + I_{a1} Z_2 + I_{a1} Z_f = I_{a1} (Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f)$$ Solving for $I_{a1}$: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f}$$ The physical fault current in phase $b$ is: $$I_f = I_b = -j\sqrt{3} I_{a1} = \frac{-j\sqrt{3} E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f} = \frac{\sqrt{3} E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f} \angle -90^\circ$$ For a bolted dead-short fault ($Z_f = 0$): $$I_f = \frac{\sqrt{3} E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2}$$ Assuming $Z_1 \approx Z_2$, the magnitude of line-to-line fault current is: $$|I_{f, LL}| = \frac{\sqrt{3} E_a}{2 Z_1} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \left(\frac{E_a}{Z_1}\right) \approx 0.866 \times I_{f, 3\phi}$$ The line-to-line fault current is approximately 86.6% of the symmetrical three-phase fault current.

Sequence Network Interconnection: Because $I_{a1} = -I_{a2}$ and $V_{a1} - V_{a2} = I_{a1} Z_f$, the positive-sequence network and negative-sequence network are connected in Parallel opposition through the fault impedance $Z_f$. The zero-sequence network remains completely disconnected.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

A Line-to-Line (LL) fault occurs when two phase conductors make physical contact or become bridged by an electric arc without involving ground. LL faults represent approximately 15% of all power system faults.

The potential difference between the faulted phases equals the voltage drop across $Z_f$: $$V_b - V_c = I_b Z_f$$

Step 2: Evaluate Symmetrical Component Currents: Using the inverse symmetrical component transformation: - Zero sequence current: $$I_{a0} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + I_b + I_c) = \frac{1}{3}(0 + I_b - I_b) = 0$$ Because zero sequence current is identically zero, the zero-sequence network is completely open and inactive during an LL fault! - Positive sequence current: $$I_{a1} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + a I_b + a^2 I_c) = \frac{1}{3}(0 + a I_b + a^2(-I_b)) = \frac{1}{3}(a - a^2) I_b$$ Using the operator identity $a - a^2 = j\sqrt{3}$: $$I_{a1} = \frac{j\sqrt{3}}{3} I_b = \frac{j}{\sqrt{3}} I_b \implies I_b = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{j} I_{a1} = -j\sqrt{3} I_{a1}$$ - Negative sequence current: $$I_{a2} = \frac{1}{3}(I_a + a^2 I_b + a I_c) = \frac{1}{3}(0 + a^2 I_b + a(-I_b)) = \frac{1}{3}(a^2 - a) I_b$$ Using $a^2 - a = -j\sqrt{3}$: $$I_{a2} = -\frac{j\sqrt{3}}{3} I_b = -\frac{j}{\sqrt{3}} I_b = -I_{a1}$$ This establishes the vital sequence current relationship: $$I_{a1} = -I_{a2}, \quad I_{a0} = 0$$

Step 3: Evaluate Sequence Voltage Relationship: Expressing the phase voltages $V_b$ and $V_c$ in terms of sequence components: $$V_b = V_{a0} + a^2 V_{a1} + a V_{a2}$$ $$V_c = V_{a0} + a V_{a1} + a^2 V_{a2}$$ Subtracting $V_c$ from $V_b$: $$V_b - V_c = (a^2 - a) V_{a1} + (a - a^2) V_{a2} = (a^2 - a)(V_{a1} - V_{a2}) = -j\sqrt{3}(V_{a1} - V_{a2})$$ Equating this to the boundary condition $V_b - V_c = I_b Z_f = (-j\sqrt{3} I_{a1}) Z_f$: $$-j\sqrt{3}(V_{a1} - V_{a2}) = -j\sqrt{3} I_{a1} Z_f$$ Dividing both sides by $-j\sqrt{3}$: $$V_{a1} - V_{a2} = I_{a1} Z_f$$

Step 4: Solve for Sequence Currents and Fault Current: Substituting the Thevenin voltage equations $V_{a1} = E_a - I_{a1} Z_1$ and $V_{a2} = -I_{a2} Z_2 = -(-I_{a1}) Z_2 = I_{a1} Z_2$: $$(E_a - I_{a1} Z_1) - (I_{a1} Z_2) = I_{a1} Z_f$$ $$E_a = I_{a1} Z_1 + I_{a1} Z_2 + I_{a1} Z_f = I_{a1} (Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f)$$ Solving for $I_{a1}$: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f}$$ The physical fault current in phase $b$ is: $$I_f = I_b = -j\sqrt{3} I_{a1} = \frac{-j\sqrt{3} E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f} = \frac{\sqrt{3} E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2 + Z_f} \angle -90^\circ$$ For a bolted dead-short fault ($Z_f = 0$): $$I_f = \frac{\sqrt{3} E_a}{Z_1 + Z_2}$$ Assuming $Z_1 \approx Z_2$, the magnitude of line-to-line fault current is: $$|I_{f, LL}| = \frac{\sqrt{3} E_a}{2 Z_1} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \left(\frac{E_a}{Z_1}\right) \approx 0.866 \times I_{f, 3\phi}$$ The line-to-line fault current is approximately 86.6% of the symmetrical three-phase fault current.

Sequence Network Interconnection: Because $I_{a1} = -I_{a2}$ and $V_{a1} - V_{a2} = I_{a1} Z_f$, the positive-sequence network and negative-sequence network are connected in Parallel opposition through the fault impedance $Z_f$. The zero-sequence network remains completely disconnected.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 2.4: Parallel Interconnection of Positive and Negative Sequence Networks for Line-to-Line Fault — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)
7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: PS2-Q10

Derive the expressions for sequence currents, sequence voltages, and ground fault current for a Double Line-to-Ground (LLG) fault on phases 'b' and 'c'. Draw the sequence network interconnection. Derive the expressions for sequence currents, sequence voltages, and ground fault current for a Double Line-to-Ground (LLG) fault on phases 'b' and 'c'. Draw the sequence network interconnection. Derive the expressions for sequence currents, sequence voltages, and ground fault current for a Double Line-to-Ground (LLG) fault on phases 'b' and 'c'. Draw the sequence network interconnection.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{a1} = V_{a2} = V_{a0} - 3 I_{a0} Z_f, \quad I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + \frac{Z_2 (Z_0 + 3 Z_f)}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}}, \quad I_f = 3 I_{a0}$$

Double Line-to-Ground fault enforces equal sequence voltages, requiring a Parallel interconnection of positive, negative, and zero sequence networks; total ground fault current is $3I_{a0}$.

⚡ Key Equation

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_{a1} = V_{a2} = V_{a0} - 3 I_{a0} Z_f, \quad I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + \frac{Z_2 (Z_0 + 3 Z_f)}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}}, \quad I_f = 3 I_{a0}$$

Double Line-to-Ground fault enforces equal sequence voltages, requiring a Parallel interconnection of positive, negative, and zero sequence networks; total ground fault current is $3I_{a0}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A Double Line-to-Ground (LLG) fault occurs when two phase conductors simultaneously contact each other and fall to ground, or when two phases contact a grounded metallic structure. LLG faults constitute roughly 10% of all power system faults.

Let the fault occur on phases $b$ and $c$ through a common ground impedance $Z_f$.

Step 1: Formulate the Physical Boundary Conditions at the Fault Point: 1. Phase $a$ is healthy and open-circuited: $$I_a = 0$$ 2. Phases $b$ and $c$ are tied together to ground through impedance $Z_f$: $$V_b = V_c = (I_b + I_c) Z_f$$ For a bolted fault ($Z_f = 0$): $$V_b = 0, \quad V_c = 0$$

Step 2: Symmetrical Component Voltage Relationships: Using the inverse transformation $V_{012} = A^{-1} V_{abc}$ for $V_b = V_c$: $$\begin{bmatrix} V_{a0} \\ V_{a1} \\ V_{a2} \end{bmatrix} = \frac{1}{3} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a & a^2 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} V_a \\ V_b \\ V_b \end{bmatrix}$$ Evaluating sequence voltages: $$V_{a0} = \frac{1}{3}(V_a + 2 V_b)$$ $$V_{a1} = \frac{1}{3}(V_a + (a + a^2) V_b) = \frac{1}{3}(V_a - V_b)$$ $$V_{a2} = \frac{1}{3}(V_a + (a^2 + a) V_b) = \frac{1}{3}(V_a - V_b)$$ Notice that: $$V_{a1} = V_{a2}$$ For a bolted fault ($V_b = 0$): $$V_{a0} = V_{a1} = V_{a2} = \frac{1}{3} V_a$$ When fault impedance $Z_f$ is present, the total ground return current is: $$I_n = I_b + I_c = (I_a + I_b + I_c) = 3 I_{a0}$$ The voltage of the faulted phases with respect to true ground is: $$V_b = V_c = 3 I_{a0} Z_f$$ Substituting this back yields the sequence voltage relation: $$V_{a1} = V_{a2} = V_{a0} - 3 I_{a0} Z_f$$ This condition proves that the sequence voltages are identical across all three networks, mandating a Parallel Interconnection of positive, negative, and zero sequence networks!

Step 3: Current Boundary Condition in Sequence Domain: Since $I_a = 0$: $$I_a = I_{a0} + I_{a1} + I_{a2} = 0 \implies I_{a1} = -(I_{a2} + I_{a0})$$ This exactly matches Kirchhoff's Current Law at the parallel junction of the three networks.

Step 4: Solve for Sequence Currents and Total Fault Current: Viewing the system from the positive-sequence source $E_a$, the negative-sequence impedance $Z_2$ and the modified zero-sequence impedance $(Z_0 + 3Z_f)$ appear in parallel: $$Z_{parallel} = Z_2 \parallel (Z_0 + 3 Z_f) = \frac{Z_2 (Z_0 + 3 Z_f)}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}$$ The positive-sequence current entering the parallel combination is: $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + Z_{parallel}} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + \frac{Z_2 (Z_0 + 3 Z_f)}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f}}$$

Using the current divider rule, the negative-sequence and zero-sequence currents are: $$I_{a2} = -I_{a1} \left( \frac{Z_0 + 3 Z_f}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f} \right)$$ $$I_{a0} = -I_{a1} \left( \frac{Z_2}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f} \right)$$

Total Fault Current Entering Ground: The total current flowing through the earth return path is: $$I_f = I_b + I_c = 3 I_{a0} = -3 I_{a1} \left( \frac{Z_2}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f} \right)$$ For a bolted LLG fault ($Z_f = 0$): $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + \frac{Z_2 Z_0}{Z_2 + Z_0}}$$ $$I_f = 3 I_{a0} = -3 I_{a1} \left(\frac{Z_2}{Z_2 + Z_0}\right)$$

Sequence Network Interconnection: The positive-sequence network, negative-sequence network, and zero-sequence network (with an additional branch $3Z_f$ in series with $Z_0$) are connected in Parallel across their respective sequence terminals.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

A Double Line-to-Ground (LLG) fault occurs when two phase conductors simultaneously contact each other and fall to ground, or when two phases contact a grounded metallic structure. LLG faults constitute roughly 10% of all power system faults.

Step 2: Symmetrical Component Voltage Relationships: Using the inverse transformation $V_{012} = A^{-1} V_{abc}$ for $V_b = V_c$: $$\begin{bmatrix} V_{a0} \\ V_{a1} \\ V_{a2} \end{bmatrix} = \frac{1}{3} \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & a & a^2 \\ 1 & a^2 & a \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} V_a \\ V_b \\ V_b \end{bmatrix}$$ Evaluating sequence voltages: $$V_{a0} = \frac{1}{3}(V_a + 2 V_b)$$ $$V_{a1} = \frac{1}{3}(V_a + (a + a^2) V_b) = \frac{1}{3}(V_a - V_b)$$ $$V_{a2} = \frac{1}{3}(V_a + (a^2 + a) V_b) = \frac{1}{3}(V_a - V_b)$$ Notice that: $$V_{a1} = V_{a2}$$ For a bolted fault ($V_b = 0$): $$V_{a0} = V_{a1} = V_{a2} = \frac{1}{3} V_a$$ When fault impedance $Z_f$ is present, the total ground return current is: $$I_n = I_b + I_c = (I_a + I_b + I_c) = 3 I_{a0}$$ The voltage of the faulted phases with respect to true ground is: $$V_b = V_c = 3 I_{a0} Z_f$$ Substituting this back yields the sequence voltage relation: $$V_{a1} = V_{a2} = V_{a0} - 3 I_{a0} Z_f$$ This condition proves that the sequence voltages are identical across all three networks, mandating a Parallel Interconnection of positive, negative, and zero sequence networks!

Step 3: Current Boundary Condition in Sequence Domain: Since $I_a = 0$: $$I_a = I_{a0} + I_{a1} + I_{a2} = 0 \implies I_{a1} = -(I_{a2} + I_{a0})$$ This exactly matches Kirchhoff's Current Law at the parallel junction of the three networks.

Using the current divider rule, the negative-sequence and zero-sequence currents are: $$I_{a2} = -I_{a1} \left( \frac{Z_0 + 3 Z_f}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f} \right)$$ $$I_{a0} = -I_{a1} \left( \frac{Z_2}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f} \right)$$

Total Fault Current Entering Ground: The total current flowing through the earth return path is: $$I_f = I_b + I_c = 3 I_{a0} = -3 I_{a1} \left( \frac{Z_2}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f} \right)$$ For a bolted LLG fault ($Z_f = 0$): $$I_{a1} = \frac{E_a}{Z_1 + \frac{Z_2 Z_0}{Z_2 + Z_0}}$$ $$I_f = 3 I_{a0} = -3 I_{a1} \left(\frac{Z_2}{Z_2 + Z_0}\right)$$

Sequence Network Interconnection: The positive-sequence network, negative-sequence network, and zero-sequence network (with an additional branch $3Z_f$ in series with $Z_0$) are connected in Parallel across their respective sequence terminals.

Using the current divider rule, the negative-sequence and zero-sequence currents are: $$I_{a2} = -I_{a1} \left( \frac{Z_0 + 3 Z_f}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f} \right)$$ $$I_{a0} = -I_{a1} \left( \frac{Z_2}{Z_2 + Z_0 + 3 Z_f} \right)$$

Power Flow Analysis

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: PS2-Q11

Classify the buses in a power system for load flow studies. State the known and unknown variables for each bus type and explain the formulation of Bus Admittance Matrix (Y_bus) by inspection. Classify the buses in a power system for load flow studies. State the known and unknown variables for each bus type and explain the formulation of Bus Admittance Matrix (Y_bus) by inspection. Classify the buses in a power system for load flow studies. State the known and unknown variables for each bus type and explain the formulation of Bus Admittance Matrix (Y_bus) by inspection.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$Y_{ii} = \sum_{k\ne i} y_{ik} + y_{i0}, \quad Y_{ik} = -y_{ik}; \quad \text{Slack: } |V|, \delta; \quad \text{PV: } P, |V|; \quad \text{PQ: } P, Q$$

Slack bus fixes $|V|, \delta=0$ to balance losses; PV bus regulates voltage with reactive limits ($Q_{min} \le Q \le Q_{max}$); PQ bus demands real and reactive power. $Y_{bus}$ is symmetric and sparse.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$Y_{ii} = \sum_{k\ne i} y_{ik} + y_{i0}, \quad Y_{ik} = -y_{ik}; \quad \text{Slack: } |V|, \delta; \quad \text{PV: } P, |V|; \quad \text{PQ: } P, Q$$

Slack bus fixes $|V|, \delta=0$ to balance losses; PV bus regulates voltage with reactive limits ($Q_{min} \le Q \le Q_{max}$); PQ bus demands real and reactive power. $Y_{bus}$ is symmetric and sparse.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

In power system engineering, power flow (load flow) analysis computes the steady-state operating state of a transmission network under specified generation and loading schedules. At each bus $i$ in an $n$-bus system, four fundamental electrical variables are defined: 1. Real power injection: $P_i = P_{Gi} - P_{Di}$ 2. Reactive power injection: $Q_i = Q_{Gi} - Q_{Di}$ 3. Voltage magnitude: $|V_i|$ 4. Voltage phase angle: $\delta_i$

Because only two independent algebraic equations (real and reactive power balance) can be formulated at each node, exactly two of these four variables must be specified a priori, leaving the remaining two to be calculated. Based on this specification, buses are categorized into three distinct classes:

1. Slack / Swing / Reference Bus (Usually Bus 1): - Specified (Known) Variables: Voltage magnitude $|V_1|$ (typically set to $1.0 \text{ pu}$) and voltage phase angle $\delta_1 = 0^\circ$ (acting as the system angular reference). - Unknown (Calculated) Variables: Active power $P_1$ and reactive power $Q_1$. - Physical Role: Because total transmission grid losses ($P_{loss}$) cannot be known until all bus voltages and line currents are computed, the slack bus generator supplies or absorbs the residual active and reactive power necessary to maintain overall power balance ($\sum P_G = \sum P_D + P_{loss}$).

2. Generator / Voltage-Controlled / PV Bus: - Specified (Known) Variables: Real power generation $P_i$ and terminal voltage magnitude $|V_i|$ (held constant by the automatic voltage regulator excitation system). - Unknown (Calculated) Variables: Reactive power generation $Q_i$ and voltage phase angle $\delta_i$. - Reactive Limits: The excitation system has physical thermal boundaries: $Q_{i,min} \le Q_i \le Q_{i,max}$. If the calculated $Q_i$ violates these limits during iteration, the generator can no longer regulate voltage; $|V_i|$ is released, and the bus is converted into a PQ bus.

3. Load / PQ Bus: - Specified (Known) Variables: Net real power $P_i$ and reactive power $Q_i$ demanded by the consumer load. - Unknown (Calculated) Variables: Voltage magnitude $|V_i|$ and phase angle $\delta_i$.

Formulation of the Bus Admittance Matrix ($Y_{bus}$): The relationship between injected nodal currents and bus voltages is governed by: $$I_{bus} = Y_{bus} V_{bus}$$ In an $n$-bus network, $Y_{bus}$ is an $n \times n$ complex symmetric matrix synthesized by the direct Inspection Method: 1. Diagonal Elements (Self Admittances $Y_{ii}$): The diagonal element $Y_{ii}$ is equal to the algebraic sum of all admittances connected directly to bus $i$, including line charging susceptances and shunt capacitor/reactor elements connected from bus $i$ to ground: $$Y_{ii} = \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n y_{ik} + y_{i0}$$ where $y_{ik} = \frac{1}{R_{ik} + j X_{ik}}$ is the series admittance of the branch between buses $i$ and $k$, and $y_{i0} = j \frac{B_{ik}'}{2} + y_{shunt, i}$ is the total shunt admittance at bus $i$. 2. Off-Diagonal Elements (Mutual Admittances $Y_{ik}$): The off-diagonal element $Y_{ik}$ equals the negative of the total series branch admittance connected directly between bus $i$ and bus $k$: $$Y_{ik} = Y_{ki} = -y_{ik} = -\frac{1}{Z_{ik}}$$ If no direct transmission line connects bus $i$ and bus $k$, $Y_{ik} = 0$.

Because typical power grids are sparsely connected (a substation connects to only 2 to 4 adjacent stations), $Y_{bus}$ is extremely sparse with over 90% zero entries, enabling highly efficient sparse-matrix storage and computation.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide
