3. Load / PQ Bus: - Specified (Known) Variables: Net real power $P_i$ and reactive power $Q_i$ demanded by the consumer load. - Unknown (Calculated) Variables: Voltage magnitude $|V_i|$ and phase angle $\delta_i$.

Formulation of the Bus Admittance Matrix ($Y_{bus}$): The relationship between injected nodal currents and bus voltages is governed by: $$I_{bus} = Y_{bus} V_{bus}$$ In an $n$-bus network, $Y_{bus}$ is an $n \times n$ complex symmetric matrix synthesized by the direct Inspection Method: 1. Diagonal Elements (Self Admittances $Y_{ii}$): The diagonal element $Y_{ii}$ is equal to the algebraic sum of all admittances connected directly to bus $i$, including line charging susceptances and shunt capacitor/reactor elements connected from bus $i$ to ground: $$Y_{ii} = \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n y_{ik} + y_{i0}$$ where $y_{ik} = \frac{1}{R_{ik} + j X_{ik}}$ is the series admittance of the branch between buses $i$ and $k$, and $y_{i0} = j \frac{B_{ik}'}{2} + y_{shunt, i}$ is the total shunt admittance at bus $i$. 2. Off-Diagonal Elements (Mutual Admittances $Y_{ik}$): The off-diagonal element $Y_{ik}$ equals the negative of the total series branch admittance connected directly between bus $i$ and bus $k$: $$Y_{ik} = Y_{ki} = -y_{ik} = -\frac{1}{Z_{ik}}$$ If no direct transmission line connects bus $i$ and bus $k$, $Y_{ik} = 0$.

Because typical power grids are sparsely connected (a substation connects to only 2 to 4 adjacent stations), $Y_{bus}$ is extremely sparse with over 90% zero entries, enabling highly efficient sparse-matrix storage and computation.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

In power system engineering, power flow (load flow) analysis computes the steady-state operating state of a transmission network under specified generation and loading schedules. At each bus $i$ in an $n$-bus system, four fundamental electrical variables are defined: 1. Real power injection: $P_i = P_{Gi} - P_{Di}$ 2. Reactive power injection: $Q_i = Q_{Gi} - Q_{Di}$ 3. Voltage magnitude: $|V_i|$ 4. Voltage phase angle: $\delta_i$

Because only two independent algebraic equations (real and reactive power balance) can be formulated at each node, exactly two of these four variables must be specified a priori, leaving the remaining two to be calculated. Based on this specification, buses are categorized into three distinct classes:

1. Slack / Swing / Reference Bus (Usually Bus 1): - Specified (Known) Variables: Voltage magnitude $|V_1|$ (typically set to $1.0 \text{ pu}$) and voltage phase angle $\delta_1 = 0^\circ$ (acting as the system angular reference). - Unknown (Calculated) Variables: Active power $P_1$ and reactive power $Q_1$. - Physical Role: Because total transmission grid losses ($P_{loss}$) cannot be known until all bus voltages and line currents are computed, the slack bus generator supplies or absorbs the residual active and reactive power necessary to maintain overall power balance ($\sum P_G = \sum P_D + P_{loss}$).

2. Generator / Voltage-Controlled / PV Bus: - Specified (Known) Variables: Real power generation $P_i$ and terminal voltage magnitude $|V_i|$ (held constant by the automatic voltage regulator excitation system). - Unknown (Calculated) Variables: Reactive power generation $Q_i$ and voltage phase angle $\delta_i$. - Reactive Limits: The excitation system has physical thermal boundaries: $Q_{i,min} \le Q_i \le Q_{i,max}$. If the calculated $Q_i$ violates these limits during iteration, the generator can no longer regulate voltage; $|V_i|$ is released, and the bus is converted into a PQ bus.

3. Load / PQ Bus: - Specified (Known) Variables: Net real power $P_i$ and reactive power $Q_i$ demanded by the consumer load. - Unknown (Calculated) Variables: Voltage magnitude $|V_i|$ and phase angle $\delta_i$.

Formulation of the Bus Admittance Matrix ($Y_{bus}$): The relationship between injected nodal currents and bus voltages is governed by: $$I_{bus} = Y_{bus} V_{bus}$$ In an $n$-bus network, $Y_{bus}$ is an $n \times n$ complex symmetric matrix synthesized by the direct Inspection Method: 1. Diagonal Elements (Self Admittances $Y_{ii}$): The diagonal element $Y_{ii}$ is equal to the algebraic sum of all admittances connected directly to bus $i$, including line charging susceptances and shunt capacitor/reactor elements connected from bus $i$ to ground: $$Y_{ii} = \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n y_{ik} + y_{i0}$$ where $y_{ik} = \frac{1}{R_{ik} + j X_{ik}}$ is the series admittance of the branch between buses $i$ and $k$, and $y_{i0} = j \frac{B_{ik}'}{2} + y_{shunt, i}$ is the total shunt admittance at bus $i$. 2. Off-Diagonal Elements (Mutual Admittances $Y_{ik}$): The off-diagonal element $Y_{ik}$ equals the negative of the total series branch admittance connected directly between bus $i$ and bus $k$: $$Y_{ik} = Y_{ki} = -y_{ik} = -\frac{1}{Z_{ik}}$$ If no direct transmission line connects bus $i$ and bus $k$, $Y_{ik} = 0$.

Because typical power grids are sparsely connected (a substation connects to only 2 to 4 adjacent stations), $Y_{bus}$ is extremely sparse with over 90% zero entries, enabling highly efficient sparse-matrix storage and computation.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

To establish the fundamental mathematical framework for numerical load flow algorithms, we derive the non-linear algebraic static power flow equations governing active and reactive power balance at each bus.

Consider an $n$-bus electrical power transmission system. Let: - $V_i = |V_i| \angle \delta_i = |V_i| e^{j \delta_i}$ represent the complex phasor voltage at bus $i$. - $I_i$ represent the net complex current injected into the network at bus $i$. - $Y_{ik} = |Y_{ik}| \angle \theta_{ik} = |Y_{ik}| e^{j \theta_{ik}} = G_{ik} + j B_{ik}$ represent the $(i, k)$-th entry of the bus admittance matrix $Y_{bus}$, where $G_{ik}$ is conductance and $B_{ik}$ is susceptance.

Applying Kirchhoff's Current Law (KCL) at bus $i$, the total injected current equals the sum of currents leaving bus $i$ to all other connected buses $k = 1, 2, \dots, n$: $$I_i = \sum_{k=1}^n Y_{ik} V_k$$

The net complex power $S_i$ injected into bus $i$ is defined by the phasor relation: $$S_i = P_i + j Q_i = V_i I_i^*$$ where $P_i$ is net real power ($P_{Gi} - P_{Di}$), $Q_i$ is net reactive power ($Q_{Gi} - Q_{Di}$), and $I_i^*$ is the complex conjugate of the injected current phasor. Taking the complex conjugate on both sides of the power equation: $$S_i^* = P_i - j Q_i = V_i^* I_i$$ Substituting the KCL current summation into this expression: $$P_i - j Q_i = V_i^* \sum_{k=1}^n Y_{ik} V_k = \sum_{k=1}^n V_i^* Y_{ik} V_k$$

Substituting the polar forms $V_i^* = |V_i| e^{-j \delta_i}$, $Y_{ik} = |Y_{ik}| e^{j \theta_{ik}}$, and $V_k = |V_k| e^{j \delta_k}$: $$P_i - j Q_i = \sum_{k=1}^n \left( |V_i| e^{-j \delta_i} \right) \left( |Y_{ik}| e^{j \theta_{ik}} \right) \left( |V_k| e^{j \delta_k} \right)$$ Combining the exponential terms: $$P_i - j Q_i = \sum_{k=1}^n |V_i| |V_k| |Y_{ik}| e^{j (\theta_{ik} + \delta_k - \delta_i)}$$

Applying Euler's identity $e^{j\phi} = \cos\phi + j\sin\phi$: $$P_i - j Q_i = \sum_{k=1}^n |V_i| |V_k| |Y_{ik}| \left[ \cos(\theta_{ik} + \delta_k - \delta_i) + j \sin(\theta_{ik} + \delta_k - \delta_i) \right]$$

Equating real parts on both sides yields the Static Real Power Flow Equation: $$P_i = \sum_{k=1}^n |V_i| |V_k| |Y_{ik}| \cos(\theta_{ik} + \delta_k - \delta_i)$$

Equating imaginary parts on both sides (noting the negative sign in $-j Q_i$): $$-Q_i = \sum_{k=1}^n |V_i| |V_k| |Y_{ik}| \sin(\theta_{ik} + \delta_k - \delta_i)$$ Multiplying by $-1$ yields the Static Reactive Power Flow Equation: $$Q_i = -\sum_{k=1}^n |V_i| |V_k| |Y_{ik}| \sin(\theta_{ik} + \delta_k - \delta_i)$$

Alternative Rectangular Formulation using Conductance ($G_{ik}$) and Susceptance ($B_{ik}$): Using the trigonometric angle addition formula on $\theta_{ik} - (\delta_i - \delta_k)$: $$\cos(\theta_{ik} - \delta_i + \delta_k) = \cos\theta_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) + \sin\theta_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k)$$ Since $G_{ik} = |Y_{ik}|\cos\theta_{ik}$ and $B_{ik} = |Y_{ik}|\sin\theta_{ik}$: $$P_i = |V_i| \sum_{k=1}^n |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) + B_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right]$$ $$Q_i = |V_i| \sum_{k=1}^n |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) - B_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right]$$

Power Mismatches: During iterative numerical solution, the calculated injections $P_i^{calc}$ and $Q_i^{calc}$ evaluated at iteration $(m)$ are compared against specified scheduled values to form power mismatches: $$\Delta P_i^{(m)} = P_i^{sch} - P_i^{calc,(m)}$$ $$\Delta Q_i^{(m)} = Q_i^{sch} - Q_i^{calc,(m)}$$ Convergence is achieved when all mismatches satisfy $|\Delta P_i| \le \epsilon$ and $|\Delta Q_i| \le \epsilon$ across all non-slack buses.

To establish the fundamental mathematical framework for numerical load flow algorithms, we derive the non-linear algebraic static power flow equations governing active and reactive power balance at each bus.

Consider an $n$-bus electrical power transmission system. Let: - $V_i = |V_i| \angle \delta_i = |V_i| e^{j \delta_i}$ represent the complex phasor voltage at bus $i$. - $I_i$ represent the net complex current injected into the network at bus $i$. - $Y_{ik} = |Y_{ik}| \angle \theta_{ik} = |Y_{ik}| e^{j \theta_{ik}} = G_{ik} + j B_{ik}$ represent the $(i, k)$-th entry of the bus admittance matrix $Y_{bus}$, where $G_{ik}$ is conductance and $B_{ik}$ is susceptance.

Applying Kirchhoff's Current Law (KCL) at bus $i$, the total injected current equals the sum of currents leaving bus $i$ to all other connected buses $k = 1, 2, \dots, n$: $$I_i = \sum_{k=1}^n Y_{ik} V_k$$

The net complex power $S_i$ injected into bus $i$ is defined by the phasor relation: $$S_i = P_i + j Q_i = V_i I_i^*$$ where $P_i$ is net real power ($P_{Gi} - P_{Di}$), $Q_i$ is net reactive power ($Q_{Gi} - Q_{Di}$), and $I_i^*$ is the complex conjugate of the injected current phasor. Taking the complex conjugate on both sides of the power equation: $$S_i^* = P_i - j Q_i = V_i^* I_i$$ Substituting the KCL current summation into this expression: $$P_i - j Q_i = V_i^* \sum_{k=1}^n Y_{ik} V_k = \sum_{k=1}^n V_i^* Y_{ik} V_k$$

Substituting the polar forms $V_i^* = |V_i| e^{-j \delta_i}$, $Y_{ik} = |Y_{ik}| e^{j \theta_{ik}}$, and $V_k = |V_k| e^{j \delta_k}$: $$P_i - j Q_i = \sum_{k=1}^n \left( |V_i| e^{-j \delta_i} \right) \left( |Y_{ik}| e^{j \theta_{ik}} \right) \left( |V_k| e^{j \delta_k} \right)$$ Combining the exponential terms: $$P_i - j Q_i = \sum_{k=1}^n |V_i| |V_k| |Y_{ik}| e^{j (\theta_{ik} + \delta_k - \delta_i)}$$

Applying Euler's identity $e^{j\phi} = \cos\phi + j\sin\phi$: $$P_i - j Q_i = \sum_{k=1}^n |V_i| |V_k| |Y_{ik}| \left[ \cos(\theta_{ik} + \delta_k - \delta_i) + j \sin(\theta_{ik} + \delta_k - \delta_i) \right]$$

Equating real parts on both sides yields the Static Real Power Flow Equation: $$P_i = \sum_{k=1}^n |V_i| |V_k| |Y_{ik}| \cos(\theta_{ik} + \delta_k - \delta_i)$$

Equating imaginary parts on both sides (noting the negative sign in $-j Q_i$): $$-Q_i = \sum_{k=1}^n |V_i| |V_k| |Y_{ik}| \sin(\theta_{ik} + \delta_k - \delta_i)$$ Multiplying by $-1$ yields the Static Reactive Power Flow Equation: $$Q_i = -\sum_{k=1}^n |V_i| |V_k| |Y_{ik}| \sin(\theta_{ik} + \delta_k - \delta_i)$$

Alternative Rectangular Formulation using Conductance ($G_{ik}$) and Susceptance ($B_{ik}$): Using the trigonometric angle addition formula on $\theta_{ik} - (\delta_i - \delta_k)$: $$\cos(\theta_{ik} - \delta_i + \delta_k) = \cos\theta_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) + \sin\theta_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k)$$ Since $G_{ik} = |Y_{ik}|\cos\theta_{ik}$ and $B_{ik} = |Y_{ik}|\sin\theta_{ik}$: $$P_i = |V_i| \sum_{k=1}^n |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) + B_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right]$$ $$Q_i = |V_i| \sum_{k=1}^n |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) - B_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right]$$

Power Mismatches: During iterative numerical solution, the calculated injections $P_i^{calc}$ and $Q_i^{calc}$ evaluated at iteration $(m)$ are compared against specified scheduled values to form power mismatches: $$\Delta P_i^{(m)} = P_i^{sch} - P_i^{calc,(m)}$$ $$\Delta Q_i^{(m)} = Q_i^{sch} - Q_i^{calc,(m)}$$ Convergence is achieved when all mismatches satisfy $|\Delta P_i| \le \epsilon$ and $|\Delta Q_i| \le \epsilon$ across all non-slack buses.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Explain the Gauss-Seidel method for power flow solution. ⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V_i^{(m+1)} = \frac{1}{Y_{ii}} \left[ \frac{P_i - j Q_i}{(V_i^{(m)})^*} - \sum_{k=1}^{i-1} Y_{ik} V_k^{(m+1)} - \sum_{k=i+1}^n Y_{ik} V_k^{(m)} \right], \quad V_i^{acc} = V_i^{(m)} + \alpha(V_i^{(m+1)} - V_i^{(m)})$$

Gauss-Seidel substitutes updated voltages immediately, accelerated by factor $\alpha \approx 1.4 \text{ to } 1.6$. PV buses estimate $Q_i$; if $Q_i$ violates limits, bus converts to PQ type.

$$V_i^{(m+1)} = \frac{1}{Y_{ii}} \left[ \frac{P_i - j Q_i}{(V_i^{(m)})^*} - \sum_{k=1}^{i-1} Y_{ik} V_k^{(m+1)} - \sum_{k=i+1}^n Y_{ik} V_k^{(m)} \right], \quad V_i^{acc} = V_i^{(m)} + \alpha(V_i^{(m+1)} - V_i^{(m)})$$

Gauss-Seidel substitutes updated voltages immediately, accelerated by factor $\alpha \approx 1.4 \text{ to } 1.6$. PV buses estimate $Q_i$; if $Q_i$ violates limits, bus converts to PQ type.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Gauss-Seidel method is an iterative numerical technique for solving large sets of non-linear algebraic equations. In power flow studies, its primary advantages are computational simplicity, low computer memory requirements, and straightforward programming.

Derivation of the Iterative Voltage Equation: From the complex power injection equation at bus $i$: $$S_i^* = P_i - j Q_i = V_i^* I_i \implies I_i = \frac{P_i - j Q_i}{V_i^*}$$ Expressing injected current using the bus admittance matrix: $$I_i = \sum_{k=1}^n Y_{ik} V_k = Y_{ii} V_i + \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n Y_{ik} V_k$$ Equating both expressions for $I_i$: $$Y_{ii} V_i + \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n Y_{ik} V_k = \frac{P_i - j Q_i}{V_i^*}$$ Isolating the bus voltage $V_i$: $$Y_{ii} V_i = \frac{P_i - j Q_i}{V_i^*} - \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n Y_{ik} V_k$$ $$V_i = \frac{1}{Y_{ii}} \left[ \frac{P_i - j Q_i}{V_i^*} - \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n Y_{ik} V_k \right]$$

In the Gauss-Seidel scheme, newly computed voltage values are immediately substituted as soon as they become available within the same iteration cycle. When updating bus $i$ during the $(m+1)$-th iteration, the voltages for buses $k = 1$ to $(i - 1)$ have already been updated to iteration $(m+1)$, whereas voltages for buses $k = (i + 1)$ to $n$ remain at iteration $(m)$: $$V_i^{(m+1)} = \frac{1}{Y_{ii}} \left[ \frac{P_i^{sch} - j Q_i^{sch}}{\left(V_i^{(m)}\right)^*} - \sum_{k=1}^{i-1} Y_{ik} V_k^{(m+1)} - \sum_{k=i+1}^n Y_{ik} V_k^{(m)} \right]$$

Acceleration Factor ($\alpha$): Because the Gauss-Seidel algorithm exhibits slow, linear convergence, an acceleration factor $\alpha$ ($1.4 \le \alpha \le 1.6$) is universally applied to speed up convergence by magnifying the incremental correction: $$V_i^{(m+1), acc} = V_i^{(m)} + \alpha \left[ V_i^{(m+1)} - V_i^{(m)} \right]$$ Applying acceleration to both real and imaginary components reduces the total number of iterations by up to 50%.

Handling of Generator (PV) Buses: At a PV bus $i$, active power $P_i^{sch}$ and voltage magnitude $|V_i|^{spec}$ are specified, while reactive power $Q_i$ and phase angle $\delta_i$ are unknown. Because $Q_i$ is not specified, it must be estimated at every iteration: 1. Estimate Reactive Power $Q_i^{(m+1)}$: Using the current estimates of bus voltages: $$Q_i^{(m+1)} = -\text{Im} \left\{ \left(V_i^{(m)}\right)^* \left[ \sum_{k=1}^{i-1} Y_{ik} V_k^{(m+1)} + \sum_{k=i}^n Y_{ik} V_k^{(m)} \right] \right\}$$ 2. Check Reactive Power Capability Limits: Compare the calculated $Q_i^{(m+1)}$ against the generator physical excitation limits: $$Q_{i,min} \le Q_i^{(m+1)} \le Q_{i,max}$$ - If $Q_i^{(m+1)}$ falls strictly within limits, the bus remains a PV bus. Compute temporary voltage $V_{i,temp}^{(m+1)}$ using the standard Gauss-Seidel formula with $Q_i = Q_i^{(m+1)}$. Because the magnitude $|V_i|^{spec}$ must remain fixed, preserve the newly computed phase angle $\delta_i^{(m+1)}$ while resetting the magnitude: $$V_i^{(m+1)} = |V_i|^{spec} \angle \delta_{i,temp}^{(m+1)} = |V_i|^{spec} \frac{V_{i,temp}^{(m+1)}}{|V_{i,temp}^{(m+1)}|}$$ - If $Q_i^{(m+1)} > Q_{i,max}$: Clamp $Q_i = Q_{i,max}$. The generator has reached its overexcitation thermal limit. Release voltage magnitude $|V_i|^{spec}$ and treat the bus as a PQ bus for all subsequent iterations. - If $Q_i^{(m+1)} < Q_{i,min}$: Clamp $Q_i = Q_{i,min}$. The generator has reached its stability underexcitation limit. Treat the bus as a PQ bus.

Algorithm Termination: Iterations terminate when the maximum voltage change between successive iterations falls below a specified convergence tolerance $\epsilon$: $$\max_{i} \left| V_i^{(m+1)} - V_i^{(m)} \right| \le \epsilon \approx 10^{-4} \text{ pu}$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

The Gauss-Seidel method is an iterative numerical technique for solving large sets of non-linear algebraic equations. In power flow studies, its primary advantages are computational simplicity, low computer memory requirements, and straightforward programming.

Derivation of the Iterative Voltage Equation: From the complex power injection equation at bus $i$: $$S_i^* = P_i - j Q_i = V_i^* I_i \implies I_i = \frac{P_i - j Q_i}{V_i^*}$$ Expressing injected current using the bus admittance matrix: $$I_i = \sum_{k=1}^n Y_{ik} V_k = Y_{ii} V_i + \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n Y_{ik} V_k$$ Equating both expressions for $I_i$: $$Y_{ii} V_i + \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n Y_{ik} V_k = \frac{P_i - j Q_i}{V_i^*}$$ Isolating the bus voltage $V_i$: $$Y_{ii} V_i = \frac{P_i - j Q_i}{V_i^*} - \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n Y_{ik} V_k$$ $$V_i = \frac{1}{Y_{ii}} \left[ \frac{P_i - j Q_i}{V_i^*} - \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n Y_{ik} V_k \right]$$

In the Gauss-Seidel scheme, newly computed voltage values are immediately substituted as soon as they become available within the same iteration cycle. When updating bus $i$ during the $(m+1)$-th iteration, the voltages for buses $k = 1$ to $(i - 1)$ have already been updated to iteration $(m+1)$, whereas voltages for buses $k = (i + 1)$ to $n$ remain at iteration $(m)$: $$V_i^{(m+1)} = \frac{1}{Y_{ii}} \left[ \frac{P_i^{sch} - j Q_i^{sch}}{\left(V_i^{(m)}\right)^*} - \sum_{k=1}^{i-1} Y_{ik} V_k^{(m+1)} - \sum_{k=i+1}^n Y_{ik} V_k^{(m)} \right]$$

Acceleration Factor ($\alpha$): Because the Gauss-Seidel algorithm exhibits slow, linear convergence, an acceleration factor $\alpha$ ($1.4 \le \alpha \le 1.6$) is universally applied to speed up convergence by magnifying the incremental correction: $$V_i^{(m+1), acc} = V_i^{(m)} + \alpha \left[ V_i^{(m+1)} - V_i^{(m)} \right]$$ Applying acceleration to both real and imaginary components reduces the total number of iterations by up to 50%.

Handling of Generator (PV) Buses: At a PV bus $i$, active power $P_i^{sch}$ and voltage magnitude $|V_i|^{spec}$ are specified, while reactive power $Q_i$ and phase angle $\delta_i$ are unknown. Because $Q_i$ is not specified, it must be estimated at every iteration: 1. Estimate Reactive Power $Q_i^{(m+1)}$: Using the current estimates of bus voltages: $$Q_i^{(m+1)} = -\text{Im} \left\{ \left(V_i^{(m)}\right)^* \left[ \sum_{k=1}^{i-1} Y_{ik} V_k^{(m+1)} + \sum_{k=i}^n Y_{ik} V_k^{(m)} \right] \right\}$$ 2. Check Reactive Power Capability Limits: Compare the calculated $Q_i^{(m+1)}$ against the generator physical excitation limits: $$Q_{i,min} \le Q_i^{(m+1)} \le Q_{i,max}$$ - If $Q_i^{(m+1)}$ falls strictly within limits, the bus remains a PV bus. Compute temporary voltage $V_{i,temp}^{(m+1)}$ using the standard Gauss-Seidel formula with $Q_i = Q_i^{(m+1)}$. Because the magnitude $|V_i|^{spec}$ must remain fixed, preserve the newly computed phase angle $\delta_i^{(m+1)}$ while resetting the magnitude: $$V_i^{(m+1)} = |V_i|^{spec} \angle \delta_{i,temp}^{(m+1)} = |V_i|^{spec} \frac{V_{i,temp}^{(m+1)}}{|V_{i,temp}^{(m+1)}|}$$ - If $Q_i^{(m+1)} > Q_{i,max}$: Clamp $Q_i = Q_{i,max}$. The generator has reached its overexcitation thermal limit. Release voltage magnitude $|V_i|^{spec}$ and treat the bus as a PQ bus for all subsequent iterations. - If $Q_i^{(m+1)} < Q_{i,min}$: Clamp $Q_i = Q_{i,min}$. The generator has reached its stability underexcitation limit. Treat the bus as a PQ bus.

Algorithm Termination: Iterations terminate when the maximum voltage change between successive iterations falls below a specified convergence tolerance $\epsilon$: $$\max_{i} \left| V_i^{(m+1)} - V_i^{(m)} \right| \le \epsilon \approx 10^{-4} \text{ pu}$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

$$\begin{bmatrix} \Delta P \\ \Delta Q \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} H & N \\ M & L \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \Delta\delta \\ \Delta|V|/|V| \end{bmatrix}, \quad H_{ii} = -Q_i - |V_i|^2 B_{ii}, \quad L_{ii} = Q_i - |V_i|^2 B_{ii}$$

Newton-Raphson linearizes power flow using Jacobian matrix with quadratic convergence (3-5 iterations). Off-diagonals satisfy $M_{ik} = -N_{ik}$ and $L_{ik} = H_{ik}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Newton-Raphson (N-R) method is the industrial standard algorithm for power flow analysis in modern transmission grids. Based on multi-variable Taylor series expansion, it possesses quadratic convergence characteristics, typically achieving convergence to machine precision in only 3 to 5 iterations regardless of network size.

Mathematical Formulation: Let an $n$-bus system contain 1 Slack bus (Bus 1), $m$ PV buses, and $(n - 1 - m)$ PQ buses. The unknown state variables are: - Voltage phase angles $\delta_i$ for all $n - 1$ non-slack buses ($i = 2, 3, \dots, n$). - Voltage magnitudes $|V_i|$ for all $(n - 1 - m)$ PQ buses.

Expanding the non-linear real and reactive power equations in Taylor series around operating point $(\delta^{(0)}, |V|^{(0)})$ and truncating higher-order terms: $$\begin{bmatrix} \Delta P \\ \Delta Q \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} J_1 & J_2 \\ J_3 & J_4 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \Delta \delta \\ \Delta |V| \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} H & N \\ M & L \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \Delta \delta \\ \Delta |V| / |V| \end{bmatrix}$$ where $\Delta P_i = P_i^{sch} - P_i^{calc}$ and $\Delta Q_i = Q_i^{sch} - Q_i^{calc}$ are the mismatch vectors, and $J$ is the Jacobian Matrix.

To achieve mathematical elegance and computational symmetry, the voltage correction terms are normalized by dividing by $|V|$, scaling the Jacobian sub-matrices: $$H_{ik} = \frac{\partial P_i}{\partial \delta_k}, \quad N_{ik} = |V_k| \frac{\partial P_i}{\partial |V_k|}, \quad M_{ik} = \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial \delta_k}, \quad L_{ik} = |V_k| \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial |V_k|}$$

Derivations of Jacobian Sub-Matrix Elements: From the static power equations: $$P_i = |V_i|^2 G_{ii} + \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) + B_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right]$$ $$Q_i = -|V_i|^2 B_{ii} + \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) - B_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right]$$

1. Sub-matrix $H$ ($\frac{\partial P}{\partial \delta}$): - Off-Diagonal Elements ($k \ne i$): $$H_{ik} = \frac{\partial P_i}{\partial \delta_k} = |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) - B_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right]$$ - Diagonal Elements ($k = i$): $$H_{ii} = \frac{\partial P_i}{\partial \delta_i} = -\sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) - B_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right] = -Q_i - |V_i|^2 B_{ii}$$

2. Sub-matrix $N$ ($|V| \frac{\partial P}{\partial |V|}$): - Off-Diagonal Elements ($k \ne i$): $$N_{ik} = |V_k| \frac{\partial P_i}{\partial |V_k|} = |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) + B_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right]$$ - Diagonal Elements ($k = i$): $$N_{ii} = |V_i| \frac{\partial P_i}{\partial |V_i|} = P_i + |V_i|^2 G_{ii}$$

3. Sub-matrix $M$ ($\frac{\partial Q}{\partial \delta}$): - Off-Diagonal Elements ($k \ne i$): $$M_{ik} = \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial \delta_k} = -|V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) + B_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right] = -N_{ik}$$ - Diagonal Elements ($k = i$): $$M_{ii} = \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial \delta_i} = \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) + B_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right] = P_i - |V_i|^2 G_{ii}$$

4. Sub-matrix $L$ ($|V| \frac{\partial Q}{\partial |V|}$): - Off-Diagonal Elements ($k \ne i$): $$L_{ik} = |V_k| \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial |V_k|} = |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) - B_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right] = H_{ik}$$ - Diagonal Elements ($k = i$): $$L_{ii} = |V_i| \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial |V_i|} = Q_i - |V_i|^2 B_{ii}$$

Computational Algorithm: 1. Initialize flat start: $\delta_i^{(0)} = 0^\circ$ and $|V_i|^{(0)} = 1.0 \text{ pu}$ for PQ buses. 2. Evaluate $P_i^{calc}$ and $Q_i^{calc}$ using current states. 3. Compute mismatches $\Delta P_i$ and $\Delta Q_i$. If all mismatches are less than tolerance ($10^{-4}$), stop. 4. Formulate the Jacobian matrix $J$ using derived analytical derivatives. 5. Solve the linear algebraic system $J \begin{bmatrix} \Delta\delta \\ \Delta|V|/|V| \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \Delta P \\ \Delta Q \end{bmatrix}$ via LU decomposition. 6. Update states: $\delta_i^{(m+1)} = \delta_i^{(m)} + \Delta\delta_i$ and $|V_i|^{(m+1)} = |V_i|^{(m)} + \Delta|V_i|$. Repeat until convergence.

Comparison: N-R requires significantly fewer iterations (3-5) compared to Gauss-Seidel (50-200), converges reliably on ill-conditioned systems, but requires evaluating and factorizing the Jacobian matrix at every iteration.

Sub-matrix $M$ ($\frac{\partial Q}{\partial \delta}$): - Off-Diagonal Elements ($k \ne i$): $$M_{ik} = \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial \delta_k} = -|V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) + B_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right] = -N_{ik}$$ - Diagonal Elements ($k = i$): $$M_{ii} = \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial \delta_i} = \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) + B_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right] = P_i - |V_i|^2 G_{ii}$$

4. Sub-matrix $L$ ($|V| \frac{\partial Q}{\partial |V|}$): - Off-Diagonal Elements ($k \ne i$): $$L_{ik} = |V_k| \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial |V_k|} = |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) - B_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right] = H_{ik}$$ - Diagonal Elements ($k = i$): $$L_{ii} = |V_i| \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial |V_i|} = Q_i - |V_i|^2 B_{ii}$$

Update states: $\delta_i^{(m+1)} = \delta_i^{(m)} + \Delta\delta_i$ and $|V_i|^{(m+1)} = |V_i|^{(m)} + \Delta|V_i|$. Repeat until convergence.

To achieve mathematical elegance and computational symmetry, the voltage correction terms are normalized by dividing by $|V|$, scaling the Jacobian sub-matrices: $$H_{ik} = \frac{\partial P_i}{\partial \delta_k}, \quad N_{ik} = |V_k| \frac{\partial P_i}{\partial |V_k|}, \quad M_{ik} = \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial \delta_k}, \quad L_{ik} = |V_k| \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial |V_k|}$$

Derivations of Jacobian Sub-Matrix Elements: From the static power equations: $$P_i = |V_i|^2 G_{ii} + \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) + B_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right]$$ $$Q_i = -|V_i|^2 B_{ii} + \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) - B_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right]$$

1. Sub-matrix $H$ ($\frac{\partial P}{\partial \delta}$): - Off-Diagonal Elements ($k \ne i$): $$H_{ik} = \frac{\partial P_i}{\partial \delta_k} = |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) - B_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right]$$ - Diagonal Elements ($k = i$): $$H_{ii} = \frac{\partial P_i}{\partial \delta_i} = -\sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) - B_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right] = -Q_i - |V_i|^2 B_{ii}$$

2. Sub-matrix $N$ ($|V| \frac{\partial P}{\partial |V|}$): - Off-Diagonal Elements ($k \ne i$): $$N_{ik} = |V_k| \frac{\partial P_i}{\partial |V_k|} = |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) + B_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right]$$ - Diagonal Elements ($k = i$): $$N_{ii} = |V_i| \frac{\partial P_i}{\partial |V_i|} = P_i + |V_i|^2 G_{ii}$$

3. Sub-matrix $M$ ($\frac{\partial Q}{\partial \delta}$): - Off-Diagonal Elements ($k \ne i$): $$M_{ik} = \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial \delta_k} = -|V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) + B_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right] = -N_{ik}$$ - Diagonal Elements ($k = i$): $$M_{ii} = \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial \delta_i} = \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) + B_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right] = P_i - |V_i|^2 G_{ii}$$

4. Sub-matrix $L$ ($|V| \frac{\partial Q}{\partial |V|}$): - Off-Diagonal Elements ($k \ne i$): $$L_{ik} = |V_k| \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial |V_k|} = |V_i| |V_k| \left[ G_{ik} \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) - B_{ik} \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \right] = H_{ik}$$ - Diagonal Elements ($k = i$): $$L_{ii} = |V_i| \frac{\partial Q_i}{\partial |V_i|} = Q_i - |V_i|^2 B_{ii}$$

Computational Algorithm: 1. Initialize flat start: $\delta_i^{(0)} = 0^\circ$ and $|V_i|^{(0)} = 1.0 \text{ pu}$ for PQ buses. 2. Evaluate $P_i^{calc}$ and $Q_i^{calc}$ using current states. 3. Compute mismatches $\Delta P_i$ and $\Delta Q_i$. If all mismatches are less than tolerance ($10^{-4}$), stop. 4. Formulate the Jacobian matrix $J$ using derived analytical derivatives. 5. Solve the linear algebraic system $J \begin{bmatrix} \Delta\delta \\ \Delta|V|/|V| \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \Delta P \\ \Delta Q \end{bmatrix}$ via LU decomposition. 6. Update states: $\delta_i^{(m+1)} = \delta_i^{(m)} + \Delta\delta_i$ and $|V_i|^{(m+1)} = |V_i|^{(m)} + \Delta|V_i|$. Repeat until convergence.

Comparison: N-R requires significantly fewer iterations (3-5) compared to Gauss-Seidel (50-200), converges reliably on ill-conditioned systems, but requires evaluating and factorizing the Jacobian matrix at every iteration.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 3.4: Partitioned Structure of the Newton-Raphson Power Flow Jacobian Matrix — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)
3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: PS2-Q15

State the physical assumptions underlying the Fast Decoupled Load Flow (FDLF) method. Derive the decoupled matrix equations involving B' and B'' and discuss its computational advantages. State the physical assumptions underlying the Fast Decoupled Load Flow (FDLF) method. Derive the decoupled matrix equations involving B' and B'' and discuss its computational advantages. State the physical assumptions underlying the Fast Decoupled Load Flow (FDLF) method. Derive the decoupled matrix equations involving B' and B'' and discuss its computational advantages.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\left[\frac{\Delta P}{|V|}\right] = [B'][\Delta\delta], \quad \left[\frac{\Delta Q}{|V|}\right] = [B''][\Delta|V|], \quad R \ll X, \quad \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \approx 1$$

Fast Decoupled Load Flow exploits $P-\delta$ and $Q-V$ physical decoupling. Matrices $B'$ and $B''$ are constant, real, symmetric, and inverted only once, accelerating computations 5-fold.

⚡ Key Equation

$$\left[\frac{\Delta P}{|V|}\right] = [B'][\Delta\delta], \quad \left[\frac{\Delta Q}{|V|}\right] = [B''][\Delta|V|], \quad R \ll X, \quad \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \approx 1$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\left[\frac{\Delta P}{|V|}\right] = [B'][\Delta\delta], \quad \left[\frac{\Delta Q}{|V|}\right] = [B''][\Delta|V|], \quad R \ll X, \quad \cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \approx 1$$

Fast Decoupled Load Flow exploits $P-\delta$ and $Q-V$ physical decoupling. Matrices $B'$ and $B''$ are constant, real, symmetric, and inverted only once, accelerating computations 5-fold.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Fast Decoupled Load Flow (FDLF) method, developed by Brian Stott and Ongun Alsac in 1974, represents one of the most brilliant practical simplifications of the Newton-Raphson algorithm. It exploits the inherent physical properties of high-voltage electrical transmission systems to decouple active and reactive power computations into two independent, fast matrix equations.

Physical Decoupling Assumptions: In high-voltage transmission networks: 1. $P - \delta$ and $Q - |V|$ Decoupling: Active power flow $P$ is predominantly driven by voltage phase angle differences $\delta$, showing very weak sensitivity to voltage magnitude variations ($\frac{\partial P}{\partial |V|} \approx 0 \implies N \approx 0$). Conversely, reactive power flow $Q$ is primarily controlled by voltage magnitude differences $|V|$, being virtually insensitive to phase angles ($\frac{\partial Q}{\partial \delta} \approx 0 \implies M \approx 0$). This sets the off-diagonal sub-matrices of the Jacobian to zero: $$\begin{bmatrix} \Delta P \\ \Delta Q \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} H & 0 \\ 0 & L \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} \Delta \delta \\ \Delta |V| \end{bmatrix}$$ 2. Small Angle Differences: Under normal operating conditions, phase angle differences between adjacent buses are small (typically less than $5^\circ$): $$\cos(\delta_i - \delta_k) \approx 1, \quad \sin(\delta_i - \delta_k) \approx 0$$ 3. High $X/R$ Ratio: Overhead lines have line reactances far exceeding line resistances ($X \gg R \implies R/X \ll 1$): $$G_{ik} = \frac{R}{R^2 + X^2} \ll B_{ik} = \frac{-X}{R^2 + X^2} \implies G_{ik} \approx 0$$ 4. Reactive Injection Dominance: Bus reactive power injection is negligible compared to bus short-circuit susceptance ($Q_i \ll |V_i|^2 B_{ii}$).

Derivation of Decoupled Matrices $B'$ and $B''$: Under these physical simplifications, the diagonal and off-diagonal elements of $H$ and $L$ reduce to: $$H_{ik} = L_{ik} = -|V_i| |V_k| B_{ik}$$ $$H_{ii} = L_{ii} = -|V_i|^2 B_{ii}$$ Dividing both sides by $|V_i|$ and assuming $|V_k| \approx 1.0 \text{ pu}$: $$\frac{\Delta P_i}{|V_i|} = \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n -B_{ik} (\Delta\delta_k) - B_{ii} (\Delta\delta_i)$$ $$\frac{\Delta Q_i}{|V_i|} = \sum_{k=1, k\ne i}^n -B_{ik} (\Delta|V_k|) - B_{ii} (\Delta|V_i|)$$

In compact matrix notation: $$\left[ \frac{\Delta P}{|V|} \right] = \left[ B' \right] [\Delta \delta]$$ $$\left[ \frac{\Delta Q}{|V|} \right] = \left[ B'' \right] [\Delta |V|]$$ where: - Matrix $B'$ has dimension $(n - 1) \times (n - 1)$, formed by omitting the slack bus row and column from the imaginary part of $Y_{bus}$. Furthermore, elements that primarily influence MVAR flow (such as shunt reactances and transformer off-nominal taps) are omitted from $B'$. - Matrix $B''$ has dimension $(n - 1 - m) \times (n - 1 - m)$, formed by omitting the slack bus and all PV bus rows and columns. Phase-shifting transformer angle effects are omitted from $B''$.

Key Engineering Advantages: 1. Invariable Matrices: Both $B'$ and $B''$ are real, symmetric, sparse, and strictly constant. They are factorized into triangular $LU$ forms once at the start of the program and never re-evaluated during iterations. 2. Computational Speed: Each iteration requires only simple forward-backward substitutions. An FDLF iteration is roughly 5 times faster than a standard Newton-Raphson iteration. 3. Reliability: While it may require 1 to 2 more iterations than full N-R, overall solution speed is 3 to 4 times faster with virtually identical numerical accuracy.

Module 4

Power System Dynamics and Transient Stability

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: PS2-Q16

Derive the swing equation governing the rotor dynamics of a synchronous machine during transients. Define inertia constant H and angular momentum M, and express the equation in electrical radians and degrees. Derive the swing equation governing the rotor dynamics of a synchronous machine during transients. Define inertia constant H and angular momentum M, and express the equation in electrical radians and degrees. Derive the swing equation governing the rotor dynamics of a synchronous machine during transients. Define inertia constant H and angular momentum M, and express the equation in electrical radians and degrees.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{2H}{\omega_s} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e \quad \text{or} \quad \frac{H}{\pi f} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e, \quad M = \frac{G H}{\pi f}$$

Swing equation governs rotor acceleration driven by power imbalance $P_m - P_e$. Inertia constant $H$ defines kinetic energy in MJ per MVA rating, normalized across machines.

$$\frac{2H}{\omega_s} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e \quad \text{or} \quad \frac{H}{\pi f} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e, \quad M = \frac{G H}{\pi f}$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{2H}{\omega_s} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e \quad \text{or} \quad \frac{H}{\pi f} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e, \quad M = \frac{G H}{\pi f}$$

Swing equation governs rotor acceleration driven by power imbalance $P_m - P_e$. Inertia constant $H$ defines kinetic energy in MJ per MVA rating, normalized across machines.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Under steady-state operating conditions, the mechanical input torque supplied by the prime mover to a synchronous generator rotor is balanced by the internal electromagnetic opposing torque, causing the rotor to spin at synchronous angular velocity $\omega_s$. When a sudden disturbance occurs—such as a transmission line short-circuit, sudden load rejection, or generator tripping—this torque equilibrium is shattered. The resulting torque imbalance accelerates or decelerates the massive rotor according to the laws of rotational dynamics. The mathematical equation describing this relative angular motion is called the Swing Equation.

Let: - $J$ = total moment of inertia of the rotor and prime mover assembly ($\text{kg}\cdot\text{m}^2$). - $\theta_m$ = angular position of the rotor shaft with respect to a stationary reference axis (in mechanical radians). - $\omega_m = \frac{d\theta_m}{dt}$ = actual angular shaft velocity ($\text{rad/s}$). - $\omega_{sm}$ = synchronous mechanical angular velocity ($\text{rad/s}$). - $T_m$ = mechanical shaft torque supplied by the prime mover ($\text{N}\cdot\text{m}$). - $T_e$ = electromagnetic counter-torque developed by the generator ($\text{N}\cdot\text{m}$).

Applying Newton's second law of rotational mechanics: $$J \frac{d^2\theta_m}{dt^2} = T_m - T_e = T_a$$ where $T_a$ is the net accelerating torque.

It is physically convenient to measure the rotor position relative to a reference coordinate frame rotating at synchronous speed $\omega_{sm}$: $$\theta_m(t) = \omega_{sm} t + \delta_m(t)$$ where $\delta_m$ is the rotor power angle in mechanical radians. Differentiating with respect to time: $$\frac{d\theta_m}{dt} = \omega_{sm} + \frac{d\delta_m}{dt}$$ $$\frac{d^2\theta_m}{dt^2} = \frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2}$$ Substituting this back into the rotational equation: $$J \frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2} = T_m - T_e$$

Multiplying both sides by the synchronous shaft velocity $\omega_{sm}$: $$J \omega_{sm} \frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2} = \omega_{sm} T_m - \omega_{sm} T_e$$ The product $J \omega_{sm} = M_m$ is the angular momentum of the rotor. Recognizing that power is torque multiplied by angular velocity ($P = T \omega$): $$M_m \frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e = P_a$$ where $P_m$ is mechanical input power, $P_e$ is electrical power output, and $P_a$ is accelerating power.

Conversion to Electrical Radians and Degrees: Let $p$ be the number of magnetic poles. The relationship between electrical angle $\delta$ and mechanical angle $\delta_m$ is $\delta = \left(\frac{p}{2}\right) \delta_m$. $$\frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2} = \frac{2}{p} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2}$$ Substituting this yields: $$\left(\frac{2}{p} M_m\right) \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e \implies M \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e$$ where $M = \frac{2}{p} M_m$ is the angular momentum in electrical units.

The Inertia Constant $H$: In power engineering, machines of vastly different physical sizes have widely varying values of $J$ and $M$. To achieve a normalized, machine-independent metric, the Inertia Constant $H$ is defined as the stored kinetic energy at rated synchronous speed divided by the rated three-phase apparent power $G$ (in MVA): $$H = \frac{\text{Stored Kinetic Energy at Synchronous Speed (MJ)}}{\text{Generator Rating } G \text{ (MVA)}} = \frac{\frac{1}{2} J \omega_{sm}^2}{G} \text{ (seconds)}$$ The physical dimension of $H$ is seconds, physically representing the time in seconds a machine could supply rated power entirely from its stored kinetic energy. Typical values range from 2 to 9 seconds. Since stored kinetic energy is $G H = \frac{1}{2} M_m \omega_{sm} = \frac{1}{2} M \omega_s$: $$M = \frac{2 G H}{\omega_s} = \frac{2 G H}{2\pi f} = \frac{G H}{\pi f} \text{ (MJ}\cdot\text{s/elec rad)}$$ In electrical degrees: $$M = \frac{G H}{180 f} \text{ (MJ}\cdot\text{s/elec deg)}$$

Dividing by the generator rating $G$ to convert powers to per-unit: $$\frac{H}{\pi f} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_{m,pu} - P_{e,pu} \quad (\delta \text{ in elec radians})$$ $$\frac{H}{180 f} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_{m,pu} - P_{e,pu} \quad (\delta \text{ in elec degrees})$$ This fundamental second-order non-linear differential equation is the Swing Equation, governing all transient stability simulations in power systems.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

To achieve a normalized, machine-independent metric, the Inertia Constant $H$ is defined as the stored kinetic energy at rated synchronous speed divided by the rated three-phase apparent power $G$ (in MVA): $$H = \frac{\text{Stored Kinetic Energy at Synchronous Speed (MJ)}}{\text{Generator Rating } G \text{ (MVA)}} = \frac{\frac{1}{2} J \omega_{sm}^2}{G} \text{ (seconds)}$$ The physical dimension of $H$ is seconds, physically representing the time in seconds a machine could supply rated power entirely from its stored kinetic energy. Typical values range from 2 to 9 seconds. Since stored kinetic energy is $G H = \frac{1}{2} M_m \omega_{sm} = \frac{1}{2} M \omega_s$: $$M = \frac{2 G H}{\omega_s} = \frac{2 G H}{2\pi f} = \frac{G H}{\pi f} \text{ (MJ}\cdot\text{s/elec rad)}$$ In electrical degrees: $$M = \frac{G H}{180 f} \text{ (MJ}\cdot\text{s/elec deg)}$$

Dividing by the generator rating $G$ to convert powers to per-unit: $$\frac{H}{\pi f} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_{m,pu} - P_{e,pu} \quad (\delta \text{ in elec radians})$$ $$\frac{H}{180 f} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_{m,pu} - P_{e,pu} \quad (\delta \text{ in elec degrees})$$ This fundamental second-order non-linear differential equation is the Swing Equation, governing all transient stability simulations in power systems.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Under steady-state operating conditions, the mechanical input torque supplied by the prime mover to a synchronous generator rotor is balanced by the internal electromagnetic opposing torque, causing the rotor to spin at synchronous angular velocity $\omega_s$. When a sudden disturbance occurs—such as a transmission line short-circuit, sudden load rejection, or generator tripping—this torque equilibrium is shattered. The resulting torque imbalance accelerates or decelerates the massive rotor according to the laws of rotational dynamics. The mathematical equation describing this relative angular motion is called the Swing Equation.

Let: - $J$ = total moment of inertia of the rotor and prime mover assembly ($\text{kg}\cdot\text{m}^2$). - $\theta_m$ = angular position of the rotor shaft with respect to a stationary reference axis (in mechanical radians). - $\omega_m = \frac{d\theta_m}{dt}$ = actual angular shaft velocity ($\text{rad/s}$). - $\omega_{sm}$ = synchronous mechanical angular velocity ($\text{rad/s}$). - $T_m$ = mechanical shaft torque supplied by the prime mover ($\text{N}\cdot\text{m}$). - $T_e$ = electromagnetic counter-torque developed by the generator ($\text{N}\cdot\text{m}$).

Applying Newton's second law of rotational mechanics: $$J \frac{d^2\theta_m}{dt^2} = T_m - T_e = T_a$$ where $T_a$ is the net accelerating torque.

It is physically convenient to measure the rotor position relative to a reference coordinate frame rotating at synchronous speed $\omega_{sm}$: $$\theta_m(t) = \omega_{sm} t + \delta_m(t)$$ where $\delta_m$ is the rotor power angle in mechanical radians. Differentiating with respect to time: $$\frac{d\theta_m}{dt} = \omega_{sm} + \frac{d\delta_m}{dt}$$ $$\frac{d^2\theta_m}{dt^2} = \frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2}$$ Substituting this back into the rotational equation: $$J \frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2} = T_m - T_e$$

Multiplying both sides by the synchronous shaft velocity $\omega_{sm}$: $$J \omega_{sm} \frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2} = \omega_{sm} T_m - \omega_{sm} T_e$$ The product $J \omega_{sm} = M_m$ is the angular momentum of the rotor. Recognizing that power is torque multiplied by angular velocity ($P = T \omega$): $$M_m \frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e = P_a$$ where $P_m$ is mechanical input power, $P_e$ is electrical power output, and $P_a$ is accelerating power.

Conversion to Electrical Radians and Degrees: Let $p$ be the number of magnetic poles. The relationship between electrical angle $\delta$ and mechanical angle $\delta_m$ is $\delta = \left(\frac{p}{2}\right) \delta_m$. $$\frac{d^2\delta_m}{dt^2} = \frac{2}{p} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2}$$ Substituting this yields: $$\left(\frac{2}{p} M_m\right) \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e \implies M \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_m - P_e$$ where $M = \frac{2}{p} M_m$ is the angular momentum in electrical units.

The Inertia Constant $H$: In power engineering, machines of vastly different physical sizes have widely varying values of $J$ and $M$. To achieve a normalized, machine-independent metric, the Inertia Constant $H$ is defined as the stored kinetic energy at rated synchronous speed divided by the rated three-phase apparent power $G$ (in MVA): $$H = \frac{\text{Stored Kinetic Energy at Synchronous Speed (MJ)}}{\text{Generator Rating } G \text{ (MVA)}} = \frac{\frac{1}{2} J \omega_{sm}^2}{G} \text{ (seconds)}$$ The physical dimension of $H$ is seconds, physically representing the time in seconds a machine could supply rated power entirely from its stored kinetic energy. Typical values range from 2 to 9 seconds. Since stored kinetic energy is $G H = \frac{1}{2} M_m \omega_{sm} = \frac{1}{2} M \omega_s$: $$M = \frac{2 G H}{\omega_s} = \frac{2 G H}{2\pi f} = \frac{G H}{\pi f} \text{ (MJ}\cdot\text{s/elec rad)}$$ In electrical degrees: $$M = \frac{G H}{180 f} \text{ (MJ}\cdot\text{s/elec deg)}$$

Dividing by the generator rating $G$ to convert powers to per-unit: $$\frac{H}{\pi f} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_{m,pu} - P_{e,pu} \quad (\delta \text{ in elec radians})$$ $$\frac{H}{180 f} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = P_{m,pu} - P_{e,pu} \quad (\delta \text{ in elec degrees})$$ This fundamental second-order non-linear differential equation is the Swing Equation, governing all transient stability simulations in power systems.

Derive the power-angle equation Pe = Pmax sin(delta) for a synchronous machine connected to an infinite bus. Define synchronizing power coefficient and determine the steady-state stability limit. Derive the power-angle equation Pe = Pmax sin(delta) for a synchronous machine connected to an infinite bus. Define synchronizing power coefficient and determine the steady-state stability limit. Derive the power-angle equation Pe = Pmax sin(delta) for a synchronous machine connected to an infinite bus. Define synchronizing power coefficient and determine the steady-state stability limit.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$P_e = \frac{|E||V|}{X} \sin\delta = P_{max} \sin\delta, \quad P_{syn} = \frac{dP_e}{d\delta} = P_{max} \cos\delta > 0 \implies 0 \le \delta < 90^\circ$$

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

To understand how active and reactive power are transferred across a transmission network and to establish the theoretical boundaries of power transmission, we derive the Power-Angle relationship for a synchronous machine connected to an infinite bus.

Consider a round-rotor (cylindrical) synchronous generator represented by an internal excitation voltage phasor $E = |E| \angle \delta$ connected to an infinite bus of constant voltage $V = |V| \angle 0^\circ$ through a total series inductive reactance $X$. The total reactance $X = X_d + X_{tr} + X_{line}$ incorporates the generator synchronous reactance, step-up transformer reactance, and transmission line reactance. Line resistance is neglected ($R \ll X$).

Step 1: Stator Current Phasor Formulation: Applying Kirchhoff's Voltage Law around the circuit loop: $$E = V + j X I \implies I = \frac{E - V}{j X} = \frac{|E| \angle \delta - |V| \angle 0^\circ}{j X}$$ Expressing phasors in Cartesian rectangular coordinates: $$|E| \angle \delta = |E| \cos\delta + j |E| \sin\delta$$ $$|V| \angle 0^\circ = |V| + j 0$$ Substituting these expressions: $$I = \frac{(|E|\cos\delta - |V|) + j |E|\sin\delta}{j X} = \frac{|E|\sin\delta}{X} - j \frac{|E|\cos\delta - |V|}{X}$$

Step 2: Complex Power Output: The complex power $S = P_e + j Q_e$ delivered to the infinite bus is: $$S = V I^* = (|V| \angle 0^\circ) \left[ \frac{|E|\sin\delta}{X} + j \frac{|E|\cos\delta - |V|}{X} \right]$$ $$S = \frac{|E| |V|}{X} \sin\delta + j \left[ \frac{|E| |V|}{X} \cos\delta - \frac{|V|^2}{X} \right]$$

Equating real and imaginary parts: - Real Power Transfer Equation: $$P_e = \frac{|E| |V|}{X} \sin\delta = P_{max} \sin\delta$$ - Reactive Power Transfer Equation: $$Q_e = \frac{|E| |V|}{X} \cos\delta - \frac{|V|^2}{X}$$ where $P_{max} = \frac{|E||V|}{X}$ is the peak steady-state power transfer capability of the transmission link.

Graphical Characterization of the Power-Angle Curve: The real electrical power $P_e$ varies as a pure sinusoidal function of the rotor power angle $\delta$: - At $\delta = 0^\circ$: $P_e = 0$. (No active power is transferred). - As $\delta$ increases from $0^\circ$ toward $90^\circ$: $P_e$ increases monotonically, reaching its absolute peak $P_{max}$ at $\delta = 90^\circ$. - For $\delta > 90^\circ$: $P_e$ decreases with increasing rotor angle $\delta$.

Synchronizing Power Coefficient ($P_{syn}$): Steady-state stability refers to the capability of the power system to maintain synchronism when subjected to small, gradual disturbances (such as slow load variations). Let the machine operate at an equilibrium angle $\delta_0$ with mechanical input $P_{m0} = P_e(\delta_0)$. Suppose a small perturbation $\Delta\delta$ occurs. The incremental change in electrical power output is: $$\Delta P_e = \left.\frac{dP_e}{d\delta}\right|_{\delta_0} \Delta\delta = P_{syn} \Delta\delta$$ where $P_{syn} = \frac{dP_e}{d\delta}$ is the Synchronizing Power Coefficient (or stiffness coefficient): $$P_{syn} = \frac{d}{d\delta} \left[ P_{max} \sin\delta \right] = P_{max} \cos\delta = \frac{|E| |V|}{X} \cos\delta$$

Steady-State Stability Criterion: Substituting this perturbation into the linearized swing equation: $$M \frac{d^2(\Delta\delta)}{dt^2} + P_{syn} \Delta\delta = 0 \implies \frac{d^2(\Delta\delta)}{dt^2} + \left(\frac{P_{syn}}{M}\right) \Delta\delta = 0$$ The roots of the characteristic equation are $s = \pm \sqrt{-\frac{P_{syn}}{M}}$: - Case 1 ($P_{syn} > 0$): When $0 \le \delta_0 < 90^\circ$, $\cos\delta_0 > 0$, making $P_{syn}$ positive. The characteristic roots are purely imaginary: $s = \pm j \sqrt{P_{syn}/M} = \pm j \omega_n$. The system executes stable, bounded harmonic oscillations around $\delta_0$. In the presence of natural damper winding friction, these oscillations decay to zero, maintaining stability. - Case 2 ($P_{syn} < 0$): When $90^\circ < \delta_0 \le 180^\circ$, $\cos\delta_0 < 0$, making $P_{syn}$ negative. One characteristic root is real and positive: $s = +\sqrt{|P_{syn}|/M}$. The rotor angle diverges exponentially without bound, causing loss of synchronism (pole slipping).

Steady-State Stability Limit: The maximum power that can be transmitted through the system without losing stability under gradual changes is attained when $P_{syn} = 0$, which occurs at: $$\delta = 90^\circ \implies P_{limit} = P_{max} = \frac{|E| |V|}{X}$$ Operating beyond $\delta = 90^\circ$ is inherently unstable under constant excitation.

$$\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{max}} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta = 0 \implies A_1 = A_2 \quad (\text{Accelerating Area } = \text{ Decelerating Area})$$

Equal Area Criterion determines transient stability without solving differential equations. Stored kinetic energy gained during acceleration ($A_1$) must be completely absorbed during deceleration ($A_2$).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Transient stability analysis investigates the ability of a power system to maintain synchronism following severe, sudden disturbances such as short-circuit faults, line switching, or sudden heavy load additions. In single-machine infinite-bus (SMIB) systems, the Equal Area Criterion (EAC) provides an unshakeable graphical and mathematical method to determine transient stability directly without numerically integrating the non-linear swing differential equation.

Mathematical Derivation of the Equal Area Criterion: The dynamics of a synchronous machine are governed by the swing equation: $$\frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = \frac{P_m - P_e}{M} = \frac{P_a}{M}$$ where $P_a = P_m - P_e$ is the net accelerating power. Multiplying both sides of the equation by $2 \frac{d\delta}{dt}$: $$2 \frac{d\delta}{dt} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = \frac{2}{M}(P_m - P_e)\frac{d\delta}{dt}$$ Using the chain rule of calculus, the left-hand side is the exact time derivative of $\left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2$: $$\frac{d}{dt}\left[\left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2\right] = \frac{2}{M}(P_m - P_e)\frac{d\delta}{dt}$$ Multiplying through by differential time $dt$: $$d\left[\left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2\right] = \frac{2}{M}(P_m - P_e) \, d\delta$$

Integrating both sides from an initial steady-state operating angle $\delta_0$ to an arbitrary angle $\delta_1$: $$\left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2_{\delta_1} - \left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2_{\delta_0} = \frac{2}{M} \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_1} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta$$ Prior to the disturbance at $t = 0$, the machine is in steady-state equilibrium spinning synchronously, so the initial relative rotor angular velocity is zero: $\left.\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right|_{\delta_0} = 0$. Therefore: $$\left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)_{\delta_1} = \sqrt{\frac{2}{M} \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_1} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta}$$

Physical Principle of Stability: For the system to maintain transient stability, the rotor angle $\delta(t)$ must not increase indefinitely. The rotor must stop advancing relative to the synchronously rotating frame, reach a maximum swing angle $\delta_{max}$, and reverse its direction of motion. At the point of maximum rotor swing ($\delta = \delta_{max}$), the relative speed deviation must momentarily vanish: $$\left.\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right|_{\delta_{max}} = 0$$ Setting this condition in the integral yields the mathematical statement of the Equal Area Criterion: $$\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{max}} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta = 0$$

Splitting this integral at the intermediate equilibrium point $\delta_1$ where $P_m = P_e$: $$\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_1} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta + \int_{\delta_1}^{\delta_{max}} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta = 0$$ $$\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_1} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta = \int_{\delta_1}^{\delta_{max}} (P_e - P_m) \, d\delta$$ $$A_1 = A_2$$ - Accelerating Area ($A_1$): During $\delta_0 \le \delta \le \delta_1$, mechanical power exceeds electrical power ($P_m > P_e$). The net accelerating torque injects kinetic energy into the rotor, causing rotor speed to rise above synchronous speed ($\omega > \omega_s$). Area $A_1$ represents the total kinetic energy accumulated by the rotor. - Decelerating Area ($A_2$): Once the rotor angle swings past $\delta_1$, electrical output exceeds mechanical input ($P_e > P_m$). The net retarding torque extracts kinetic energy from the rotor, decelerating it back toward synchronous speed. Area $A_2$ represents the kinetic energy removed from the rotor. Transient stability is preserved if and only if the available decelerating area $A_2$ is equal to or greater than the accelerating area $A_1$ ($A_2 \ge A_1$).

Application to a Sudden Increase in Mechanical Power: Consider a generator operating initially at steady-state angle $\delta_0$ delivering power $P_{m0} = P_{max}\sin\delta_0$. Suppose the prime mover mechanical input abruptly steps up from $P_{m0}$ to $P_{m1}$. 1. Accelerating Area ($A_1$): $$A_1 = \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_1} (P_{m1} - P_{max}\sin\delta) \, d\delta = P_{m1}(\delta_1 - \delta_0) + P_{max}(\cos\delta_1 - \cos\delta_0)$$ where $\delta_1 = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{P_{m1}}{P_{max}}\right)$ is the new steady-state operating point. 2. Decelerating Area ($A_2$): The maximum angle to which the rotor can swing without losing stability is $\delta_{max} = 180^\circ - \delta_1 = \pi - \delta_1$. $$A_2 = \int_{\delta_1}^{\delta_{max}} (P_{max}\sin\delta - P_{m1}) \, d\delta = P_{max}(\cos\delta_1 - \cos\delta_{max}) - P_{m1}(\delta_{max} - \delta_1)$$ Equating $A_1 = A_2$ determines whether the system stabilizes at $\delta_1$ after damped oscillations or loses synchronism.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Transient stability analysis investigates the ability of a power system to maintain synchronism following severe, sudden disturbances such as short-circuit faults, line switching, or sudden heavy load additions. In single-machine infinite-bus (SMIB) systems, the Equal Area Criterion (EAC) provides an unshakeable graphical and mathematical method to determine transient stability directly without numerically integrating the non-linear swing differential equation.

Mathematical Derivation of the Equal Area Criterion: The dynamics of a synchronous machine are governed by the swing equation: $$\frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = \frac{P_m - P_e}{M} = \frac{P_a}{M}$$ where $P_a = P_m - P_e$ is the net accelerating power. Multiplying both sides of the equation by $2 \frac{d\delta}{dt}$: $$2 \frac{d\delta}{dt} \frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = \frac{2}{M}(P_m - P_e)\frac{d\delta}{dt}$$ Using the chain rule of calculus, the left-hand side is the exact time derivative of $\left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2$: $$\frac{d}{dt}\left[\left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2\right] = \frac{2}{M}(P_m - P_e)\frac{d\delta}{dt}$$ Multiplying through by differential time $dt$: $$d\left[\left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2\right] = \frac{2}{M}(P_m - P_e) \, d\delta$$

Integrating both sides from an initial steady-state operating angle $\delta_0$ to an arbitrary angle $\delta_1$: $$\left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2_{\delta_1} - \left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2_{\delta_0} = \frac{2}{M} \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_1} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta$$ Prior to the disturbance at $t = 0$, the machine is in steady-state equilibrium spinning synchronously, so the initial relative rotor angular velocity is zero: $\left.\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right|_{\delta_0} = 0$. Therefore: $$\left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)_{\delta_1} = \sqrt{\frac{2}{M} \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_1} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta}$$

Physical Principle of Stability: For the system to maintain transient stability, the rotor angle $\delta(t)$ must not increase indefinitely. The rotor must stop advancing relative to the synchronously rotating frame, reach a maximum swing angle $\delta_{max}$, and reverse its direction of motion. At the point of maximum rotor swing ($\delta = \delta_{max}$), the relative speed deviation must momentarily vanish: $$\left.\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right|_{\delta_{max}} = 0$$ Setting this condition in the integral yields the mathematical statement of the Equal Area Criterion: $$\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{max}} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta = 0$$

Splitting this integral at the intermediate equilibrium point $\delta_1$ where $P_m = P_e$: $$\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_1} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta + \int_{\delta_1}^{\delta_{max}} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta = 0$$ $$\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_1} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta = \int_{\delta_1}^{\delta_{max}} (P_e - P_m) \, d\delta$$ $$A_1 = A_2$$ - Accelerating Area ($A_1$): During $\delta_0 \le \delta \le \delta_1$, mechanical power exceeds electrical power ($P_m > P_e$). The net accelerating torque injects kinetic energy into the rotor, causing rotor speed to rise above synchronous speed ($\omega > \omega_s$). Area $A_1$ represents the total kinetic energy accumulated by the rotor. - Decelerating Area ($A_2$): Once the rotor angle swings past $\delta_1$, electrical output exceeds mechanical input ($P_e > P_m$). The net retarding torque extracts kinetic energy from the rotor, decelerating it back toward synchronous speed. Area $A_2$ represents the kinetic energy removed from the rotor. Transient stability is preserved if and only if the available decelerating area $A_2$ is equal to or greater than the accelerating area $A_1$ ($A_2 \ge A_1$).

Application to a Sudden Increase in Mechanical Power: Consider a generator operating initially at steady-state angle $\delta_0$ delivering power $P_{m0} = P_{max}\sin\delta_0$. Suppose the prime mover mechanical input abruptly steps up from $P_{m0}$ to $P_{m1}$. 1. Accelerating Area ($A_1$): $$A_1 = \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_1} (P_{m1} - P_{max}\sin\delta) \, d\delta = P_{m1}(\delta_1 - \delta_0) + P_{max}(\cos\delta_1 - \cos\delta_0)$$ where $\delta_1 = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{P_{m1}}{P_{max}}\right)$ is the new steady-state operating point. 2. Decelerating Area ($A_2$): The maximum angle to which the rotor can swing without losing stability is $\delta_{max} = 180^\circ - \delta_1 = \pi - \delta_1$. $$A_2 = \int_{\delta_1}^{\delta_{max}} (P_{max}\sin\delta - P_{m1}) \, d\delta = P_{max}(\cos\delta_1 - \cos\delta_{max}) - P_{m1}(\delta_{max} - \delta_1)$$ Equating $A_1 = A_2$ determines whether the system stabilizes at $\delta_1$ after damped oscillations or loses synchronism.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

In single-machine infinite-bus (SMIB) systems, the Equal Area Criterion (EAC) provides an unshakeable graphical and mathematical method to determine transient stability directly without numerically integrating the non-linear swing differential equation.

Mathematical Derivation of the Equal Area Criterion: The dynamics of a synchronous machine are governed by the swing equation: $$\frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = \frac{P_m - P_e}{M} = \frac{P_a}{M}$$ where $P_a = P_m - P_e$ is the net accelerating power. Integrating both sides from an initial steady-state operating angle $\delta_0$ to an arbitrary angle $\delta_1$: $$\left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2_{\delta_1} - \left(\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right)^2_{\delta_0} = \frac{2}{M} \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_1} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta$$ Prior to the disturbance at $t = 0$, the machine is in steady-state equilibrium spinning synchronously, so the initial relative rotor angular velocity is zero: $\left.\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right|_{\delta_0} = 0$. Splitting this integral at the intermediate equilibrium point $\delta_1$ where $P_m = P_e$: $$\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_1} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta + \int_{\delta_1}^{\delta_{max}} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta = 0$$ $$\int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_1} (P_m - P_e) \, d\delta = \int_{\delta_1}^{\delta_{max}} (P_e - P_m) \, d\delta$$ $$A_1 = A_2$$ - Accelerating Area ($A_1$): During $\delta_0 \le \delta \le \delta_1$, mechanical power exceeds electrical power ($P_m > P_e$). The net accelerating torque injects kinetic energy into the rotor, causing rotor speed to rise above synchronous speed ($\omega > \omega_s$). Area $A_1$ represents the total kinetic energy accumulated by the rotor. - Decelerating Area ($A_2$): Once the rotor angle swings past $\delta_1$, electrical output exceeds mechanical input ($P_e > P_m$). The net retarding torque extracts kinetic energy from the rotor, decelerating it back toward synchronous speed. Area $A_2$ represents the kinetic energy removed from the rotor. Application to a Sudden Increase in Mechanical Power: Consider a generator operating initially at steady-state angle $\delta_0$ delivering power $P_{m0} = P_{max}\sin\delta_0$. Suppose the prime mover mechanical input abruptly steps up from $P_{m0}$ to $P_{m1}$. 1. Decelerating Area ($A_2$): The maximum angle to which the rotor can swing without losing stability is $\delta_{max} = 180^\circ - \delta_1 = \pi - \delta_1$. $$A_2 = \int_{\delta_1}^{\delta_{max}} (P_{max}\sin\delta - P_{m1}) \, d\delta = P_{max}(\cos\delta_1 - \cos\delta_{max}) - P_{m1}(\delta_{max} - \delta_1)$$ Equating $A_1 = A_2$ determines whether the system stabilizes at $\delta_1$ after damped oscillations or loses synchronism.

Apply the Equal Area Criterion to derive the expression for the Critical Clearing Angle (delta_cr) for a three-phase fault on a double-circuit transmission line. $$\cos\delta_{cr} = \frac{P_m(\delta_{max} - \delta_0) - P_{max2}\cos\delta_0 + P_{max3}\cos\delta_{max}}{P_{max3} - P_{max2}}, \quad t_{cr} = \sqrt{\frac{2H(\delta_{cr} - \delta_0)}{\pi f P_m}}$$

Critical Clearing Angle $\delta_{cr}$ defines the maximum allowable rotor advance before fault isolation. Breakers must trip before critical clearing time $t_{cr}$ to preserve transient stability.

Critical Clearing Angle $\delta_{cr}$ defines the maximum allowable rotor advance before fault isolation. Breakers must trip before critical clearing time $t_{cr}$ to preserve transient stability.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

When a severe three-phase short-circuit occurs on a power transmission line, the electrical power that can be transferred across the faulted grid drops precipitously. During the fault, the prime mover continues to supply full mechanical power $P_m$, causing the generator rotor to accelerate rapidly. To prevent the rotor from losing synchronism, circuit breakers must trip and isolate the faulted line segment before the rotor advances past a critical threshold called the Critical Clearing Angle ($\delta_{cr}$). The corresponding elapsed time is the Critical Clearing Time ($t_{cr}$).

Consider a generator connected to an infinite bus through a double-circuit transmission line. The system operates across three distinct operational regimes: 1. Pre-Fault Regime: Both transmission lines are healthy. The power-angle relationship is: $$P_{e1} = P_{max1} \sin\delta$$ The initial operating angle $\delta_0$ is given by $P_m = P_{max1} \sin\delta_0 \implies \delta_0 = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{P_m}{P_{max1}}\right)$. 2. During-Fault Regime: A three-phase fault strikes one circuit. The electrical power drops to: $$P_{e2} = P_{max2} \sin\delta$$ (If the fault is a bolted fault directly at the generator bus terminals, $P_{max2} = 0$). 3. Post-Fault Regime: The faulted line circuit is isolated by circuit breakers, leaving only a single circuit in service. The transfer reactance increases, yielding intermediate power capability: $$P_{e3} = P_{max3} \sin\delta \quad (P_{max2} < P_{max3} < P_{max1})$$

Mathematical Derivation of Critical Clearing Angle $\delta_{cr}$: Let the fault be cleared at angle $\delta = \delta_{cr}$. 1. Accelerating Area ($A_1$): From initial angle $\delta_0$ to clearing angle $\delta_{cr}$, the system operates on the during-fault curve $P_{e2}$: $$A_1 = \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{cr}} (P_m - P_{e2}) \, d\delta = \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{cr}} (P_m - P_{max2}\sin\delta) \, d\delta$$ $$A_1 = \left[ P_m \delta + P_{max2}\cos\delta \right]_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{cr}} = P_m(\delta_{cr} - \delta_0) + P_{max2}(\cos\delta_{cr} - \cos\delta_0)$$

2. Decelerating Area ($A_2$): Once cleared at $\delta_{cr}$, the system operates on the post-fault curve $P_{e3}$ until reaching maximum swing angle $\delta_{max}$: $$A_2 = \int_{\delta_{cr}}^{\delta_{max}} (P_{e3} - P_m) \, d\delta = \int_{\delta_{cr}}^{\delta_{max}} (P_{max3}\sin\delta - P_m) \, d\delta$$ The maximum stable swing angle $\delta_{max}$ is the intersection of mechanical power $P_m$ with the post-fault power curve: $$\delta_{max} = \pi - \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{P_m}{P_{max3}}\right)$$ Evaluating the integral: $$A_2 = \left[ -P_{max3}\cos\delta - P_m \delta \right]_{\delta_{cr}}^{\delta_{max}} = P_{max3}(\cos\delta_{cr} - \cos\delta_{max}) - P_m(\delta_{max} - \delta_{cr})$$

3. Enforcing the Equal Area Criterion ($A_1 = A_2$): $$P_m(\delta_{cr} - \delta_0) + P_{max2}(\cos\delta_{cr} - \cos\delta_0) = P_{max3}(\cos\delta_{cr} - \cos\delta_{max}) - P_m(\delta_{max} - \delta_{cr})$$ Expanding and collecting terms involving $\cos\delta_{cr}$ on the left-hand side: $$P_{max3}\cos\delta_{cr} - P_{max2}\cos\delta_{cr} = P_m(\delta_{max} - \delta_{cr} + \delta_{cr} - \delta_0) - P_{max2}\cos\delta_0 + P_{max3}\cos\delta_{max}$$ $$(P_{max3} - P_{max2})\cos\delta_{cr} = P_m(\delta_{max} - \delta_0) - P_{max2}\cos\delta_0 + P_{max3}\cos\delta_{max}$$ Dividing by $(P_{max3} - P_{max2})$ yields the celebrated formula: $$\cos\delta_{cr} = \frac{P_m(\delta_{max} - \delta_0) - P_{max2}\cos\delta_0 + P_{max3}\cos\delta_{max}}{P_{max3} - P_{max2}}$$ Special Case (Bolted Terminal Fault with $P_{max2} = 0$): $$\cos\delta_{cr} = \frac{P_m(\delta_{max} - \delta_0) + P_{max3}\cos\delta_{max}}{P_{max3}}$$

Derivation of Critical Clearing Time ($t_{cr}$): During the fault, if $P_{max2} = 0$, the electrical power output is zero ($P_e = 0$). The swing equation becomes: $$\frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = \frac{P_m}{M} = \frac{\pi f P_m}{H}$$ Integrating with respect to time, with initial condition $\left.\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right|_{t=0} = 0$: $$\frac{d\delta}{dt} = \left(\frac{\pi f P_m}{H}\right) t$$ Integrating a second time with initial condition $\delta(0) = \delta_0$: $$\delta(t) = \delta_0 + \frac{1}{2} \left(\frac{\pi f P_m}{H}\right) t^2$$ At clearing time $t = t_{cr}$, the angle reaches $\delta_{cr}$: $$\delta_{cr} - \delta_0 = \frac{\pi f P_m}{2 H} t_{cr}^2$$ Solving explicitly for the Critical Clearing Time: $$t_{cr} = \sqrt{\frac{2 H (\delta_{cr} - \delta_0)}{\pi f P_m}} \text{ seconds} \quad (\delta \text{ in radians})$$ If protective relays and circuit breakers do not clear the short circuit within $t_{cr}$, transient stability is irrecoverably lost.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

When a severe three-phase short-circuit occurs on a power transmission line, the electrical power that can be transferred across the faulted grid drops precipitously. 2. 3. Derivation of Critical Clearing Time ($t_{cr}$): During the fault, if $P_{max2} = 0$, the electrical power output is zero ($P_e = 0$). The swing equation becomes: $$\frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = \frac{P_m}{M} = \frac{\pi f P_m}{H}$$ Integrating with respect to time, with initial condition $\left.\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right|_{t=0} = 0$: $$\frac{d\delta}{dt} = \left(\frac{\pi f P_m}{H}\right) t$$ Integrating a second time with initial condition $\delta(0) = \delta_0$: $$\delta(t) = \delta_0 + \frac{1}{2} \left(\frac{\pi f P_m}{H}\right) t^2$$ At clearing time $t = t_{cr}$, the angle reaches $\delta_{cr}$: $$\delta_{cr} - \delta_0 = \frac{\pi f P_m}{2 H} t_{cr}^2$$ Solving explicitly for the Critical Clearing Time: $$t_{cr} = \sqrt{\frac{2 H (\delta_{cr} - \delta_0)}{\pi f P_m}} \text{ seconds} \quad (\delta \text{ in radians})$$ If protective relays and circuit breakers do not clear the short circuit within $t_{cr}$, transient stability is irrecoverably lost.

When a severe three-phase short-circuit occurs on a power transmission line, the electrical power that can be transferred across the faulted grid drops precipitously. The power-angle relationship is: $$P_{e1} = P_{max1} \sin\delta$$ The initial operating angle $\delta_0$ is given by $P_m = P_{max1} \sin\delta_0 \implies \delta_0 = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{P_m}{P_{max1}}\right)$. 2. During-Fault Regime: A three-phase fault strikes one circuit. The electrical power drops to: $$P_{e2} = P_{max2} \sin\delta$$ (If the fault is a bolted fault directly at the generator bus terminals, $P_{max2} = 0$). 3.
