The transfer reactance increases, yielding intermediate power capability: $$P_{e3} = P_{max3} \sin\delta \quad (P_{max2} < P_{max3} < P_{max1})$$

Mathematical Derivation of Critical Clearing Angle $\delta_{cr}$: Let the fault be cleared at angle $\delta = \delta_{cr}$. 1. Accelerating Area ($A_1$): From initial angle $\delta_0$ to clearing angle $\delta_{cr}$, the system operates on the during-fault curve $P_{e2}$: $$A_1 = \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{cr}} (P_m - P_{e2}) \, d\delta = \int_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{cr}} (P_m - P_{max2}\sin\delta) \, d\delta$$ $$A_1 = \left[ P_m \delta + P_{max2}\cos\delta \right]_{\delta_0}^{\delta_{cr}} = P_m(\delta_{cr} - \delta_0) + P_{max2}(\cos\delta_{cr} - \cos\delta_0)$$

2. Decelerating Area ($A_2$): Once cleared at $\delta_{cr}$, the system operates on the post-fault curve $P_{e3}$ until reaching maximum swing angle $\delta_{max}$: $$A_2 = \int_{\delta_{cr}}^{\delta_{max}} (P_{e3} - P_m) \, d\delta = \int_{\delta_{cr}}^{\delta_{max}} (P_{max3}\sin\delta - P_m) \, d\delta$$ The maximum stable swing angle $\delta_{max}$ is the intersection of mechanical power $P_m$ with the post-fault power curve: $$\delta_{max} = \pi - \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{P_m}{P_{max3}}\right)$$ Evaluating the integral: $$A_2 = \left[ -P_{max3}\cos\delta - P_m \delta \right]_{\delta_{cr}}^{\delta_{max}} = P_{max3}(\cos\delta_{cr} - \cos\delta_{max}) - P_m(\delta_{max} - \delta_{cr})$$

3. Enforcing the Equal Area Criterion ($A_1 = A_2$): $$P_m(\delta_{cr} - \delta_0) + P_{max2}(\cos\delta_{cr} - \cos\delta_0) = P_{max3}(\cos\delta_{cr} - \cos\delta_{max}) - P_m(\delta_{max} - \delta_{cr})$$ Expanding and collecting terms involving $\cos\delta_{cr}$ on the left-hand side: $$P_{max3}\cos\delta_{cr} - P_{max2}\cos\delta_{cr} = P_m(\delta_{max} - \delta_{cr} + \delta_{cr} - \delta_0) - P_{max2}\cos\delta_0 + P_{max3}\cos\delta_{max}$$ $$(P_{max3} - P_{max2})\cos\delta_{cr} = P_m(\delta_{max} - \delta_0) - P_{max2}\cos\delta_0 + P_{max3}\cos\delta_{max}$$ Dividing by $(P_{max3} - P_{max2})$ yields the celebrated formula: $$\cos\delta_{cr} = \frac{P_m(\delta_{max} - \delta_0) - P_{max2}\cos\delta_0 + P_{max3}\cos\delta_{max}}{P_{max3} - P_{max2}}$$ Special Case (Bolted Terminal Fault with $P_{max2} = 0$): $$\cos\delta_{cr} = \frac{P_m(\delta_{max} - \delta_0) + P_{max3}\cos\delta_{max}}{P_{max3}}$$

Derivation of Critical Clearing Time ($t_{cr}$): During the fault, if $P_{max2} = 0$, the electrical power output is zero ($P_e = 0$). The swing equation becomes: $$\frac{d^2\delta}{dt^2} = \frac{P_m}{M} = \frac{\pi f P_m}{H}$$ Integrating with respect to time, with initial condition $\left.\frac{d\delta}{dt}\right|_{t=0} = 0$: $$\frac{d\delta}{dt} = \left(\frac{\pi f P_m}{H}\right) t$$ Integrating a second time with initial condition $\delta(0) = \delta_0$: $$\delta(t) = \delta_0 + \frac{1}{2} \left(\frac{\pi f P_m}{H}\right) t^2$$ At clearing time $t = t_{cr}$, the angle reaches $\delta_{cr}$: $$\delta_{cr} - \delta_0 = \frac{\pi f P_m}{2 H} t_{cr}^2$$ Solving explicitly for the Critical Clearing Time: $$t_{cr} = \sqrt{\frac{2 H (\delta_{cr} - \delta_0)}{\pi f P_m}} \text{ seconds} \quad (\delta \text{ in radians})$$ If protective relays and circuit breakers do not clear the short circuit within $t_{cr}$, transient stability is irrecoverably lost.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 4.4: Equal Area Criterion for Fault Clearing Showing Pre-fault, During-fault, and Post-fault Power Curves — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)
3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: PS2-Q20

Explain the various practical methods employed in modern power systems to improve transient and dynamic stability, including fast fault clearing, auto-reclosing, dynamic braking, and FACTS devices. Explain the various practical methods employed in modern power systems to improve transient and dynamic stability, including fast fault clearing, auto-reclosing, dynamic braking, and FACTS devices. Explain the various practical methods employed in modern power systems to improve transient and dynamic stability, including fast fault clearing, auto-reclosing, dynamic braking, and FACTS devices.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$E_{acc} \propto t^2, \quad X_{eff} = X_{line} - X_C, \quad P_{max} = \frac{|E||V|}{X_{eff}}$$

Stability is enhanced by 2-cycle fast fault clearing, high-speed auto-reclosing, dynamic braking resistors, fast static excitation with PSS, series capacitors, and FACTS controllers (STATCOM/SVC/TCSC).

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$E_{acc} \propto t^2, \quad X_{eff} = X_{line} - X_C, \quad P_{max} = \frac{|E||V|}{X_{eff}}$$

Stability is enhanced by 2-cycle fast fault clearing, high-speed auto-reclosing, dynamic braking resistors, fast static excitation with PSS, series capacitors, and FACTS controllers (STATCOM/SVC/TCSC).

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$E_{acc} \propto t^2, \quad X_{eff} = X_{line} - X_C, \quad P_{max} = \frac{|E||V|}{X_{eff}}$$

Stability is enhanced by 2-cycle fast fault clearing, high-speed auto-reclosing, dynamic braking resistors, fast static excitation with PSS, series capacitors, and FACTS controllers (STATCOM/SVC/TCSC).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Power system transient stability depends directly on minimizing the accelerating area $A_1$ and maximizing the available decelerating area $A_2$ during and immediately following a fault. To prevent loss of synchronism and wide-area blackouts, power transmission utilities deploy a multi-layered suite of stability enhancement measures:

1. High-Speed Fault Clearing and Fast Circuit Breakers: Because accelerating energy accumulates as the square of elapsed time ($E_{acc} \propto t^2$), rapid fault clearance is the most effective single measure to preserve transient stability. Modern numerical microprocessor-based protection relays operate within 1 cycle ($20 \text{ ms}$), driving ultra-fast SF6 or vacuum circuit breakers with 2-cycle ($40 \text{ ms}$) contact separation times. Total fault clearing within 3 cycles dramatically reduces the clearing angle $\delta_{cr}$, keeping accelerating area $A_1$ small.

2. High-Speed Automatic Reclosing: Over 80% of high-voltage transmission line faults are transient in nature, caused by lightning flashovers that deionize within 0.3 to 0.5 seconds after breaker opening. High-speed single-pole or three-pole auto-reclosing re-energizes the healthy line within 20 to 30 cycles, promptly restoring full post-fault power transfer capability ($P_{max1}$) before the rotor swings into instability.

3. Dynamic Braking Resistors: Large shunt-connected resistive banks (typically rated at 50% to 100% of generator capacity) are installed at generator terminal buses. When a severe transmission fault is detected, the braking resistors are switched on via fast thyristor switches for approximately 0.5 seconds. The resistors artificially absorb the surplus mechanical prime mover power ($P_e = P_{fault} + P_{brake} \approx P_m$), suppressing rotor acceleration.

4. Fast Excitation Systems and Power System Stabilizers (PSS): Static excitation systems utilizing thyristor rectifiers provide exceptionally fast ceiling voltage response (ceiling voltages up to 3 to 4 times rated field voltage within $20 \text{ ms}$). This boosts generator internal flux linkages during and immediately after the fault, elevating the peak power capability $P_{max} = \frac{EV}{X}$. To prevent fast excitation from introducing negative damping into low-frequency inter-area electromechanical oscillations (0.2 to 2.0 Hz), Power System Stabilizers (PSS) inject auxiliary stabilizing phase-lead signals derived from rotor speed deviation ($\Delta\omega$) into the voltage regulator.

5. Series Capacitor Compensation: Installing series capacitor banks in long transmission lines cancels a portion of the inductive line reactance: $$X_{effective} = X_{line} - X_C$$ Reducing $X$ directly scales up the maximum power transfer capability ($P_{max} = \frac{EV}{X_{effective}}$), expanding the decelerating area $A_2$.

6. Flexible AC Transmission Systems (FACTS): Power electronic controllers enhance stability dynamically: - STATCOM (Static Synchronous Compensator) and SVC (Static Var Compensator): Inject high-speed reactive power at intermediate buses to support bus voltages during voltage dips. - TCSC (Thyristor Controlled Series Capacitor): Rapidly modulates series capacitive reactance to damp post-fault power swings.

Power System Control and Economic Dispatch

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: PS2-Q21

Develop the transfer function block diagram of an isolated single-area power system for Load Frequency Control (LFC). Derive the expression for steady-state frequency deviation following a step load change. Develop the transfer function block diagram of an isolated single-area power system for Load Frequency Control (LFC). Derive the expression for steady-state frequency deviation following a step load change. Develop the transfer function block diagram of an isolated single-area power system for Load Frequency Control (LFC). Derive the expression for steady-state frequency deviation following a step load change.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\Delta f_{ss} = \frac{-\Delta P_L}{D + \frac{1}{R}} = \frac{-\Delta P_L}{\beta}, \quad K_p = \frac{1}{D}, \quad \tau_p = \frac{2H}{f_0 D}$$

Primary governor droop leaves a steady-state frequency drop $\Delta f_{ss} = -\Delta P_L / \beta$, where $\beta = D + 1/R$ is system stiffness. Secondary integral control is required to restore frequency to $50 \text{ Hz}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\Delta f_{ss} = \frac{-\Delta P_L}{D + \frac{1}{R}} = \frac{-\Delta P_L}{\beta}, \quad K_p = \frac{1}{D}, \quad \tau_p = \frac{2H}{f_0 D}$$

Primary governor droop leaves a steady-state frequency drop $\Delta f_{ss} = -\Delta P_L / \beta$, where $\beta = D + 1/R$ is system stiffness. Secondary integral control is required to restore frequency to $50 \text{ Hz}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

In an interconnected electrical power grid, active power balance must be maintained continuously to preserve system frequency at nominal value ($50 \text{ Hz}$). If total electrical power demand exceeds mechanical generation, kinetic energy is drawn from the spinning rotors, causing system frequency to drop. Conversely, if generation exceeds load, frequency rises. Load Frequency Control (LFC) is the automatic control mechanism that senses frequency deviations and regulates turbine governor valves to restore equilibrium.

To derive the dynamic model of an isolated single-area power system, we formulate the transfer functions of its three constituent subsystems:

1. Speed Governor Mechanism: The mechanical-hydraulic or electro-hydraulic flyball governor compares the actual machine frequency against reference setpoint $\Delta P_{ref}$. The speed changer position is influenced by the speed droop regulation parameter $R$: $$\Delta P_g(s) = \frac{1}{1 + s \tau_g} \left[ \Delta P_{ref}(s) - \frac{1}{R} \Delta f(s) \right]$$ where $\tau_g$ is the governor time constant (typically 0.1 to 0.2 seconds), and $R$ is the speed regulation (droop) parameter expressed in $\text{Hz/MW}$ (or per-unit droop, typically 4% to 5%).

2. Prime Mover (Turbine) Model: The movement of the governor main steam valve $\Delta P_g$ alters the steam mass flow rate entering the turbine stages, developing mechanical power $\Delta P_t$: $$\Delta P_t(s) = \frac{1}{1 + s \tau_t} \Delta P_g(s)$$ where $\tau_t$ is the turbine charging time constant (typically 0.2 to 2.0 seconds).

3. Generator-Load Dynamic Model: The net accelerating power driving the system is the difference between mechanical turbine power $\Delta P_t$ and consumer load perturbation $\Delta P_L$: $$\Delta P_a = \Delta P_t - \Delta P_L$$ The electrical power load demanded by induction motors and industrial drives varies inherently with system frequency: $$\Delta P_e = \Delta P_L + D \Delta f$$ where $D = \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial f}$ is the load damping constant expressed in $\text{MW/Hz}$ (or per-unit $\text{pu/Hz}$), typically 1% to 2% power change per 1% frequency change ($D \approx 1.0 \text{ to } 2.0$). Substituting into the swing equation in per-unit: $$\frac{2H}{f_0} \frac{d(\Delta f)}{dt} = \Delta P_t - (\Delta P_L + D \Delta f)$$ Taking the Laplace transform on both sides: $$\left( \frac{2H}{f_0} s + D \right) \Delta f(s) = \Delta P_t(s) - \Delta P_L(s)$$ Dividing through by $D$: $$\Delta f(s) = \frac{1/D}{1 + s \left(\frac{2H}{f_0 D}\right)} \left[ \Delta P_t(s) - \Delta P_L(s) \right] = \frac{K_p}{1 + s \tau_p} \left[ \Delta P_t(s) - \Delta P_L(s) \right]$$ where: - Power system gain: $K_p = \frac{1}{D}$ - Power system time constant: $\tau_p = \frac{2H}{f_0 D}$ (typically 10 to 20 seconds).

Closed-Loop System Derivation for Uncontrolled Primary Droop Case: In primary frequency control, the reference speed changer is fixed ($\Delta P_{ref}(s) = 0$). The overall closed-loop transfer function relating frequency deviation $\Delta f(s)$ to a disturbance step in load $\Delta P_L(s) = \frac{\Delta P_L}{s}$ is: $$\Delta f(s) = \frac{- \frac{K_p}{1 + s \tau_p}}{1 + \left(\frac{1}{1 + s \tau_g}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1 + s \tau_t}\right)\left(\frac{K_p}{1 + s \tau_p}\right)\frac{1}{R}} \cdot \Delta P_L(s)$$

Evaluating Steady-State Frequency Deviation ($\Delta f_{ss}$): Using the Final Value Theorem of Laplace transforms: $$\Delta f_{ss} = \lim_{t \to \infty} \Delta f(t) = \lim_{s \to 0} s \Delta f(s)$$ Substituting $\Delta P_L(s) = \frac{\Delta P_L}{s}$ and setting $s \to 0$: $$\Delta f_{ss} = \lim_{s \to 0} s \left[ \frac{-\frac{K_p}{1 + s\tau_p}}{1 + \frac{K_p / R}{(1 + s\tau_g)(1 + s\tau_t)(1 + s\tau_p)}} \cdot \frac{\Delta P_L}{s} \right]$$ $$\Delta f_{ss} = \frac{-K_p}{1 + \frac{K_p}{R}} \Delta P_L = \frac{-1/D}{1 + \frac{1/D}{R}} \Delta P_L = \frac{-1}{D + \frac{1}{R}} \Delta P_L = \frac{-\Delta P_L}{D + \frac{1}{R}}$$ Defining the Area Frequency Response Characteristic (AFRC) or system stiffness parameter: $$\beta = D + \frac{1}{R} \text{ (in MW/Hz)}$$ The steady-state frequency drop is: $$\Delta f_{ss} = -\frac{\Delta P_L}{\beta} = -\frac{\Delta P_L}{D + \frac{1}{R}}$$

Physical Significance: This derivation proves that primary governor droop control inherently leaves a non-zero steady-state frequency offset ($\Delta f_{ss} \ne 0$) following any load change. Because $1/R \gg D$, the governor droop provides over 95% of the primary frequency restoring capability. To force this residual frequency error completely to zero, a secondary integral control loop must be incorporated.

3. Generator-Load Dynamic Model: The net accelerating power driving the system is the difference between mechanical turbine power $\Delta P_t$ and consumer load perturbation $\Delta P_L$: $$\Delta P_a = \Delta P_t - \Delta P_L$$ The electrical power load demanded by induction motors and industrial drives varies inherently with system frequency: $$\Delta P_e = \Delta P_L + D \Delta f$$ where $D = \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial f}$ is the load damping constant expressed in $\text{MW/Hz}$ (or per-unit $\text{pu/Hz}$), typically 1% to 2% power change per 1% frequency change ($D \approx 1.0 \text{ to } 2.0$). Substituting into the swing equation in per-unit: $$\frac{2H}{f_0} \frac{d(\Delta f)}{dt} = \Delta P_t - (\Delta P_L + D \Delta f)$$ Taking the Laplace transform on both sides: $$\left( \frac{2H}{f_0} s + D \right) \Delta f(s) = \Delta P_t(s) - \Delta P_L(s)$$ Dividing through by $D$: $$\Delta f(s) = \frac{1/D}{1 + s \left(\frac{2H}{f_0 D}\right)} \left[ \Delta P_t(s) - \Delta P_L(s) \right] = \frac{K_p}{1 + s \tau_p} \left[ \Delta P_t(s) - \Delta P_L(s) \right]$$ where: - Power system gain: $K_p = \frac{1}{D}$ - Power system time constant: $\tau_p = \frac{2H}{f_0 D}$ (typically 10 to 20 seconds).

Closed-Loop System Derivation for Uncontrolled Primary Droop Case: In primary frequency control, the reference speed changer is fixed ($\Delta P_{ref}(s) = 0$). The overall closed-loop transfer function relating frequency deviation $\Delta f(s)$ to a disturbance step in load $\Delta P_L(s) = \frac{\Delta P_L}{s}$ is: $$\Delta f(s) = \frac{- \frac{K_p}{1 + s \tau_p}}{1 + \left(\frac{1}{1 + s \tau_g}\right)\left(\frac{1}{1 + s \tau_t}\right)\left(\frac{K_p}{1 + s \tau_p}\right)\frac{1}{R}} \cdot \Delta P_L(s)$$

Evaluating Steady-State Frequency Deviation ($\Delta f_{ss}$): Using the Final Value Theorem of Laplace transforms: $$\Delta f_{ss} = \lim_{t \to \infty} \Delta f(t) = \lim_{s \to 0} s \Delta f(s)$$ Substituting $\Delta P_L(s) = \frac{\Delta P_L}{s}$ and setting $s \to 0$: $$\Delta f_{ss} = \lim_{s \to 0} s \left[ \frac{-\frac{K_p}{1 + s\tau_p}}{1 + \frac{K_p / R}{(1 + s\tau_g)(1 + s\tau_t)(1 + s\tau_p)}} \cdot \frac{\Delta P_L}{s} \right]$$ $$\Delta f_{ss} = \frac{-K_p}{1 + \frac{K_p}{R}} \Delta P_L = \frac{-1/D}{1 + \frac{1/D}{R}} \Delta P_L = \frac{-1}{D + \frac{1}{R}} \Delta P_L = \frac{-\Delta P_L}{D + \frac{1}{R}}$$ Defining the Area Frequency Response Characteristic (AFRC) or system stiffness parameter: $$\beta = D + \frac{1}{R} \text{ (in MW/Hz)}$$ The steady-state frequency drop is: $$\Delta f_{ss} = -\frac{\Delta P_L}{\beta} = -\frac{\Delta P_L}{D + \frac{1}{R}}$$

Physical Significance: This derivation proves that primary governor droop control inherently leaves a non-zero steady-state frequency offset ($\Delta f_{ss} \ne 0$) following any load change. Because $1/R \gg D$, the governor droop provides over 95% of the primary frequency restoring capability. To force this residual frequency error completely to zero, a secondary integral control loop must be incorporated.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 5.1: Transfer Function Block Diagram of Isolated Single-Area Load Frequency Control System — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)
7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: PS2-Q22

Explain secondary frequency control and the dynamics of a two-area interconnected power system. Derive the tie-line power flow equation and define the Area Control Error (ACE) for each control area. Explain secondary frequency control and the dynamics of a two-area interconnected power system. Derive the tie-line power flow equation and define the Area Control Error (ACE) for each control area. Explain secondary frequency control and the dynamics of a two-area interconnected power system. Derive the tie-line power flow equation and define the Area Control Error (ACE) for each control area.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$ACE_1 = \Delta P_{tie1} + B_1 \Delta f_1, \quad \Delta P_{tie12} = \frac{2\pi T_{12}}{s} (\Delta f_1 - \Delta f_2), \quad B_i = \beta_i = D_i + \frac{1}{R_i}$$

In interconnected two-area systems, each area regulates its own Area Control Error ($ACE = \Delta P_{tie} + B \Delta f$) via integral control, driving steady-state frequency and tie-line deviations to zero.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$ACE_1 = \Delta P_{tie1} + B_1 \Delta f_1, \quad \Delta P_{tie12} = \frac{2\pi T_{12}}{s} (\Delta f_1 - \Delta f_2), \quad B_i = \beta_i = D_i + \frac{1}{R_i}$$

In interconnected two-area systems, each area regulates its own Area Control Error ($ACE = \Delta P_{tie} + B \Delta f$) via integral control, driving steady-state frequency and tie-line deviations to zero.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$ACE_1 = \Delta P_{tie1} + B_1 \Delta f_1, \quad \Delta P_{tie12} = \frac{2\pi T_{12}}{s} (\Delta f_1 - \Delta f_2), \quad B_i = \beta_i = D_i + \frac{1}{R_i}$$

In interconnected two-area systems, each area regulates its own Area Control Error ($ACE = \Delta P_{tie} + B \Delta f$) via integral control, driving steady-state frequency and tie-line deviations to zero.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

While primary governor action halts dynamic frequency decay following a load surge, it leaves a permanent steady-state frequency offset ($\Delta f_{ss} = -\Delta P_L / \beta$). To eliminate this steady-state error and maintain contracted power exchanges across inter-utility tie-lines, Automatic Generation Control (AGC) introduces secondary integral control.

Secondary Frequency Control in an Isolated Area: An integral controller operates on the frequency error $\Delta f(t)$ to adjust the speed changer setpoint $\Delta P_{ref}$: $$\Delta P_{ref}(t) = -K_i \int_0^t \Delta f(\tau) \, d\tau \implies \Delta P_{ref}(s) = -\frac{K_i}{s} \Delta f(s)$$ Applying the Final Value Theorem to the closed-loop system: $$\Delta f_{ss} = \lim_{s \to 0} s \Delta f(s) = 0$$ The infinite DC gain of the integrator forces the steady-state frequency deviation identically to zero ($\Delta f_{ss} = 0$), restoring system frequency precisely to $50 \text{ Hz}$.

Two-Area Interconnected Power System Dynamics: In modern grids, neighboring control areas are linked via high-voltage AC tie-lines to share operating reserves and enhance reliability. Let Area 1 and Area 2 have nominal voltages $|V_1|$ and $|V_2|$ interconnected by a tie-line of reactance $X_{12}$. The real power transferred from Area 1 to Area 2 under steady-state conditions is: $$P_{tie12} = \frac{|V_1| |V_2|}{X_{12}} \sin(\delta_1 - \delta_2)$$ For small perturbations around the nominal operating angle difference $(\delta_1^0 - \delta_2^0)$: $$\Delta P_{tie12} = \left[ \frac{|V_1| |V_2|}{X_{12}} \cos(\delta_1^0 - \delta_2^0) \right] (\Delta\delta_1 - \Delta\delta_2) = T_{12} (\Delta\delta_1 - \Delta\delta_2)$$ where $T_{12}$ is the synchronizing torque coefficient of the tie-line (in $\text{MW/rad}$). Because electrical angle is the time integral of frequency ($\delta(t) = 2\pi \int \Delta f dt \implies \Delta\delta(s) = \frac{2\pi}{s} \Delta f(s)$): $$\Delta P_{tie12}(s) = \frac{2\pi T_{12}}{s} \left[ \Delta f_1(s) - \Delta f_2(s) \right]$$ By energy conservation, the tie-line power leaving Area 1 equals the power entering Area 2: $$\Delta P_{tie21} = -\Delta P_{tie12}$$

Steady-State Analysis for Uncontrolled Case: Let a load step increase $\Delta P_{L1}$ strike Area 1, with zero load change in Area 2 ($\Delta P_{L2} = 0$). Under primary governor control, the steady-state frequency deviations in both interconnected areas must become identical ($\Delta f_1 = \Delta f_2 = \Delta f_{ss}$): From the steady-state power balance equations: $$\Delta P_{m1} - \Delta P_{L1} - \Delta P_{tie12} = D_1 \Delta f_{ss} \implies -\frac{1}{R_1}\Delta f_{ss} - \Delta P_{L1} - \Delta P_{tie12} = D_1 \Delta f_{ss}$$ $$-\Delta P_{L1} - \Delta P_{tie12} = \left( D_1 + \frac{1}{R_1} \right) \Delta f_{ss} = \beta_1 \Delta f_{ss}$$ Similarly for Area 2: $$-\Delta P_{tie21} = \Delta P_{tie12} = \left( D_2 + \frac{1}{R_2} \right) \Delta f_{ss} = \beta_2 \Delta f_{ss}$$ Summing both area balance equations eliminates tie-line power $\Delta P_{tie12}$: $$-\Delta P_{L1} = (\beta_1 + \beta_2) \Delta f_{ss} \implies \Delta f_{ss} = \frac{-\Delta P_{L1}}{\beta_1 + \beta_2}$$ The resulting steady-state tie-line power flow is: $$\Delta P_{tie12} = \beta_2 \Delta f_{ss} = -\frac{\beta_2}{\beta_1 + \beta_2} \Delta P_{L1}$$ This demonstrates that Area 2 inherently helps Area 1 by supplying a portion of the load increase over the tie-line.

Area Control Error (ACE): Under tie-line bias control, each control area must absorb its own internal load changes rather than drawing permanently from its neighbor, while maintaining scheduled tie-line flows and system frequency. The Area Control Error ($ACE$) for each area is formulated as a linear combination of tie-line power deviation and frequency deviation: $$ACE_1 = \Delta P_{tie1} + B_1 \Delta f_1$$ $$ACE_2 = \Delta P_{tie2} + B_2 \Delta f_2$$ where $B_1$ and $B_2$ are the frequency bias factors. To achieve completely decoupled control, the frequency bias factor is set exactly equal to the area frequency response characteristic ($B_i = \beta_i = D_i + 1/R_i$). The secondary integral controllers integrate the respective ACE signals: $$\Delta P_{ref1} = -K_{i1} \int ACE_1 \, dt, \quad \Delta P_{ref2} = -K_{i2} \int ACE_2 \, dt$$ Under steady state ($ACE_1 = 0$ and $ACE_2 = 0$), both conditions are simultaneously satisfied: $$\Delta f_{ss} = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \Delta P_{tie, ss} = 0$$ Each area successfully adjusts its own generation to meet its local load change.

Draw the functional block diagram of an Automatic Voltage Regulator (AVR) for a synchronous generator. State the transfer functions of each constituent component and discuss stability compensation. ⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$G_{open}(s) = \frac{K_A K_E K_F K_R}{(1 + s\tau_A)(1 + s\tau_E)(1 + s\tau_F)(1 + s\tau_R)}$$

Automatic Voltage Regulator controls exciter voltage to maintain terminal voltage $|V_t|$. Rate feedback transformer stabilizers introduce phase lead to prevent oscillatory instability from exciter-generator lag poles.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

While Load Frequency Control (LFC) regulates real power and system frequency, the Automatic Voltage Regulator (AVR) governs the excitation system of a synchronous generator to maintain terminal voltage magnitude $|V_t|$ within strict operating limits ($\pm 2\%$) under changing reactive power loads.

The AVR is a closed-loop negative feedback servomechanism. Its primary functional subsystems and linearized transfer functions are modeled as follows:

1. Potential Transformer and Rectifier Sensor: Measures the three-phase terminal voltage $V_t$, rectifies it, and filters high-frequency ripple. It is modeled as a simple first-order lag: $$V_s(s) = \frac{K_R}{1 + s \tau_R} V_t(s)$$ where sensor gain $K_R \approx 1.0$, and sensor time constant $\tau_R$ is very small (0.01 to 0.05 seconds).

2. Voltage Error Comparator and Magnetic/Electronic Amplifier: The sensed feedback voltage $V_s$ is compared against reference setpoint $V_{ref}$ to produce error $\Delta V = V_{ref} - V_s$. The error is amplified to drive the exciter: $$V_a(s) = \frac{K_A}{1 + s \tau_A} \Delta V(s) = \frac{K_A}{1 + s \tau_A} [V_{ref}(s) - V_s(s)]$$ where amplifier gain $K_A$ is large (typically 10 to 400), and time constant $\tau_A$ is small (0.02 to 0.1 seconds).

3. Exciter System (Brushless AC or Static DC Exciter): The exciter supplies DC current to the synchronous generator rotor field winding. Modern brushless exciters consist of an inverted alternator and rotating diode bridge: $$V_f(s) = \frac{K_E}{1 + s \tau_E} V_a(s)$$ where exciter gain $K_E \approx 1.0$, and time constant $\tau_E$ ranges from 0.4 to 1.0 seconds.

4. Synchronous Generator Field and Armature Reaction Dynamics: The generator field winding voltage $V_f$ produces field current, developing internal armature flux and open-circuit terminal voltage. Neglecting saturation, the transfer function relating terminal voltage to field excitation is: $$V_t(s) = \frac{K_F}{1 + s \tau_F} V_f(s)$$ where generator field time constant $\tau_F$ is relatively large, ranging from 1.0 to 5.0 seconds.

Overall Open-Loop and Closed-Loop Transfer Functions: The open-loop transfer function of the uncompensated AVR is: $$G_{open}(s) = \frac{K_A K_E K_F K_R}{(1 + s\tau_A)(1 + s\tau_E)(1 + s\tau_F)(1 + s\tau_R)} \approx \frac{K}{(1 + s\tau_A)(1 + s\tau_E)(1 + s\tau_F)}$$ The closed-loop transfer function is: $$\frac{V_t(s)}{V_{ref}(s)} = \frac{G(s)}{1 + G(s) H(s)}$$

Stability Analysis and Transient Compensation: Because the loop contains three or four cascaded lag poles (with $\tau_F$ and $\tau_E$ dominating), increasing the amplifier gain $K_A$ to reduce steady-state terminal voltage error ($e_{ss} = \frac{1}{1 + K_A}$) inevitably pushes closed-loop poles across the imaginary axis into the right half plane, causing oscillatory instability. To ensure stable transient performance, a derivative feedback stabilizer (rate feedback transformer) with transfer function $\frac{s K_{st}}{1 + s \tau_{st}}$ is connected across the exciter output terminals back to the amplifier summing junction, providing phase lead compensation that damps oscillations and ensures rapid terminal voltage recovery.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

The error is amplified to drive the exciter: $$V_a(s) = \frac{K_A}{1 + s \tau_A} \Delta V(s) = \frac{K_A}{1 + s \tau_A} [V_{ref}(s) - V_s(s)]$$ where amplifier gain $K_A$ is large (typically 10 to 400), and time constant $\tau_A$ is small (0.02 to 0.1 seconds).

Neglecting saturation, the transfer function relating terminal voltage to field excitation is: $$V_t(s) = \frac{K_F}{1 + s \tau_F} V_f(s)$$ where generator field time constant $\tau_F$ is relatively large, ranging from 1.0 to 5.0 seconds.

Overall Open-Loop and Closed-Loop Transfer Functions: The open-loop transfer function of the uncompensated AVR is: $$G_{open}(s) = \frac{K_A K_E K_F K_R}{(1 + s\tau_A)(1 + s\tau_E)(1 + s\tau_F)(1 + s\tau_R)} \approx \frac{K}{(1 + s\tau_A)(1 + s\tau_E)(1 + s\tau_F)}$$ The closed-loop transfer function is: $$\frac{V_t(s)}{V_{ref}(s)} = \frac{G(s)}{1 + G(s) H(s)}$$

Stability Analysis and Transient Compensation: Because the loop contains three or four cascaded lag poles (with $\tau_F$ and $\tau_E$ dominating), increasing the amplifier gain $K_A$ to reduce steady-state terminal voltage error ($e_{ss} = \frac{1}{1 + K_A}$) inevitably pushes closed-loop poles across the imaginary axis into the right half plane, causing oscillatory instability. To ensure stable transient performance, a derivative feedback stabilizer (rate feedback transformer) with transfer function $\frac{s K_{st}}{1 + s \tau_{st}}$ is connected across the exciter output terminals back to the amplifier summing junction, providing phase lead compensation that damps oscillations and ensures rapid terminal voltage recovery.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

While Load Frequency Control (LFC) regulates real power and system frequency, the Automatic Voltage Regulator (AVR) governs the excitation system of a synchronous generator to maintain terminal voltage magnitude $|V_t|$ within strict operating limits ($\pm 2\%$) under changing reactive power loads.

The AVR is a closed-loop negative feedback servomechanism. Its primary functional subsystems and linearized transfer functions are modeled as follows:

1. Potential Transformer and Rectifier Sensor: Measures the three-phase terminal voltage $V_t$, rectifies it, and filters high-frequency ripple. It is modeled as a simple first-order lag: $$V_s(s) = \frac{K_R}{1 + s \tau_R} V_t(s)$$ where sensor gain $K_R \approx 1.0$, and sensor time constant $\tau_R$ is very small (0.01 to 0.05 seconds).

2. Voltage Error Comparator and Magnetic/Electronic Amplifier: The sensed feedback voltage $V_s$ is compared against reference setpoint $V_{ref}$ to produce error $\Delta V = V_{ref} - V_s$. The error is amplified to drive the exciter: $$V_a(s) = \frac{K_A}{1 + s \tau_A} \Delta V(s) = \frac{K_A}{1 + s \tau_A} [V_{ref}(s) - V_s(s)]$$ where amplifier gain $K_A$ is large (typically 10 to 400), and time constant $\tau_A$ is small (0.02 to 0.1 seconds).

3. Exciter System (Brushless AC or Static DC Exciter): The exciter supplies DC current to the synchronous generator rotor field winding. Modern brushless exciters consist of an inverted alternator and rotating diode bridge: $$V_f(s) = \frac{K_E}{1 + s \tau_E} V_a(s)$$ where exciter gain $K_E \approx 1.0$, and time constant $\tau_E$ ranges from 0.4 to 1.0 seconds.

4. Synchronous Generator Field and Armature Reaction Dynamics: The generator field winding voltage $V_f$ produces field current, developing internal armature flux and open-circuit terminal voltage. Neglecting saturation, the transfer function relating terminal voltage to field excitation is: $$V_t(s) = \frac{K_F}{1 + s \tau_F} V_f(s)$$ where generator field time constant $\tau_F$ is relatively large, ranging from 1.0 to 5.0 seconds.

Overall Open-Loop and Closed-Loop Transfer Functions: The open-loop transfer function of the uncompensated AVR is: $$G_{open}(s) = \frac{K_A K_E K_F K_R}{(1 + s\tau_A)(1 + s\tau_E)(1 + s\tau_F)(1 + s\tau_R)} \approx \frac{K}{(1 + s\tau_A)(1 + s\tau_E)(1 + s\tau_F)}$$ The closed-loop transfer function is: $$\frac{V_t(s)}{V_{ref}(s)} = \frac{G(s)}{1 + G(s) H(s)}$$

Stability Analysis and Transient Compensation: Because the loop contains three or four cascaded lag poles (with $\tau_F$ and $\tau_E$ dominating), increasing the amplifier gain $K_A$ to reduce steady-state terminal voltage error ($e_{ss} = \frac{1}{1 + K_A}$) inevitably pushes closed-loop poles across the imaginary axis into the right half plane, causing oscillatory instability. To ensure stable transient performance, a derivative feedback stabilizer (rate feedback transformer) with transfer function $\frac{s K_{st}}{1 + s \tau_{st}}$ is connected across the exciter output terminals back to the amplifier summing junction, providing phase lead compensation that damps oscillations and ensures rapid terminal voltage recovery.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 5.3: Closed-Loop Block Diagram of Excitation System and Automatic Voltage Regulator — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)

Long Derivation ID: PS2-Q24

Formulate the economic load dispatch problem for thermal generating units neglecting transmission line losses. Using Lagrange multipliers, derive the criterion of equal incremental fuel costs. Formulate the economic load dispatch problem for thermal generating units neglecting transmission line losses. Using Lagrange multipliers, derive the criterion of equal incremental fuel costs. Formulate the economic load dispatch problem for thermal generating units neglecting transmission line losses. Using Lagrange multipliers, derive the criterion of equal incremental fuel costs.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{dC_1}{dP_1} = \frac{dC_2}{dP_2} = \dots = \frac{dC_N}{dP_N} = \lambda, \quad \sum_{i=1}^N P_i = P_D, \quad P_{i, min} \le P_i \le P_{i, max}$$

Economic load dispatch neglecting losses mandates that all generating units operate at the Equal Incremental Fuel Cost $\lambda$. Constrained units are pegged at their limit, and remaining load is re-dispatched.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The fundamental objective of Economic Load Dispatch (ELD) is to allocate the total system power demand among all available committed generating units in an electrical utility in such a manner that the total operational fuel cost is strictly minimized, while satisfying all physical generation limits and power balance constraints.

Problem Formulation: Consider a thermal power station comprising $N$ generating units supplying a total consumer load demand $P_D$. Let $C_i(P_i)$ represent the hourly fuel cost function of generating unit $i$ (in $\text{Rs/h}$ or $\$ \text{/h}$), modeled by the quadratic relationship: $$C_i(P_i) = \alpha_i + \beta_i P_i + \gamma_i P_i^2 \text{ Rs/h}$$ where $\alpha_i, \beta_i, \gamma_i$ are empirical fuel cost coefficients. The overall objective function to be minimized is the total generation cost: $$\text{Minimize } C_t = \sum_{i=1}^N C_i(P_i) = C_1(P_1) + C_2(P_2) + \dots + C_N(P_N)$$

Subject to Constraints: 1. Equality Constraint (Power Balance): Neglecting transmission network line losses ($P_L \approx 0$), total generation must strictly equal total consumer demand: $$\sum_{i=1}^N P_i = P_D \implies \phi(P_1, P_2, \dots, P_N) = \sum_{i=1}^N P_i - P_D = 0$$ 2. Inequality Constraints (Generator Operating Limits): Each thermal turbine-generator has lower physical limits (boiler flame stability, minimum steam flow) and upper thermal ratings: $$P_{i,min} \le P_i \le P_{i,max} \quad \forall i = 1, 2, \dots, N$$

Mathematical Derivation using the Method of Lagrange Multipliers: To convert this constrained optimization problem into an unconstrained formulation, we introduce the Lagrange Multiplier $\lambda$ and construct the Lagrangian function $\mathcal{L}$: $$\mathcal{L}(P_1, P_2, \dots, P_N, \lambda) = C_t - \lambda \cdot \phi = \sum_{i=1}^N C_i(P_i) - \lambda \left( \sum_{i=1}^N P_i - P_D \right)$$

Enforcing the Necessary Conditions for Optimality: For $\mathcal{L}$ to attain a local minimum, its partial derivatives with respect to all independent variables ($P_1, \dots, P_N$) and the multiplier $\lambda$ must vanish simultaneously: 1. Differentiating with respect to $P_i$: $$\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial P_i} = \frac{\partial}{\partial P_i} \left[ \sum_{k=1}^N C_k(P_k) \right] - \lambda \frac{\partial}{\partial P_i} \left[ \sum_{k=1}^N P_k - P_D \right] = 0$$ Because individual unit cost functions are uncoupled ($\frac{\partial C_k}{\partial P_i} = 0$ for $k \ne i$): $$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} - \lambda(1) = 0 \implies \frac{dC_i}{dP_i} = \lambda \quad \forall i = 1, 2, \dots, N$$ 2. Differentiating with respect to $\lambda$: $$\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial \lambda} = -\left( \sum_{i=1}^N P_i - P_D \right) = 0 \implies \sum_{i=1}^N P_i = P_D$$

The Equal Incremental Fuel Cost Criterion: The necessary condition for optimum economic operation when transmission losses are neglected is that all generating units must operate at the exact same Incremental Fuel Cost ($\lambda$): $$\frac{dC_1}{dP_1} = \frac{dC_2}{dP_2} = \frac{dC_3}{dP_3} = \dots = \frac{dC_N}{dP_N} = \lambda$$ where $\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} = \beta_i + 2\gamma_i P_i$ is the Incremental Cost (in $\text{Rs/MWh}$). The economic multiplier $\lambda$ represents the marginal cost of supplying the next incremental megawatt of power to the grid.

Incorporation of Generator Inequality Constraints: When unit operating limits $P_{i,min} \le P_i \le P_{i,max}$ are active, the Kuhn-Tucker optimality conditions govern the dispatch: - If $\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} = \lambda$, then $P_{i,min} < P_i < P_{i,max}$ (unit is regulating inside its limits). - If calculated $P_i > P_{i,max}$, the unit cannot supply the optimal power; clamp $P_i = P_{i,max}$. Its incremental cost satisfies $\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} \le \lambda$. - If calculated $P_i < P_{i,min}$, the unit cannot be backed down further; clamp $P_i = P_{i,min}$. Its incremental cost satisfies $\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} \ge \lambda$. Any unit pegged at its boundary is removed from further economic adjustment; its generation is subtracted from total demand, and the remaining load is re-dispatched economically among the unconstrained units at a revised $\lambda$.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: PS2-Q25

Formulate the economic dispatch problem including transmission network losses using Kron's loss formula. Derive the coordination equations and explain the physical significance of the Penalty Factor Li. Formulate the economic dispatch problem including transmission network losses using Kron's loss formula. Derive the coordination equations and explain the physical significance of the Penalty Factor Li. Formulate the economic dispatch problem including transmission network losses using Kron's loss formula. Derive the coordination equations and explain the physical significance of the Penalty Factor Li.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} \cdot L_i = \lambda, \quad L_i = \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i}}, \quad P_L = \sum\sum P_m B_{mn} P_n$$

Economic dispatch including losses coordinates incremental fuel cost with transmission Penalty Factor $L_i$. Remote units with high incremental losses ($L_i > 1$) are backed down to optimize overall delivered cost.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} \cdot L_i = \lambda, \quad L_i = \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i}}, \quad P_L = \sum\sum P_m B_{mn} P_n$$

Economic dispatch including losses coordinates incremental fuel cost with transmission Penalty Factor $L_i$. Remote units with high incremental losses ($L_i > 1$) are backed down to optimize overall delivered cost.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} \cdot L_i = \lambda, \quad L_i = \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i}}, \quad P_L = \sum\sum P_m B_{mn} P_n$$

Economic dispatch including losses coordinates incremental fuel cost with transmission Penalty Factor $L_i$. Remote units with high incremental losses ($L_i > 1$) are backed down to optimize overall delivered cost.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

In widespread electrical power grids where generating plants are geographically separated from major load centers by hundreds of kilometers, transmission network line losses ($P_L$) cannot be neglected. A generating station with exceptionally low fuel costs may be located so far from the consumer that the $I^2 R$ transmission losses incurred in wheeling its power negate its fuel economy. Therefore, economic dispatch algorithms must coordinate unit fuel costs with marginal transmission losses.

Kron's Transmission Loss Formula: Total three-phase active power transmission losses are expressed as a quadratic function of generator outputs using Kron's B-Loss Formula: $$P_L = \sum_{m=1}^N \sum_{n=1}^N P_m B_{mn} P_n + \sum_{n=1}^N B_{n0} P_n + B_{00}$$ where $B_{mn}$ are the transmission loss coefficients (in $\text{MW}^{-1}$), $B_{n0}$ are linear loss coefficients, and $B_{00}$ is a constant representing no-load shunt losses. In matrix notation: $$P_L = P^T [B] P + B_0^T P + B_{00}$$

Mathematical Formulation of Economic Dispatch with Losses: The objective is to minimize total generation fuel cost: $$\text{Minimize } C_t = \sum_{i=1}^N C_i(P_i)$$ Subject to the exact real power balance equality constraint including grid losses: $$\sum_{i=1}^N P_i = P_D + P_L \implies \sum_{i=1}^N P_i - P_D - P_L = 0$$

Lagrangian Formulation: Formulating the Lagrangian function with multiplier $\lambda$: $$\mathcal{L}(P_1, \dots, P_N, \lambda) = \sum_{i=1}^N C_i(P_i) - \lambda \left( \sum_{i=1}^N P_i - P_D - P_L \right)$$

Enforcing the Optimality Conditions: Differentiating $\mathcal{L}$ with respect to generator output $P_i$: $$\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial P_i} = \frac{dC_i}{dP_i} - \lambda \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial P_i} \sum_{k=1}^N P_k - 0 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \right) = 0$$ $$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} - \lambda \left( 1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \right) = 0$$ where $\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i}$ is the Incremental Transmission Loss (ITL) of plant $i$. Rearranging terms: $$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} = \lambda \left( 1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \right)$$ Dividing both sides by $\left( 1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \right)$: $$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} \cdot \left[ \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i}} \right] = \lambda$$

Definition of the Penalty Factor ($L_i$): We define the Penalty Factor $L_i$ for generating plant $i$ as: $$L_i = \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i}}$$ The governing optimality criterion is formulated as the Coordination Equations: $$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} \cdot L_i = \lambda \quad \forall i = 1, 2, \dots, N$$ $$\frac{dC_1}{dP_1} L_1 = \frac{dC_2}{dP_2} L_2 = \dots = \frac{dC_N}{dP_N} L_N = \lambda$$

Physical Significance of the Penalty Factor: 1. Plant Close to Load Center: If generating unit $i$ is situated directly at a major load center, transmitting its power incurs zero incremental loss ($\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \approx 0$). Consequently, its penalty factor is unity: $L_i \approx 1.0$. The unit is dispatched at its raw incremental fuel cost. 2. Remote Plant with High Transmission Loss: If unit $j$ is situated far away over heavily loaded lines, increasing its generation increases transmission losses ($\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_j} > 0$). This forces the denominator $\left(1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_j}\right) < 1$, making its penalty factor strictly greater than unity ($L_j > 1.0$). Since $\frac{dC_j}{dP_j} = \frac{\lambda}{L_j} < \lambda$, the remote generator is penalized and forced to operate at a lower incremental cost, backing down its generation. 3. Counter-Flow Relief: In rare network topologies, increasing generation at a specific bus unloads congested lines, actually reducing net losses ($\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_k} < 0$). In this case, $L_k < 1.0$, encouraging the system to increase dispatch from that unit.

Thus, the coordination equations seamlessly balance thermodynamic fuel cost against electrical transmission efficiency.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Kron's Transmission Loss Formula: Total three-phase active power transmission losses are expressed as a quadratic function of generator outputs using Kron's B-Loss Formula: $$P_L = \sum_{m=1}^N \sum_{n=1}^N P_m B_{mn} P_n + \sum_{n=1}^N B_{n0} P_n + B_{00}$$ where $B_{mn}$ are the transmission loss coefficients (in $\text{MW}^{-1}$), $B_{n0}$ are linear loss coefficients, and $B_{00}$ is a constant representing no-load shunt losses. In matrix notation: $$P_L = P^T [B] P + B_0^T P + B_{00}$$

Mathematical Formulation of Economic Dispatch with Losses: The objective is to minimize total generation fuel cost: $$\text{Minimize } C_t = \sum_{i=1}^N C_i(P_i)$$ Subject to the exact real power balance equality constraint including grid losses: $$\sum_{i=1}^N P_i = P_D + P_L \implies \sum_{i=1}^N P_i - P_D - P_L = 0$$

Lagrangian Formulation: Formulating the Lagrangian function with multiplier $\lambda$: $$\mathcal{L}(P_1, \dots, P_N, \lambda) = \sum_{i=1}^N C_i(P_i) - \lambda \left( \sum_{i=1}^N P_i - P_D - P_L \right)$$

Enforcing the Optimality Conditions: Differentiating $\mathcal{L}$ with respect to generator output $P_i$: $$\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial P_i} = \frac{dC_i}{dP_i} - \lambda \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial P_i} \sum_{k=1}^N P_k - 0 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \right) = 0$$ $$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} - \lambda \left( 1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \right) = 0$$ where $\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i}$ is the Incremental Transmission Loss (ITL) of plant $i$. Rearranging terms: $$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} = \lambda \left( 1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \right)$$ Dividing both sides by $\left( 1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \right)$: $$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} \cdot \left[ \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i}} \right] = \lambda$$

Definition of the Penalty Factor ($L_i$): We define the Penalty Factor $L_i$ for generating plant $i$ as: $$L_i = \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i}}$$ The governing optimality criterion is formulated as the Coordination Equations: $$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} \cdot L_i = \lambda \quad \forall i = 1, 2, \dots, N$$ $$\frac{dC_1}{dP_1} L_1 = \frac{dC_2}{dP_2} L_2 = \dots = \frac{dC_N}{dP_N} L_N = \lambda$$

Physical Significance of the Penalty Factor: 1. Plant Close to Load Center: If generating unit $i$ is situated directly at a major load center, transmitting its power incurs zero incremental loss ($\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \approx 0$). Consequently, its penalty factor is unity: $L_i \approx 1.0$. The unit is dispatched at its raw incremental fuel cost. 2. Remote Plant with High Transmission Loss: If unit $j$ is situated far away over heavily loaded lines, increasing its generation increases transmission losses ($\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_j} > 0$). This forces the denominator $\left(1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_j}\right) < 1$, making its penalty factor strictly greater than unity ($L_j > 1.0$). Since $\frac{dC_j}{dP_j} = \frac{\lambda}{L_j} < \lambda$, the remote generator is penalized and forced to operate at a lower incremental cost, backing down its generation. 3. Counter-Flow Relief: In rare network topologies, increasing generation at a specific bus unloads congested lines, actually reducing net losses ($\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_k} < 0$). In this case, $L_k < 1.0$, encouraging the system to increase dispatch from that unit.

Thus, the coordination equations seamlessly balance thermodynamic fuel cost against electrical transmission efficiency.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

In widespread electrical power grids where generating plants are geographically separated from major load centers by hundreds of kilometers, transmission network line losses ($P_L$) cannot be neglected. A generating station with exceptionally low fuel costs may be located so far from the consumer that the $I^2 R$ transmission losses incurred in wheeling its power negate its fuel economy. Therefore, economic dispatch algorithms must coordinate unit fuel costs with marginal transmission losses.

Kron's Transmission Loss Formula: Total three-phase active power transmission losses are expressed as a quadratic function of generator outputs using Kron's B-Loss Formula: $$P_L = \sum_{m=1}^N \sum_{n=1}^N P_m B_{mn} P_n + \sum_{n=1}^N B_{n0} P_n + B_{00}$$ where $B_{mn}$ are the transmission loss coefficients (in $\text{MW}^{-1}$), $B_{n0}$ are linear loss coefficients, and $B_{00}$ is a constant representing no-load shunt losses. In matrix notation: $$P_L = P^T [B] P + B_0^T P + B_{00}$$

Mathematical Formulation of Economic Dispatch with Losses: The objective is to minimize total generation fuel cost: $$\text{Minimize } C_t = \sum_{i=1}^N C_i(P_i)$$ Subject to the exact real power balance equality constraint including grid losses: $$\sum_{i=1}^N P_i = P_D + P_L \implies \sum_{i=1}^N P_i - P_D - P_L = 0$$

Lagrangian Formulation: Formulating the Lagrangian function with multiplier $\lambda$: $$\mathcal{L}(P_1, \dots, P_N, \lambda) = \sum_{i=1}^N C_i(P_i) - \lambda \left( \sum_{i=1}^N P_i - P_D - P_L \right)$$

Enforcing the Optimality Conditions: Differentiating $\mathcal{L}$ with respect to generator output $P_i$: $$\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial P_i} = \frac{dC_i}{dP_i} - \lambda \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial P_i} \sum_{k=1}^N P_k - 0 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \right) = 0$$ $$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} - \lambda \left( 1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \right) = 0$$ where $\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i}$ is the Incremental Transmission Loss (ITL) of plant $i$. Rearranging terms: $$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} = \lambda \left( 1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \right)$$ Dividing both sides by $\left( 1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \right)$: $$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} \cdot \left[ \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i}} \right] = \lambda$$

Definition of the Penalty Factor ($L_i$): We define the Penalty Factor $L_i$ for generating plant $i$ as: $$L_i = \frac{1}{1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i}}$$ The governing optimality criterion is formulated as the Coordination Equations: $$\frac{dC_i}{dP_i} \cdot L_i = \lambda \quad \forall i = 1, 2, \dots, N$$ $$\frac{dC_1}{dP_1} L_1 = \frac{dC_2}{dP_2} L_2 = \dots = \frac{dC_N}{dP_N} L_N = \lambda$$

Physical Significance of the Penalty Factor: 1. Plant Close to Load Center: If generating unit $i$ is situated directly at a major load center, transmitting its power incurs zero incremental loss ($\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_i} \approx 0$). Consequently, its penalty factor is unity: $L_i \approx 1.0$. The unit is dispatched at its raw incremental fuel cost. 2. Remote Plant with High Transmission Loss: If unit $j$ is situated far away over heavily loaded lines, increasing its generation increases transmission losses ($\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_j} > 0$). This forces the denominator $\left(1 - \frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_j}\right) < 1$, making its penalty factor strictly greater than unity ($L_j > 1.0$). Since $\frac{dC_j}{dP_j} = \frac{\lambda}{L_j} < \lambda$, the remote generator is penalized and forced to operate at a lower incremental cost, backing down its generation. 3. Counter-Flow Relief: In rare network topologies, increasing generation at a specific bus unloads congested lines, actually reducing net losses ($\frac{\partial P_L}{\partial P_k} < 0$). In this case, $L_k < 1.0$, encouraging the system to increase dispatch from that unit.

Thus, the coordination equations seamlessly balance thermodynamic fuel cost against electrical transmission efficiency.

Long Derivation ID: PS2-Q26

Two thermal generating units have fuel cost functions C1 = 0.05 P1^2 + 20 P1 + 800 Rs/h and C2 = 0.06 P2^2 + 15 P2 + 1000 Rs/h with limits 20 <= Pi <= 120 MW. For a total load of 180 MW, find optimal generation schedule, lambda, and calculate hourly cost savings compared to equal load sharing. Two thermal generating units have fuel cost functions C1 = 0.05 P1^2 + 20 P1 + 800 Rs/h and C2 = 0.06 P2^2 + 15 P2 + 1000 Rs/h with limits 20 <= Pi <= 120 MW. For a total load of 180 MW, find optimal generation schedule, lambda, and calculate hourly cost savings compared to equal load sharing. Two thermal generating units have fuel cost functions C1 = 0.05 P1^2 + 20 P1 + 800 Rs/h and C2 = 0.06 P2^2 + 15 P2 + 1000 Rs/h with limits 20 <= Pi <= 120 MW. For a total load of 180 MW, find optimal generation schedule, lambda, and calculate hourly cost savings compared to equal load sharing.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\lambda = 27.55 \text{ Rs/MWh}, \quad P_1 = 75.45 \text{ MW}, \quad P_2 = 104.55 \text{ MW}, \quad \text{Net Savings} = 23.27 \text{ Rs/h}$$

Equal incremental cost dispatch allocates $P_1 = 75.45 \text{ MW}, P_2 = 104.55 \text{ MW}$ at $\lambda = 27.55 \text{ Rs/MWh}$, reducing generation costs by $23.27 \text{ Rs/h}$ ($203,845 \text{ Rs/year}$) over equal sharing.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Consider a classic KTU university examination problem: A power station has two thermal generating units supplying a total system demand of $P_D = 180 \text{ MW}$. The quadratic fuel cost functions of the units are given by: $$C_1(P_1) = 0.05 P_1^2 + 20 P_1 + 800 \text{ Rs/h}$$ $$C_2(P_2) = 0.06 P_2^2 + 15 P_2 + 1000 \text{ Rs/h}$$ The operating limits of both generating units are: $$20 \text{ MW} \le P_1 \le 120 \text{ MW}$$ $$20 \text{ MW} \le P_2 \le 120 \text{ MW}$$ Neglecting transmission losses, determine: (i) The incremental fuel cost equations for each unit. (ii) The optimal economic generation schedule ($P_1$ and $P_2$) and the system marginal cost $\lambda$. (iii) The total hourly fuel cost under optimal economic dispatch. (iv) The total hourly fuel cost if the load is shared equally between the units ($P_1 = P_2 = 90 \text{ MW}$). (v) The net monetary savings in Rs/h achieved by deploying economic dispatch.

Step 1: Formulate Incremental Fuel Cost Equations: Differentiating the individual cost functions with respect to power output: - For Unit 1: $$\frac{dC_1}{dP_1} = \frac{d}{dP_1}\left(0.05 P_1^2 + 20 P_1 + 800\right) = 0.10 P_1 + 20 \text{ Rs/MWh}$$ - For Unit 2: $$\frac{dC_2}{dP_2} = \frac{d}{dP_2}\left(0.06 P_2^2 + 15 P_2 + 1000\right) = 0.12 P_2 + 15 \text{ Rs/MWh}$$

Step 2: Apply the Equal Incremental Cost Criterion: For optimal economic dispatch neglecting losses: $$\frac{dC_1}{dP_1} = \frac{dC_2}{dP_2} = \lambda$$ $$0.10 P_1 + 20 = \lambda \implies 0.10 P_1 = \lambda - 20 \implies P_1 = \frac{\lambda - 20}{0.10} = 10\lambda - 200$$ $$0.12 P_2 + 15 = \lambda \implies 0.12 P_2 = \lambda - 15 \implies P_2 = \frac{\lambda - 15}{0.12} = \frac{100}{12}\lambda - \frac{1500}{12} = 8.3333\lambda - 125$$

Step 3: Enforce the Power Balance Constraint: Total generation must equal total demand: $$P_1 + P_2 = P_D = 180 \text{ MW}$$ Substituting the expressions for $P_1$ and $P_2$ in terms of $\lambda$: $$(10\lambda - 200) + (8.3333\lambda - 125) = 180$$ $$18.3333\lambda - 325 = 180$$ $$18.3333\lambda = 180 + 325 = 505$$ $$\lambda = \frac{505}{18.3333} = \frac{505}{55/3} = \frac{1515}{55} = \frac{303}{11} \approx 27.5455 \text{ Rs/MWh}$$

Step 4: Calculate Optimal Generation Allocations: - For Unit 1: $$P_1 = 10(27.5455) - 200 = 275.455 - 200 = 75.455 \text{ MW}$$ - For Unit 2: $$P_2 = 8.3333(27.5455) - 125 = \frac{100}{12}\left(\frac{303}{11}\right) - 125 = \frac{30300}{132} - 125 = 229.545 - 125 = 104.545 \text{ MW}$$

Check Power Balance and Unit Limits: - Total power: $P_1 + P_2 = 75.455 + 104.545 = 180.000 \text{ MW} = P_D$. - Check limits: $20 \le P_1 (75.455) \le 120$ (Satisfied). $20 \le P_2 (104.545) \le 120$ (Satisfied). Both units operate strictly within their allowed operating boundaries.

Step 5: Compute Total Fuel Cost under Economic Dispatch: - Cost of Unit 1: $$C_1(75.455) = 0.05(75.455)^2 + 20(75.455) + 800$$ $$C_1 = 0.05(5693.46) + 1509.10 + 800 = 284.67 + 1509.10 + 800 = 2593.77 \text{ Rs/h}$$ - Cost of Unit 2: $$C_2(104.545) = 0.06(104.545)^2 + 15(104.545) + 1000$$ $$C_2 = 0.06(10929.65) + 1568.18 + 1000 = 655.78 + 1568.18 + 1000 = 3223.96 \text{ Rs/h}$$ - Total Economic Operating Cost: $$C_{total, economic} = C_1 + C_2 = 2593.77 + 3223.96 = 5817.73 \text{ Rs/h}$$

Step 6: Compute Fuel Cost under Equal Load Sharing ($P_1 = P_2 = 90 \text{ MW}$): If both generators simply share the load equally: - Cost of Unit 1 at $90 \text{ MW}$: $$C_1(90) = 0.05(90)^2 + 20(90) + 800 = 0.05(8100) + 1800 + 800 = 405 + 1800 + 800 = 3005.00 \text{ Rs/h}$$ - Cost of Unit 2 at $90 \text{ MW}$: $$C_2(90) = 0.06(90)^2 + 15(90) + 1000 = 0.06(8100) + 1350 + 1000 = 486 + 1350 + 1000 = 2836.00 \text{ Rs/h}$$ - Total Cost under Equal Sharing: $$C_{total, equal} = 3005.00 + 2836.00 = 5841.00 \text{ Rs/h}$$

Step 7: Determine Net Hourly Monetary Savings: $$\text{Net Savings} = C_{total, equal} - C_{total, economic} = 5841.00 - 5817.73 = 23.27 \text{ Rs/h}$$ Annual Cost Savings: $$\text{Annual Savings} = 23.27 \text{ Rs/h} \times 24 \text{ h/day} \times 365 \text{ days/year} \approx 203,845 \text{ Rs/year}$$ This complete numerical derivation demonstrates the concrete financial savings delivered by the equal incremental cost criterion in utility system operation.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Consider a classic KTU university examination problem: A power station has two thermal generating units supplying a total system demand of $P_D = 180 \text{ MW}$. The quadratic fuel cost functions of the units are given by: $$C_1(P_1) = 0.05 P_1^2 + 20 P_1 + 800 \text{ Rs/h}$$ $$C_2(P_2) = 0.06 P_2^2 + 15 P_2 + 1000 \text{ Rs/h}$$ The operating limits of both generating units are: $$20 \text{ MW} \le P_1 \le 120 \text{ MW}$$ $$20 \text{ MW} \le P_2 \le 120 \text{ MW}$$ Neglecting transmission losses, determine: (i) The incremental fuel cost equations for each unit. (ii) The optimal economic generation schedule ($P_1$ and $P_2$) and the system marginal cost $\lambda$. (iii) The total hourly fuel cost under optimal economic dispatch. (iv) The total hourly fuel cost if the load is shared equally between the units ($P_1 = P_2 = 90 \text{ MW}$). (v) The net monetary savings in Rs/h achieved by deploying economic dispatch.

Step 1: Formulate Incremental Fuel Cost Equations: Differentiating the individual cost functions with respect to power output: - For Unit 1: $$\frac{dC_1}{dP_1} = \frac{d}{dP_1}\left(0.05 P_1^2 + 20 P_1 + 800\right) = 0.10 P_1 + 20 \text{ Rs/MWh}$$ - For Unit 2: $$\frac{dC_2}{dP_2} = \frac{d}{dP_2}\left(0.06 P_2^2 + 15 P_2 + 1000\right) = 0.12 P_2 + 15 \text{ Rs/MWh}$$

Step 2: Apply the Equal Incremental Cost Criterion: For optimal economic dispatch neglecting losses: $$\frac{dC_1}{dP_1} = \frac{dC_2}{dP_2} = \lambda$$ $$0.10 P_1 + 20 = \lambda \implies 0.10 P_1 = \lambda - 20 \implies P_1 = \frac{\lambda - 20}{0.10} = 10\lambda - 200$$ $$0.12 P_2 + 15 = \lambda \implies 0.12 P_2 = \lambda - 15 \implies P_2 = \frac{\lambda - 15}{0.12} = \frac{100}{12}\lambda - \frac{1500}{12} = 8.3333\lambda - 125$$

Step 3: Enforce the Power Balance Constraint: Total generation must equal total demand: $$P_1 + P_2 = P_D = 180 \text{ MW}$$ Substituting the expressions for $P_1$ and $P_2$ in terms of $\lambda$: $$(10\lambda - 200) + (8.3333\lambda - 125) = 180$$ $$18.3333\lambda - 325 = 180$$ $$18.3333\lambda = 180 + 325 = 505$$ $$\lambda = \frac{505}{18.3333} = \frac{505}{55/3} = \frac{1515}{55} = \frac{303}{11} \approx 27.5455 \text{ Rs/MWh}$$

Step 4: Calculate Optimal Generation Allocations: - For Unit 1: $$P_1 = 10(27.5455) - 200 = 275.455 - 200 = 75.455 \text{ MW}$$ - For Unit 2: $$P_2 = 8.3333(27.5455) - 125 = \frac{100}{12}\left(\frac{303}{11}\right) - 125 = \frac{30300}{132} - 125 = 229.545 - 125 = 104.545 \text{ MW}$$

Check Power Balance and Unit Limits: - Total power: $P_1 + P_2 = 75.455 + 104.545 = 180.000 \text{ MW} = P_D$. - Check limits: $20 \le P_1 (75.455) \le 120$ (Satisfied). $20 \le P_2 (104.545) \le 120$ (Satisfied). Both units operate strictly within their allowed operating boundaries.

Step 5: Compute Total Fuel Cost under Economic Dispatch: - Cost of Unit 1: $$C_1(75.455) = 0.05(75.455)^2 + 20(75.455) + 800$$ $$C_1 = 0.05(5693.46) + 1509.10 + 800 = 284.67 + 1509.10 + 800 = 2593.77 \text{ Rs/h}$$ - Cost of Unit 2: $$C_2(104.545) = 0.06(104.545)^2 + 15(104.545) + 1000$$ $$C_2 = 0.06(10929.65) + 1568.18 + 1000 = 655.78 + 1568.18 + 1000 = 3223.96 \text{ Rs/h}$$ - Total Economic Operating Cost: $$C_{total, economic} = C_1 + C_2 = 2593.77 + 3223.96 = 5817.73 \text{ Rs/h}$$

Step 6: Compute Fuel Cost under Equal Load Sharing ($P_1 = P_2 = 90 \text{ MW}$): If both generators simply share the load equally: - Cost of Unit 1 at $90 \text{ MW}$: $$C_1(90) = 0.05(90)^2 + 20(90) + 800 = 0.05(8100) + 1800 + 800 = 405 + 1800 + 800 = 3005.00 \text{ Rs/h}$$ - Cost of Unit 2 at $90 \text{ MW}$: $$C_2(90) = 0.06(90)^2 + 15(90) + 1000 = 0.06(8100) + 1350 + 1000 = 486 + 1350 + 1000 = 2836.00 \text{ Rs/h}$$ - Total Cost under Equal Sharing: $$C_{total, equal} = 3005.00 + 2836.00 = 5841.00 \text{ Rs/h}$$

Step 7: Determine Net Hourly Monetary Savings: $$\text{Net Savings} = C_{total, equal} - C_{total, economic} = 5841.00 - 5817.73 = 23.27 \text{ Rs/h}$$ Annual Cost Savings: $$\text{Annual Savings} = 23.27 \text{ Rs/h} \times 24 \text{ h/day} \times 365 \text{ days/year} \approx 203,845 \text{ Rs/year}$$ This complete numerical derivation demonstrates the concrete financial savings delivered by the equal incremental cost criterion in utility system operation.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

The quadratic fuel cost functions of the units are given by: $$C_1(P_1) = 0.05 P_1^2 + 20 P_1 + 800 \text{ Rs/h}$$ $$C_2(P_2) = 0.06 P_2^2 + 15 P_2 + 1000 \text{ Rs/h}$$ The operating limits of both generating units are: $$20 \text{ MW} \le P_1 \le 120 \text{ MW}$$ $$20 \text{ MW} \le P_2 \le 120 \text{ MW}$$ Neglecting transmission losses, determine: (i) The incremental fuel cost equations for each unit. (ii) The optimal economic generation schedule ($P_1$ and $P_2$) and the system marginal cost $\lambda$. (iii) The total hourly fuel cost under optimal economic dispatch. (iv) The total hourly fuel cost if the load is shared equally between the units ($P_1 = P_2 = 90 \text{ MW}$). (v) The net monetary savings in Rs/h achieved by deploying economic dispatch.

Step 1: Formulate Incremental Fuel Cost Equations: Differentiating the individual cost functions with respect to power output: - For Unit 1: $$\frac{dC_1}{dP_1} = \frac{d}{dP_1}\left(0.05 P_1^2 + 20 P_1 + 800\right) = 0.10 P_1 + 20 \text{ Rs/MWh}$$ - For Unit 2: $$\frac{dC_2}{dP_2} = \frac{d}{dP_2}\left(0.06 P_2^2 + 15 P_2 + 1000\right) = 0.12 P_2 + 15 \text{ Rs/MWh}$$

Check Power Balance and Unit Limits: - Total power: $P_1 + P_2 = 75.455 + 104.545 = 180.000 \text{ MW} = P_D$. - Check limits: $20 \le P_1 (75.455) \le 120$ (Satisfied). $20 \le P_2 (104.545) \le 120$ (Satisfied). Both units operate strictly within their allowed operating boundaries.

Step 5: Compute Total Fuel Cost under Economic Dispatch: - Cost of Unit 1: $$C_1(75.455) = 0.05(75.455)^2 + 20(75.455) + 800$$ $$C_1 = 0.05(5693.46) + 1509.10 + 800 = 284.67 + 1509.10 + 800 = 2593.77 \text{ Rs/h}$$ - Cost of Unit 2: $$C_2(104.545) = 0.06(104.545)^2 + 15(104.545) + 1000$$ $$C_2 = 0.06(10929.65) + 1568.18 + 1000 = 655.78 + 1568.18 + 1000 = 3223.96 \text{ Rs/h}$$ - Total Economic Operating Cost: $$C_{total, economic} = C_1 + C_2 = 2593.77 + 3223.96 = 5817.73 \text{ Rs/h}$$

Step 6: Compute Fuel Cost under Equal Load Sharing ($P_1 = P_2 = 90 \text{ MW}$): If both generators simply share the load equally: - Cost of Unit 1 at $90 \text{ MW}$: $$C_1(90) = 0.05(90)^2 + 20(90) + 800 = 0.05(8100) + 1800 + 800 = 405 + 1800 + 800 = 3005.00 \text{ Rs/h}$$ - Cost of Unit 2 at $90 \text{ MW}$: $$C_2(90) = 0.06(90)^2 + 15(90) + 1000 = 0.06(8100) + 1350 + 1000 = 486 + 1350 + 1000 = 2836.00 \text{ Rs/h}$$ - Total Cost under Equal Sharing: $$C_{total, equal} = 3005.00 + 2836.00 = 5841.00 \text{ Rs/h}$$

Step 7: Determine Net Hourly Monetary Savings: $$\text{Net Savings} = C_{total, equal} - C_{total, economic} = 5841.00 - 5817.73 = 23.27 \text{ Rs/h}$$ Annual Cost Savings: $$\text{Annual Savings} = 23.27 \text{ Rs/h} \times 24 \text{ h/day} \times 365 \text{ days/year} \approx 203,845 \text{ Rs/year}$$ This complete numerical derivation demonstrates the concrete financial savings delivered by the equal incremental cost criterion in utility system operation.
